    \section{Introduction to the Model}\label{sec:SI_model_intro}
        The purpose of this section is to introduce the mathematical background required to understand the model developed in subsequent sections. We proceed through three levels of generality: spatial resource games (SRGs), expected spatial resource games (ESRGs), and finally influencer's games as a canonical subclass of ESRGs defined by a proportional influence allocation rule. 
       
        \subsection{Spatial Resource Games and ESRGs}
            A spatial resource game (SRG) augments a standard normal-form game \cite{osborne1994course} with an environmental state: a measurable space $\mathbb{B}$ of resource locations, each endowed with a resource value $B(b) \geq 0$. Players choose strategies from their strategy space $\mathbb{X_i}$, and their payoffs depend jointly on the full strategy profile and on how the resource landscape $B:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is distributed.

            As an informal illustration, consider $N$ players searching for coins scattered across a room. The room is the resource space $\mathbb{B}$, the coin density at each location is $B(b)$, and each player's strategy $x_i$ encodes their search trajectory. The player's payoff is the total coins collected, which depends on all players' strategies and the coin distribution: a quintessential SRG.

            \begin{definition}[Spatial Resource Game]\label{def:SI_SRG}
                A spatial resource game is a normal-form game $T = (I,\{X_i\}_{i\in I}, \{u_i\}_{i\in I})$, where:
                \begin{itemize}
                    \item $I = \{1,2,\dots,N\}$ is the finite player set;
                     \item $X_i$ is the strategy space of player $i$, with $X_i \subseteq \mathbb{R}^L$ compact and convex;
                    \item $(\mathbb{B}, \mathcal{F}, \mu)$ is a $\sigma$-finite measure space of resources, with $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$, $B\geq 0$ $\mu$-a.e., the resource distribution;
                    \item the utility function of player $i$ takes the form
                    \begin{equation}
                        u_i : \otimes_{i=1}^N \mathbb{X}_i \to \mathbb{R}, \qquad
                        u_i = u_i\bigl(\mathbf{x}; B\bigr),
                    \end{equation}
                    where $\mathbf{x} = (x_1, x_2, \dots, x_N) \in X_1 \times X_2 \times \cdots \times X_N$ is the strategy profile.
                \end{itemize}
            \end{definition}
            \begin{remark}
                The defining feature of a SRG is that the payoff $u_i$ depends on the resource landscape $B(b)$ and the strategy profile $\mathbf{x}$ simultaneously.
            \end{remark}
            There are many spatial resource games in the game theory literature. Examples that are explicitly spatial include Hotelling's and facility location games. In a Hotelling's game firms choose locations on a line segment to maximize market share, with consumers distributed uniformly. The equilibrium tendency for firms to cluster at the center is the famous "principle of minimum differentiation" \cite{hotelling1929stability}. Facility location games generalize this to multiple dimensions and more complex consumer distributions, with applications in economics, political science, and computer science \cite{drezner2024competitive}.


            An important sub-class of SRGs is the expected spatial resource game (ESRG). Rather than computing exact returns from a realized environmental state, an ESRG endows each resource point $b$ with a probability $G_i(\mathbf{x}, b) \in [0,1]$ that player $i$ captures the resource at $b$, and defines payoffs by the resulting expected return. In the coin-search example, $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ is the probability that player $i$ reaches location $b$ before any other player.

            \begin{definition}[Expected Spatial Resource Game (ESRG)]\label{def:SI_ESRG}
                A spatial resource game $T = (I, \{X_i\}_{i\in I}, \{u_i\}_{i\in I})$ is an expected spatial resource game if there exists a measurable function
                \[
                    G_i : \otimes_{i=1}^N \mathbb{X}_i \times \mathbb{B} \to [0,1]
                \]
                satisfying $\sum_{i \in I} G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \leq 1$ for $\mu$-almost every $b$, such that the utility of player $i$ under strategy profile $\mathbf{x} = (x_1,\dots,x_N) \in \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ is
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_ESRG_utility}
                    u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \int_{\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, d\mu(b).
                \end{equation}
                In the two canonical cases:\smallskip
                \begin{itemize}
                    \item Discrete environments: if $\mu$ is the counting measure on a countable $\mathbb{B}$, then
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_disc_ESRG}
                        u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},b).
                    \end{equation}
                    \item Continuous environments: if $\mu$ is the Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^K$, then
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_cont_ESRG}
                        u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \int_{\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\,db.
                    \end{equation}
                \end{itemize}
            \end{definition}
            \begin{remark}
                The condition $\sum_{i\in I} G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \leq 1$ ensures that total allocations do not exceed the available resource at $b$. Equality, $\sum_{i} G_i = 1$, corresponds to exhaustive allocation (all of the resource is distributed to players), which is the case in influencer's games.
            \end{remark}
            Many prominent game-theoretic models are ESRGs. In political competition models, politicians choose positions in an ideological space $\D$ to maximize their vote share; each voter at location $b$ is a resource allocated with probability $G_i$ proportional to proximity or valence \cite{yang2020us}. In ecological competition, species choose home-range centers or migration corridors; food and mates distributed across a landscape $\B$ are shared according to encounter probabilities \cite{borger2008effects}. Colonel Blotto games are a finite, two-player instance of an ESRG: each player allocates a fixed budget across $K$ battlefields, and the winner of battlefield $b$ (i.e.~$G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \in \{0,1\}$) is determined by an all-or-nothing rule \cite{kovenock2012colonel,roberson2006colonel}. Classical Blotto games are limited in scope: they typically admit only two players, discrete and budget-constrained allocations, and binary (all-or-nothing) assignment of each battlefield. influencer's games address all three limitations by allowing continuously varying, proportional allocation across an arbitrary number of players and a resource space of any dimension. 

            \subsection{influencer's games}\label{subsec:SI_model}
                We now introduce the central model of this paper.
                \begin{definition}[Influence Kernel]\label{def:SI_influence_kernel}
                    Given player set $I = \{1,\dots,N\}$, strategy space $\mathbb{X_i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^L$, and resource space $(\mathbb{B},\mathcal{F},\mu)$ as in \Cref{def:SI_ESRG}, the influence kernel of player $i\in I$ is a function
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_influence_kernel_def}
                        f_i : X_i \times \mathbb{B} \to \mathbb{R}_{>0},
                        \qquad (x_i,b) \mapsto f_i(x_i,b) > 0,
                    \end{equation}
                    jointly measurable with respect to the product $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{B}(X_i)\otimes\mathcal{F}$, where $\mathcal{B}(X_i)$ is the Borel $\sigma$-algebra on $X_i$. The strict positivity $f_i > 0$ ensures that every player exerts non-zero influence at every resource point, so that $\sum_{j=1}^N f_j(x_j,b)$ is bounded strictly away from zero for all $(\mathbf{x},b)\in\mathbb{X_i}\times\mathbb{B}$, guaranteeing the allocation rule below is well-defined at every point. Joint measurability ensures $b\mapsto f_i(x_i,b)$ is $\mathcal{F}$-measurable for each fixed $x_i$, which is required for the integrals in \Cref{def:SI_utility_function} to be well defined.
                \end{definition}
                In this way, an influence kernel measures the impact that a player's strategy $x_i$ in $X_i \subset \mathbb{X_i}$ will have on a resource point $b$ in $\mathbb{B}$. An influence kernel can model a species' home range: a function centered at the den (or nest) that gives the probability of the species being at a given location \cite{worton1989kernel,powell2000animal}. A species' strategy could then be the den location or the shape/scale of the home range, chosen to maximize expected resource intake. In this example resources could be food or mates distributed across a landscape \cite{borger2008there}. In social media, an influence kernel can represent reach or engagement across a trait space (e.g., topics, sentiment, posting time) \cite{lee2018advertising,huang2021strategic}. An influencer's strategy then corresponds to where in trait space they position their content (topic selection, tone, posting frequency). Competing resources are engagement metrics (followers, likes, shares) allocated according to relative influence. In both examples influence is gradual (not binary) and captured by the kernel. Overlapping kernels produce competition: resource allocation at a point is shared proportionally by competing influences. 
                
                We model a player's expected return from a resource as proportional to the player's influence over the resource at point $b$ relative to the total influence being exerted on that resource point. This proportional allocation rule is the multi-player generalisation of the Tullock contest success function \cite{tullock1980efficient,skaperdas1996contest,clark1998competition}, extended here to a continuum of resource points.
                \begin{definition}[Relative Influence and Influence Probability]
                    Let $f_i : X_i \times \mathbb{B} \to \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ denote the influence 
                    kernel of player $i$, where $X_i \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ is the strategy space 
                    and $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^K$ is the resource space. We assume 
                    $f_i(x_i, b) > 0$ for all $i$ such that $x_i \in X_i$ and $b \in \mathbb{B}$, so that 
                    the denominator $\sum_{j=1}^{N} f_j(x_j,b) > 0$ is guaranteed. The 
                    relative influence of player $i$ at point $b \in \mathbb{B}$ under 
                    strategy profile $\mathbf{x}$ is defined as
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_relative_influence}
                        G_i(\mathbf{x},b) = \frac{f_i(x_i,b)}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_j(x_j,b)}
                    \end{equation}
                    with complement
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_relative_influence_comp}
                        G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) = 1 - G_i(\mathbf{x},b) = 
                        \frac{\sum_{j \neq i} f_j(x_j,b)}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_j(x_j,b)}
                    \end{equation}
                    By construction, $G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \in (0,1)$ for all $i \in I$ and 
                    $\sum_{i \in I} G_i(\mathbf{x},b) = 1$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$.

                    \noindent This admits a lottery contest probabilistic interpretation \cite{tullock1980efficient,skaperdas1996contest}: for a fixed resource point $b \in \mathbb{B}$ and strategy profile $\mathbf{x}$, $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ is the probability that player $i$ wins the resource at $b$ in a weighted lottery whose ticket shares are proportional to each player's kernel value $f_i(x_i,b)$. Formally, for each $b\in\mathbb{B}$ and $\mathbf{x}\in\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, define a discrete random variable $\beta_b$ on the probability space $(I, 2^I, \mathbb{P}_b^{\mathbf{x}})$ where
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_relative_influence_prob}
                        \mathbb{P}_b^{\mathbf{x}}(\beta_b = i) = G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \quad \forall i \in I,
                    \end{equation}
                    which is a valid probability distribution since $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)>0$ for all 
                    $i\in I$ and $\sum_{i \in I} G_i(\mathbf{x},b) = 1$. The superscript $\mathbf{x}$ on $\mathbb{P}_b^{\mathbf{x}}$ makes explicit that this pointwise allocation probability is a parameter of the strategy profile, reflecting the competitive nature of the game.
                \end{definition}
                With this foundation, an Influencer Game is a type of ESRG where players' utility functions are defined as the sum of expected resources that each player will receive from all $b\in \mathbb{B}$. 
                \begin{definition}[Utility Function]\label{def:SI_utility_function}
                    Let $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B}, \mu)$ with $B(b) \geq 0$ $\mu$-almost everywhere, 
                    where $(\mathbb{B}, \mathcal{F}, \mu)$ is the resource measure space with 
                    $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^K$ as in \Cref{def:SI_ESRG}. The utility function 
                    for player $i \in I$ under strategy profile $\mathbf{x} \in \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ is 
                    defined as
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_utility}
                        u_{i}(\mathbf{x}) = \int_{\mathbb{B}} B(b) \cdot G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \, d\mu(b).
                    \end{equation}
                    This formulation unifies both discrete and continuous influencer's games:
                    \begin{itemize}
                        \item Discrete: if $\mu$ is the counting measure on a countable set 
                        $\mathbb{B}$, then $u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b) \cdot 
                        G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)$.
                        \item Continuous: if $\mu$ is the $K$-dimensional Lebesgue measure 
                        on $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^K$, then $u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \int_{\mathbb{B}} 
                        B(b) \cdot G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \, d^K b$.
                    \end{itemize}
                    Well-definedness and finiteness follow from the pointwise domination 
                    $0 < G_i(\mathbf{x},b) < 1$ $\mu$-a.e., which gives 
                    $B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \leq B(b)$ $\mu$-a.e., so the integrand is dominated 
                    by $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$. Finiteness of the bound then gives 
                    $u_i(\mathbf{x}) \in \left[0, \int_{\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d\mu(b)\right)$ 
                    for all $\mathbf{x} \in \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$. The strict inequality on the right 
                    follows from $G_i < 1$, i.e., each player captures only a strict fraction of 
                    total resources whenever $N \geq 2$.
                \end{definition}
                
                \begin{figure}[ht!]
                    \includesvg[width=\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/2_2/fig1/demo.svg}
                    \caption{\Cref{fig:game_setup_demo} Represents a general influencer game set up. Starting from the left, resources are distributed in via a resource landscape $B(b)$. In this case resources are distributed in a two dimensional space with two traits. Players choose strategies $x_i$ in the strategy space $X_i$ represented as their positions. In the center the environment divides the resources in the resource landscape via relative influence \Cref{eq:SI_relative_influence}. The total resources distributed to a player is their utility function, which players use to optimize their strategy choice, represented as their movement in strategy space on the right.}
                    \label{fig:game_setup_demo}
                \end{figure}


                \Cref{fig:game_setup_demo} illustrates the general setup of an influencer game. influencer's games extend Colonel‑Blotto–style contests to continuous resource and strategy spaces: here $f_i(x_i,b)$ models a player's continuous allocation (influence) at location $b$. Unlike many classical Blotto formulations, the resource density $B(b)$ may vary across the domain (battlefields can have heterogeneous values) and allocations need not be discrete. The framework naturally handles multi‑dimensional strategy and resource spaces and arbitrarily many players. This generalization admits a richer set of equilibria; below we focus on symmetric influencer's games and derive existence, uniqueness, and stability conditions for symmetric Nash equilibria.

        
        
    \section{General Results on Nash Analysis}

       We study Nash equilibria to characterize the long-run behavior of influencer's games. Since influencer's games are normal-form games, classical existence results apply. We impose the following standing assumptions throughout.

        \begin{assumption}[Standing Assumptions]\label{ass:SI_standing}
            
            \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
                \item\label{ass:SI_nonneg} Non-negative resources: $B(b) \geq 0$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$. This is without loss of generality, as adding a positive constant to $B$ does not alter the first-order conditions or the equilibrium structure.
                \item\label{ass:SI_f_smooth} Smooth, strictly positive kernels: For each $i \in I$ and $\mu$-almost every $b \in \mathbb{B}$, the influence kernel satisfies $f_i(\cdot\,,b) \in C^2(X_i)$ and $f_i(x_i,b) > 0$ for all $x_i \in X_i$. This ensures: (a)~the denominator $\sum_{j\in I}f_j(x_j,b) > 0$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, so $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\in(0,1)$ is well-defined and bounded away from the indeterminate $0/0$ case; (b)~$G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ is $C^2$ on $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ for every fixed $b\in\mathbb{B}$, by the smoothness of $f_i$ and the quotient rule; and (c)~the log-derivative $d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\ln f_i(x_i,b)$ is well-defined and continuous on $X_i$.
                \item\label{ass:SI_integrability} Uniform domination for differentiation: We work with the finite resource-space setting of \Cref{def:SI_utility_function} throughout, so all sums are finite. For each player $i \in I$ and each component $l = 1,\ldots,L$, the maps
                \begin{equation*}
                    b \mapsto \sup_{x_i \in X_i}\bigl|d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)\bigr|, \qquad
                    b \mapsto \sup_{x_i \in X_i}\Bigl|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)\Bigr|
                \end{equation*}
                are bounded on $\mathbb{B}$. Since $ G_i, G_i^c \in [0,1]$ pointwise and $|\mathbb{B}| < \infty$, this provides the uniform dominating functions required to apply the dominated convergence theorem \cite[Thm.~1.34]{rudin1987real}, justifying interchange of differentiation and summation for $u_i$, $\nabla u_i$, and $\nabla^2 u_i$. Many of the results extend to continuous resource spaces provided analogous $L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$-domination holds.
            \end{enumerate}
        \end{assumption}
    
        \subsection{Existence of Nash Equilibria}
            Let $T(I,\mathbf{X},\mathbf{u})$ denote the influencer game, where $I = \{1,\ldots,N\}$, $\mathbf{X} = \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, and $\mathbf{u} = (u_1,\ldots,u_N)$ is defined in \Cref{def:SI_utility_function}. We invoke \Cref{thm:SI_Reny}, a version of Reny's pure-strategy existence result \cite{reny1999existence,reny2020nash}, to establish existence.
            \begin{theorem}[Pure-Strategy Nash Equilibrium Existence \cite{debreu1952social,fan1952fixed,glicksberg1952further}]\label{thm:SI_Reny}
                Let $T$ be a normal-form game in which each strategy space $X_i$ is a nonempty compact convex subset of a Euclidean space, each payoff function $u_i$ is continuous on $X_1 \times \cdots \times X_N$, and quasi-concave in own strategy $x_i$ for each fixed $x_{-i}$. Then $T$ possesses a pure-strategy Nash equilibrium.
            \end{theorem}

            \begin{remark}
                \Cref{thm:SI_Reny} is the classical Debreu--Fan--Glicksberg theorem \cite{debreu1952social,fan1952fixed,glicksberg1952further}, each of whose three eponymous authors established the requisite fixed-point machinery independently. It is recoverable as a special case of Reny's broader framework \cite{reny1999existence,reny2016nash,reny2020nash} for potentially discontinuous games. We invoke the Reny framework as a unified foundation that subsumes both the continuous setting treated here and potential discontinuous extensions of the model (e.g., if influence kernels vanish at strategy boundaries).
            \end{remark}

            Suppose $X_i \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ is compact and convex for each $i \in I$. By \Cref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_f_smooth}, $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ is $C^2$ on $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ for every $b \in \mathbb{B}$. By \Cref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_integrability} and the dominated convergence theorem \cite[Thm.~1.34]{rudin1987real}, the sum $u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ is $C^2$ on $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$; in particular $u_i$ is continuous. We further require that $u_i(x_i, x_{-i})$ is quasi-concave in $x_i$ for each fixed $x_{-i}$; a sufficient condition is that the Hessian $\nabla^2_{x_i}u_i$ is negative semi-definite at each fixed $x_{-i}$. 
            \begin{proposition}[Log-Concavity of the Allocation Share]\label{prop:SI_Gi_logconcave}
                Let Assumptions~\ref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_f_smooth} hold and suppose $f_i(\cdot,b)$ is log-concave in $x_i$ on $X_i$ for $\mu$-almost every $b\in\mathbb{B}$. Then for every fixed opponent profile $x_{-i}\in X_{-i}$ and $\mu$-almost every $b\in\mathbb{B}$, the allocation share
                \[
                    x_i \;\mapsto\; G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \;=\; \frac{f_i(x_i,b)}{\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I}f_j(x_j,b)}
                \]
                is log-concave in $x_i$ on $X_i$.
            \end{proposition}
            \begin{proof}
                Fix $x_{-i}\in X_{-i}$, $b\in\mathbb{B}$, and write $p := f_i(x_i,b)$, $\nabla p := \nabla_{x_i}f_i(x_i,b)$, and $H_p := \nabla^2_{x_i}f_i(x_i,b)$. Set
                \[
                    C \;:=\; \sum_{j\neq i}f_j(x_j,b) \;>\; 0,
                \]
                where strict positivity follows from \Cref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_f_smooth}. Then $G_i(\mathbf{x},b) = p/(p+C)$, so
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_logGi_decomp}
                    \ln G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \;=\; \ln p \;-\; \ln(p + C).
                \end{equation}
                We show that the Hessian $\nabla^2_{x_i}\ln G_i \preceq 0$ by computing each term of \Cref{eq:SI_logGi_decomp} separately. Using the standard identities for Hessians of composed logarithms:
                \begin{align}
                    \nabla^2_{x_i}\ln p &= \frac{H_p}{p} - \frac{\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top}{p^2}, \label{eq:SI_hess_lnp}\\
                    \nabla^2_{x_i}\ln(p+C) &= \frac{H_p}{p+C} - \frac{\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top}{(p+C)^2}. \label{eq:SI_hess_lnpC}
                \end{align}
                Subtracting \Cref{eq:SI_hess_lnpC} from \Cref{eq:SI_hess_lnp}:
                \begin{align}
                    \nabla^2_{x_i}\ln G_i
                    &= \left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p+C}\right)H_p
                      - \left(\frac{1}{p^2}-\frac{1}{(p+C)^2}\right)\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top \notag\\
                    &= \frac{C}{p(p+C)}\,H_p
                      - \frac{C(2p+C)}{p^2(p+C)^2}\,\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top \notag\\
                    &= \frac{C}{p(p+C)}\!\left[H_p - \left(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p+C}\right)\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top\right]. \label{eq:SI_hess_Gi}
                \end{align}
                Since $C>0$ and $p>0$, the scalar prefactor $\frac{C}{p(p+C)}>0$. It therefore suffices to show that the bracketed matrix in \Cref{eq:SI_hess_Gi} is negative semi-definite, i.e.,
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_need_to_show}
                    H_p \;\preceq\; \left(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p+C}\right)\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top.
                \end{equation}
                By hypothesis, $f_i(\cdot,b)$ is log-concave, meaning $\nabla^2_{x_i}\ln f_i \preceq 0$, which by \Cref{eq:SI_hess_lnp} is equivalent to
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_logconcave_cond}
                    H_p \;\preceq\; \frac{\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top}{p}.
                \end{equation}
                Since $\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top$ is positive semi-definite and $\frac{1}{p} \leq \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p+C}$ (as $\frac{1}{p+C}\geq 0$), \Cref{eq:SI_logconcave_cond} implies
                \[
                    H_p \;\preceq\; \frac{\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top}{p} \;\preceq\; \left(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p+C}\right)\nabla p\,(\nabla p)^\top,
                \]
                which is \Cref{eq:SI_need_to_show}. Substituting back into \Cref{eq:SI_hess_Gi} gives $\nabla^2_{x_i}\ln G_i\preceq 0$, so $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ is log-concave in $x_i$.
            \end{proof}

            \begin{remark}
                \Cref{prop:SI_Gi_logconcave} shows that log-concavity of the influence kernel $f_i$ propagates to log-concavity of the allocation share $G_i$. Since every log-concave function is quasiconcave \cite[Sec.~3.5.2]{boyd2004convex}, this in turn implies quasiconcavity of $u_i(x_i, x_{-i}) = \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}}B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ in $x_i$ (as a non-negative weighted sum of quasiconcave functions with the same superlevel sets ordering), which satisfies the quasi-concavity hypothesis of \Cref{thm:SI_Reny}.
            \end{remark}

            
            Under \Cref{ass:SI_standing}, \Cref{thm:SI_sym_Nash} guarantees existence, and any interior Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ must satisfy the first-order stationarity conditions of \Cref{lem:SI_requirements} by Fermat's rule \cite[Thm.~27.1]{rockafellar1970convex}.
    

    \begin{assumption}[Quasi-Symmetry]\label{ass:SI_quasisym}
        The influencer game $T(I, \mathbf{X}, \mathbf{u})$ is quasi-symmetric in the
        sense of \cite{reny2020nash}: the strategy space satisfies
        $X_1 = \cdots = X_N =: \mathbb{D}$, and the payoff functions
        satisfy
        \[
            u_1(w, z, \ldots, z)
            \;=\;
            u_2(z, w, z, \ldots, z)
            \;=\;
            \cdots
            \;=\;
            u_N(z, \ldots, z, w)
            \qquad \forall\, w, z \in \mathbb{D}.
        \]
        That is, every player faces an identical strategic problem, up to a relabelling of roles.
    \end{assumption}


    \begin{assumption}[Diagonal Quasiconcavity]\label{ass:SI_diagqc}
        The influencer game $T$ is diagonally quasiconcave: for any
        finite subset $F \subset \mathbb{D}$ and any $\bar{z} \in \operatorname{co} F$,
        \[
            u_1(\bar{z}, \ldots, \bar{z})
            \;\geq\;
            \min_{z \in F}\, u_1(z, \bar{z}, \ldots, \bar{z}).
        \]
        In particular, Assumption~\ref{ass:SI_diagqc} is satisfied whenever
        $u_1(w, z, \ldots, z)$ is quasiconcave in $w \in \mathbb{D}$ for each
        fixed $z \in \mathbb{D}$, which holds under the influence-kernel
        construction of \Cref{def:SI_utility_function} whenever the kernel
        $f_1(\cdot, b)$ is log-concave in $x_1$ for $\mu$-almost every $b \in \mathbb{B}$
        by \Cref{prop:SI_Gi_logconcave}.
    \end{assumption}


    \begin{theorem}[Existence of a Symmetric Pure-Strategy Nash Equilibrium]\label{thm:SI_sym_Nash}
        Let $T(I, \mathbf{X}, \mathbf{u})$ be an influencer game satisfying
        Assumptions~\ref{ass:SI_standing} (Standing Assumptions),
        \ref{ass:SI_quasisym} (Quasi-Symmetry), and
        \ref{ass:SI_diagqc} (Diagonal Quasiconcavity).
        Suppose further that $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ is nonempty,
        compact, and convex.  Then $T$ possesses a
        symmetric pure-strategy Nash equilibrium
        $\mathbf{x}^{*} = (z^{*}, \ldots, z^{*}) \in \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, i.e., a
        profile at which every player adopts the same strategy
        $z^{*} \in \mathbb{D}$ and no player can profitably deviate.
    \end{theorem}

    \begin{proof}
        We verify the hypotheses of the symmetric equilibrium existence theorem
        of \cite[Theorem~4]{baye1993} as extended via correspondences by
        \cite[Theorems~5]{bichlaraki2012}; see also the unified
        treatment in \cite[Theorem~5]{reny2020nash}.

    
        \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item \textbf{Compactness and convexity}
        $\mathbb{D}$ is nonempty, compact, and convex by hypothesis, so the
        joint strategy space $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ is a nonempty compact convex subset
        of $\mathbb{R}^{LN}$.


        \item \textbf{Continuity of payoffs}
        By Assumption~\ref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_f_smooth}, the influence
        kernel $f_i(\cdot, b)$ is $C^2$ on $\mathbb{D}$, so $G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)$
        is $C^2$ on $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ for every fixed $b \in \mathbb{B}$.  By
        Assumption~\ref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_integrability} and the
        dominated convergence theorem \cite[Theorem~1.34]{rudin1987real}, the
        finite sum $u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)$
        is $C^2$ on $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, hence continuous.


        \item \textbf{Quasi-symmetry}
        Assumption~\ref{ass:SI_quasisym} states that all players share the same
        strategy space and that payoffs satisfy the quasi-symmetry condition from Reny
        \cite{reny2020nash}, so $T$ is a quasi-symmetric game.

        
        \item \textbf{Diagonal quasiconcavity}
        Assumption~\ref{ass:SI_diagqc} provides diagonal quasiconcavity directly.

        
        \item \textbf{Local better-reply property on the diagonal}
        For any diagonal profile $(z, \ldots, z)$ that is not a symmetric Nash
        equilibrium, by the very definition of Nash equilibrium there exists a player
        (say player~1) and a deviation $\hat{z} \in \mathbb{D}$ such that
        $u_1(\hat{z}, z, \ldots, z) > u_1(z, \ldots, z)$ (otherwise $z$ would itself
        be a symmetric Nash equilibrium).  Since $u_1$ is continuous (Step~2), this
        strict inequality persists on an open neighborhood of $(z,\ldots,z)$ in
        $\otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, confirming that $\hat{z}$ is a local better reply on the
        diagonal.  Hence $T$ has the local better-reply property on the diagonal
        in the sense of \cite{baye1993}.
        \end{enumerate}
        Thus, we verify all hypotheses of \cite[Theorem~4]{baye1993}
        (see also \cite[Theorem~5]{reny2020nash}).  Therefore $T$ possesses
        a symmetric pure-strategy Nash equilibrium
        $\mathbf{x}^{*} = (z^{*}, \ldots, z^{*})$.
    \end{proof}

    \begin{remark}\label{rem:SI_sym_vs_general}
        Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_sym_Nash} complements the general existence result
        in Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_Reny} in two directions.

        \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
            \item \textbf{Weaker concavity requirement.}  The general result
            (Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_Reny}) requires $u_i$ to be quasiconcave in
            $x_i$ across the entire strategy space.  The symmetric result
            replaces this with the strictly weaker condition of diagonal
            quasiconcavity (Assumption~\ref{ass:SI_diagqc}), which only
            requires a quasiconcavity-type property along the diagonal
            $\{(z, \ldots, z) : z \in \mathbb{D}\}$.

            \item \textbf{Restricted conclusion.}  The trade-off is that
            Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_sym_Nash} guarantees only the existence of a
            symmetric equilibrium.  When agents are ex-ante identical
            (e.g.\ identical resource budgets $B(\cdot)$ and identical influence
            kernels $f_i \equiv f$), a symmetric equilibrium is the natural
            solution concept and Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_sym_Nash} is the
            appropriate existence result.  In heterogeneous settings,
            Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_Reny} remains the primary existence guarantee.
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{remark}

    \begin{corollary}[Symmetric Interior Equilibrium]\label{cor:SI_sym_interior}
        Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:SI_sym_Nash}, if the symmetric
        equilibrium $z^{*}$ is interior to $\mathbb{D}$, then it satisfies the
        symmetric first-order stationarity conditions
        \[
            \frac{\partial u_1}{\partial x_{(1,\ell)}}(z^{*}, \ldots, z^{*}) = 0,
            \qquad \ell = 1, \ldots, L,
        \]
        by Fermat's rule \cite[Theorem~27.1]{rockafellar1970convex}.
    \end{corollary}


            \begin{lemma}[First Order Symmetric Nash Requirements]\label{lem:SI_requirements}
                For a symmetric influencer game in dimension $L,K\in \mathbb{N}$, with $N$ players 
                $i\in I$, $I=\{1,2,\dots,N\}$, utilizing strategies 
                $x_i=(x_{(i,1)},x_{(i,2)},\dots,x_{(i,L)}) \in X_i \subset \mathbb{D}$ for all $i\in I$, 
                where $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ is a compact, convex, bounded set with non-empty 
                interior, influence kernels $f_i(x_i,b)$ continuously differentiable in $x_i$ on 
                $\mathbb{D}$ for $\mu$-almost every $b\in\mathbb{B}$, and resource distribution 
                $B:\mathbb{B}\to \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ with $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$, where 
                $\mathbb{B}\subset \mathbb{R}^K$ is compact and $\mu$ is a $\sigma$-finite measure on 
                $\mathbb{B}$.
                
                Any interior Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*\in \operatorname{int}(\mathbb{D})^N$, where 
                $\mathbf{x}^*=(x_1^*,x_2^*,\dots, x_N^*)$ and 
                $x^*_i=(x_{(i,1)}^*,x_{(i,2)}^*,\dots, x_{(i,L)}^*)$ for all $i\in I$, must satisfy 
                the following system of first-order necessary conditions:
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{\partial u_i(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^*}
                    =0 \quad \forall i\in I \text{ and } l=1,2,\dots,L
                \end{equation}
                where
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{\partial u_i(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}= 
                    \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b) \cdot d_{(i,l)}(x_{i},b) \cdot 
                    G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \cdot G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) 
                \end{equation}
                and 
                \begin{equation}
                    d_{(i,l)}(x_{i},b)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\ln(f_i(x_{i},b))
                \end{equation}
                provided that
                $l=1,2,\dots,L$.
            \end{lemma}
            \begin{proof}
                Since $u_i \in C^1(\otimes_{i=1}^N \mathbb{X}_i)$ (established in \Cref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_integrability}) and $\mathbb{D}$ has nonempty interior, player $i$'s best-response problem $\max_{x_i \in \mathbb{D}} u_i(x_i, x_{-i}^*)$ achieves its maximum on the compact set $\mathbb{D}$ by the extreme value theorem \cite[Thm.~4.16]{rudin1976principles}. At any interior maximizer (and in particular at any interior Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*\in\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{D})^N$) the gradient of $u_i$ with respect to $x_i$ must vanish by Fermat's rule \cite[Thm.~27.1]{rockafellar1970convex}. For $L$-dimensional strategies $x_i = (x_{(i,1)},\ldots,x_{(i,L)})$, the first-order necessary conditions are
                \begin{equation}
                    \nabla_{x_i}u_i(\mathbf{x})\big|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^*} = \mathbf{0}_L
                    \quad \forall\, i \in I, \label{eq:SI_first_order_cond}
                \end{equation}
                or equivalently,
                \begin{equation}
                    \pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}u_i(\mathbf{x})\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^*}=0
                    \quad \forall\, i\in I,\; l=1,2,\dots,L.
                \end{equation}
                It remains to compute $\frac{\partial u_i}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}$ in closed form. By \Cref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_integrability} and the dominated convergence theorem \cite[Thm.~1.34]{rudin1987real}, differentiation passes through the finite sum:
                \begin{align}
                    \pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}u_i(\mathbf{x})
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \\ %
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}\frac{f_{i}(x_i,b)}{\sum_{j\in I}f_{j}(x_j,b)} \\ %
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\frac{\displaystyle\pdv{f_i}{x_{(i,l)}}\cdot\sum_{j\in I} f_{j} - f_{i}\cdot\pdv{f_i}{x_{(i,l)}}}{\left(\sum_{j\in I} f_{j}\right)^2}
                    \label{eq:SI_quotient_rule}\\ %
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b) \pdv{f_i}{x_{(i,l)}} \cdot \left[\frac{1}{\sum_{j\in I} f_{j}}- \frac{f_{i}}{\left(\sum_{j\in I} f_{j}\right)^2}\right] \\ %
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b) \frac{1}{f_i}\pdv{f_i}{x_{(i,l)}} \cdot \left[\frac{f_{i}}{\sum_{j\in I} f_{j}}- \frac{f_{i}^2}{\left(\sum_{j\in I} f_{j}\right)^2}\right] \\ %
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\, d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)\left[G_i(\mathbf{x},b)- G_i^2(\mathbf{x},b)\right] \\ %
                    &=\sum_{b\in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\, d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\,G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b). \\ %
                \end{align}
                In the quotient rule step \Cref{eq:SI_quotient_rule}, only $f_i(x_i,b)$ depends on $x_{(i,l)}$, so $\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\sum_{j\in I} f_j = \frac{\partial f_i}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}$; the numerator simplifies to $\frac{\partial f_i}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\cdot\sum_{j\neq i} f_j$, yielding the stated factored form. The final equality uses $G_i - G_i^2 = G_i(1-G_i) = G_i G_i^c$.
            \end{proof}
            
        \subsection{Uniqueness of the Symmetric Nash Equilibrium}
            We next show that strict log-concavity of the influence kernels is sufficient to guarantee uniqueness of the symmetric Nash equilibrium. 
            \begin{definition}[Symmetric Influencer Game]\label{def:SI_symmetric_influencers}
                An influencer game is \textbf{symmetric} if all players share the same influence kernel: $f_i \equiv f$ for all $i\in I$. Equivalently,
                \begin{equation}
                    x_i=x_j \implies f_i(x_i,b)=f_j(x_j,b) \quad\forall\, i,j\in I,\; b\in\mathbb{B}.
                \end{equation}
                A direct consequence is payoff symmetry:
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_symmetric_payoff}
                    x_i=x_j \implies u_i(x_i,x_{-i})=u_{j}(x_j,x_{-j}).
                \end{equation}
            \end{definition}
            \begin{definition}[Log-Concave and Strictly Log-Concave Influencer Game]\label{def:SI_log-concave_influencers}
                An influencer game is \textbf{log-concave} if, for every $b\in\mathbb{B}$, the map $x_i\mapsto\ln f_i(x_i,b)$ is concave on $\mathbb{D}$ for all $i\in I$, i.e.
                \begin{equation}
                    \nabla^2_{x_i}\ln f_i(x_i,b) \preceq 0 \quad \forall\, x_i\in\mathbb{D},\; b\in\mathbb{B}.
                \end{equation}
                It is strictly log-concave if the above Hessian is negative definite:
                \begin{equation}
                    \nabla^2_{x_i}\ln f_i(x_i,b) \prec 0 \quad \forall\, x_i\in\mathbb{D},\; b\in\mathbb{B}.
                \end{equation}
                Log-concavity of a kernel is a standard condition implying quasiconcavity of $G_i(\cdot\,,x_{-i},b)$ in own strategy \cref{prop:SI_Gi_logconcave}. Strict log-concavity is the stronger condition required for the uniqueness result in \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness}; it is satisfied by Gaussian, multivariate Gaussian (both have negative-definite log-Hessian $-\Sigma^{-1}$), and Dirichlet kernels, as verified in the kernel-specific sections below.
            \end{definition}
            
            \begin{theorem}[Uniqueness of the Symmetric Nash Equilibrium]\label{thm:SI_uniqueness}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric strictly log-concave influencer game (\Cref{def:SI_log-concave_influencers}) with player set $I = \{1,\dots,N\}$, $N\geq 2$, common strategy space $X_i = \mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ compact and convex, resource space $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^K$, $f(\cdot\,,b)\in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ for $\mu$-almost every $b\in\mathbb{B}$, and $B(b)>0$ for at least one $b\in\mathbb{B}$. Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium.
            \end{theorem}
                \begin{proof}
                    By \Cref{thm:SI_sym_Nash}, a symmetric Nash equilibrium exists. Let $\mathbf{x}^*$ be any symmetric Nash equilibrium, so $x_i^* = x^*$ for all $i \in I$ by \Cref{def:SI_symmetric_influencers}. From \Cref{lem:SI_requirements}, $x^*$ must satisfy the first order conditions
                    \begin{equation}
                        \pdv{u_i(\mathbf{x})}{x_{(i,l)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^*} = 
                        \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, \pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}\ln f_i(x_i^*,b) 
                        \cdot G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b)\, G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = 0
                    \end{equation}
                    At a symmetric profile $x_i^* = x^*$ for all $i$, we have $f_i(x^*,b) = 
                    f_j(x^*,b)$ for all $i,j \in I$ and $b \in \mathbb{B}$, so
                    \begin{equation}
                        G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{f_i(x^*,b)}{\sum_{j\in I}f_j(x^*,b)} = 
                        \frac{1}{N}, \quad G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}
                    \end{equation}
                    Substituting, the first order condition reduces to
                    \begin{equation}
                        \frac{N-1}{N^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                        \pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}\ln f_i(x^*,b) = 0
                    \end{equation}
                    Since $\frac{N-1}{N^2} > 0$ for $N \geq 2$, this is equivalent to
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_reduced_FOC}
                        \pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}} \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln f_i(x^*,b) = 0 
                        \quad \forall\, l = 1,2,\dots,L
                    \end{equation}
                    Now define $\Phi(x) = \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln f(x,b)$. By strict log-concavity, $\ln f(\cdot\,,b)$ is strictly concave in $x$ for every $b\in\mathbb{B}$; since $B(b)\geq 0$ and $B(b)>0$ for at least one $b$ (by hypothesis), $\Phi$ is a non-trivially weighted sum of strictly concave functions and is therefore strictly concave on $\mathbb{D}$. A strictly concave $C^1$ function on a convex domain has at most one stationary point its gradient is strictly monotone, so $\nabla\Phi(x^*)=\mathbf{0}$ and $\nabla\Phi(y)=\mathbf{0}$ would imply $x^* = y$ by strict monotonicity of $x\mapsto \nabla\Phi(x)\cdot(x-x^*)$. Hence \Cref{eq:SI_reduced_FOC} has a unique solution $x^* \in \mathbb{D}$. Since all players share the same strategy space and influence kernel by symmetry, this unique $x^*$ is the same for all $i \in I$, and therefore $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*, x^*, \dots, x^*)$ is the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium.
                \end{proof}
             \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} is essential for our analysis later when we show that the symmetric Nash equilibrium stability conditions can help determine the convergence properties of the influencer's games. 
             
        \subsection{Stability of Symmetric Nash Equilibria in Symmetric influencer's games}
            Given the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium established in \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness}, we now characterize its local stability. We study stability with respect to the gradient-ascent dynamics
            \begin{equation}
                \dot{x}_i = \nabla_{x_i}u_i(\mathbf{x}), \qquad i \in I,
            \end{equation}
            whose fixed points coincide with the interior Nash equilibria of the game. A Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ is locally asymptotically stable if it is Lyapunov stable and every trajectory starting in a sufficiently small neighborhood of $\mathbf{x}^*$ converges to it as $t\to\infty$ \cite[§8.4]{hirsch2013differential}. By the Lyapunov linearization theorem \cite[Thm.~1 §8.4]{hirsch2013differential}, a sufficient condition for local asymptotic stability is that all eigenvalues of the Jacobian
            \begin{equation}
                \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{x}^*) := \nabla_{\mathbf{x}}(\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{u})(\mathbf{x}^*)\in\mathbb{R}^{NL\times NL}
            \end{equation}
            of the gradient field evaluated at $\mathbf{x}^*$ are strictly negative. Our strategy is to exploit the symmetric block structure of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{x}^*)$ to reduce this $NL\times NL$ eigenvalue problem to two $L\times L$ eigenproblems whose size is independent of the number of players $N$.
            \begin{theorem}[Jacobian for Symmetric Nash Equilibrium in influencer's games]\label{thm:SI_Jacobian}
                For a symmetric influencer game with $L$-dimensional strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$, $L \in \mathbb{N}$, and influence kernels $f_i(\cdot\,,b) \in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$, the Jacobian of the payoff gradient evaluated at a symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ has the block structure
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:paper_jacobian_general}
                    \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}} = \begin{pmatrix}
                        A & \Delta & \cdots & \Delta \\
                        \Delta & A & \cdots & \Delta \\
                        \vdots & & \ddots & \vdots \\
                        \Delta & \Delta & \cdots & A
                    \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                where $A = \beta_{i,i}$ and $\Delta = \beta_{i,j}$, $i \neq j$, are $L\times L$ blocks with entries
                \begin{align}
                    \beta_{(i,j)} = \begin{pmatrix}
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,1)}}{x_{(i,1)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} & 
                        \cdots & 
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,L)}}{x_{(i,1)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} \\
                        \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,1)}}{x_{(i,L)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} & 
                        \cdots & 
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,L)}}{x_{(i,L)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*}
                    \end{pmatrix}
                \end{align}
                with $A = \beta_{i,i} = \beta_{j,j}$ and $\Delta = \beta_{i,j} = \beta_{s,t}$ for all $i,j,s,t \in I$ with $i \neq j$ and $s \neq t$. Hence $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}$ is a block matrix of symmetric blocks.
            \end{theorem}

            \begin{proof}
                We must establish two claims:
                \begin{align}
                    A &= \beta_{i,i} = \beta_{j,j} \quad \forall\, i,j \in I 
                    \label{eq:SI_Jacobian_diag_sym} \\
                    \Delta &= \beta_{i,j} = \beta_{s,t} \quad \forall\, i,j,s,t \in I,\; 
                    i \neq j,\; s \neq t \label{eq:SI_Jacobian_off_sym}
                \end{align}
                where \Cref{eq:SI_Jacobian_diag_sym} establishes diagonal block symmetry and \Cref{eq:SI_Jacobian_off_sym} establishes off-diagonal block symmetry.

                \begin{enumerate}
                    \item[] \textbf{Part 1 (Diagonal blocks):} We show
                    \begin{align}
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(i,L)}}{x_{(i,l)}} &= 
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(i,l)}}{x_{(i,L)}} 
                        \label{eq:SI_Jacobian_intra_dim_sym}\\
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(i,l)}}{x_{(i,L)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} &= 
                        \pdv[2]{u_j}{x_{(j,L)}}{x_{(j,l)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*}
                        \label{eq:SI_Jacobian_inter_player_diag_sym}
                    \end{align}
                    where \Cref{eq:SI_Jacobian_intra_dim_sym} is intra-player dimensional symmetry and \Cref{eq:SI_Jacobian_inter_player_diag_sym} is inter-player diagonal block symmetry.
                    For notational convenience, define
                    \begin{equation}
                        d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b) = \pdv{}{x_{(i,l)}}\ln f_i(x_i,b)
                    \end{equation}
                    so that from \Cref{lem:SI_requirements},
                    \begin{equation}
                        \pdv{u_i}{x_{(i,l)}} = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                        d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b) \cdot G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b)
                    \end{equation}
                    Differentiating with respect to $x_{(i,L)}$ and applying the product rule, noting that $\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,L)}} G_i G_i^c = (1 - 2G_i)\frac{\partial G_i}{\partial x_{(i,L)}}$:
                    \begin{align}
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(i,L)}}{x_{(i,l)}} = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                        G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) \Bigg[
                        &\pdv{d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)}{x_{(i,L)}} \notag \\
                        &+ d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)\, d_{(i,L)}(x_i,b)
                        \left(G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) - G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\right)
                        \Bigg] \label{eq:SI_jacobians_A}
                    \end{align}
                    Since $f_i(\cdot\,,b) > 0$ and $f_i(\cdot\,,b)\in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ by \Cref{ass:SI_standing}\ref{ass:SI_f_smooth}, the composition $\ln f_i(\cdot\,,b)$ is also $C^2$ on $\mathbb{D}$. By the Clairaut--Schwarz theorem (symmetry of second partial derivatives for $C^2$ functions; \cite[Thm.~9.41]{rudin1976principles}), the mixed partial derivatives of $\ln f_i$ commute:
                    \begin{equation}
                        \pdv{d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)}{x_{(i,L)}} = 
                        \frac{\partial^2 \ln f_i}{\partial x_{(i,L)}\partial x_{(i,l)}} =
                        \frac{\partial^2 \ln f_i}{\partial x_{(i,l)}\partial x_{(i,L)}} =
                        \pdv{d_{(i,L)}(x_i,b)}{x_{(i,l)}}
                    \end{equation}
                    The remaining terms in \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_A} are symmetric in the indices $l$ 
                    and $L$, so \Cref{eq:SI_Jacobian_intra_dim_sym} follows.

                    For \Cref{eq:SI_Jacobian_inter_player_diag_sym}, at the symmetric 
                    equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ we have $x_i^* = x_j^* = x^*$ for all 
                    $i,j \in I$, so
                    \begin{align}
                        G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) &= G_j(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \tfrac{1}{N} \\
                        d_{(i,l)}(x_i^*,b) &= d_{(j,l)}(x_j^*,b) \quad \forall\, i,j \in I
                    \end{align}
                    Substituting into \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_A} shows that all diagonal 
                    blocks are equal, establishing $A = \beta_{i,i} = \beta_{j,j}$ for all 
                    $i,j \in I$.

                    \item[] \textbf{Part 2 (Off-diagonal blocks):} We show
                    \begin{align}
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,L)}}{x_{(i,l)}} &= 
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,l)}}{x_{(i,L)}} 
                        \label{eq:Jacobian_inter_player_sym} \\
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,L)}}{x_{(i,l)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} &= 
                        \pdv[2]{u_s}{x_{(t,L)}}{x_{(s,l)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} \quad 
                        \forall\, i \neq j,\; s \neq t
                        \label{eq:Jacobian_inter_off_sym}
                    \end{align}
                    where \Cref{eq:Jacobian_inter_player_sym} is intra-block symmetry and 
                    \Cref{eq:Jacobian_inter_off_sym} is inter-player off-diagonal block 
                    symmetry.

                    For $j \neq i$, $d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)$ does not depend on $x_{(j,L)}$, 
                    so differentiating $\frac{\partial u_i}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}$ with 
                    respect to $x_{(j,L)}$ only acts on $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ and 
                    $G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b)$. Using $\frac{\partial G_i}{\partial x_{(j,L)}} 
                    = -G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, d_{(j,L)}(x_j,b)\, G_j(\mathbf{x},b)$:
                    \begin{align}
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,L)}}{x_{(i,l)}} = -\sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} 
                        B(b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, G_j(\mathbf{x},b) \cdot 
                        d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)\, d_{(j,L)}(x_j,b) 
                        \left(G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) - G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\right)
                        \label{eq:SI_jacobians_B}
                    \end{align}
                    The right-hand side is symmetric in the pairs $(i,l)$ and $(j,L)$, 
                    so \Cref{eq:Jacobian_inter_player_sym} follows immediately.

                    For \Cref{eq:Jacobian_inter_off_sym}, at $\mathbf{x}^*$ we again 
                    substitute $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = G_j(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$ 
                    and $d_{(i,l)}(x_i^*,b) = d_{(j,l)}(x_j^*,b)$ for all $i,j \in I$. 
                    Since \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_B} depends on $i$ and $j$ only through 
                    these quantities, all off-diagonal blocks evaluated at $\mathbf{x}^*$ 
                    are equal, establishing $\Delta = \beta_{i,j} = \beta_{s,t}$ for all 
                    $i \neq j$, $s \neq t$.
                \end{enumerate}
            \end{proof}
            The block structure of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}$ established in \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian} admits a complete spectral decomposition via the Kronecker product structure of equitable block matrices \cite{silvester2000determinants}.

            \begin{theorem}[Spectral decomposition of equitable block matrices]\label{thm:SI_Soth}
                Let $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}$ be the $NL\times NL$ matrix with $N$ diagonal blocks equal to $A\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}$ and all $N(N-1)$ off-diagonal blocks equal to $\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}$, as in \Cref{eq:paper_jacobian_general}. Define
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_Apm_def}
                    A^{+} := A + (N-1)\Delta, \qquad A^{-} := A - \Delta.
                \end{equation}
                Then $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}$ is block-similar to $\mathrm{diag}(A^{+},\,A^{-},\dots,A^{-})$, with $A^+$ appearing once and $A^-$ appearing $N-1$ times. Consequently,
                \begin{equation}
                    \det(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}) = \det(A^{+})\cdot\det(A^{-})^{N-1},
                \end{equation}
                and the full spectrum of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}$ is
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}) = \sigma(A^{+})\cup\sigma(A^{-}),
                \end{equation}
                where each eigenvalue of $A^+$ has multiplicity $1$ and each eigenvalue of $A^-$ has multiplicity $N-1$.
            \end{theorem}

            \begin{proof}
                Write $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}} = I_N\otimes A + (J_N - I_N)\otimes\Delta$, where $I_N$ is the $N\times N$ identity and $J_N = \mathbf{1}_N\mathbf{1}_N^\top$ is the $N\times N$ all-ones matrix. Let $P\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}$ be any orthogonal matrix whose first row is $\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbf{1}_N^\top$; then $PJ_NP^\top = \mathrm{diag}(N,0,\dots,0)$. Applying the similarity transformation $(P\otimes I_L)$:
                \begin{align}
                    (P\otimes I_L)\,\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}\,(P^\top\otimes I_L)
                    &= I_N\otimes A + (PJ_NP^\top - I_N)\otimes\Delta \notag\\
                    &= I_N\otimes(A-\Delta) + \mathrm{diag}(N,0,\dots,0)\otimes\Delta \notag\\
                    &= \mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(A+(N-1)\Delta,\;A-\Delta,\;\dots,\;A-\Delta\bigr).
                \end{align}
                The determinant and spectral claims follow immediately from this block-diagonal form \cite{silvester2000determinants}.
            \end{proof}

            By Lyapunov's theorem \cite[Thm.~1 §8.4]{hirsch2013differential}, the symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ is locally asymptotically stable under gradient dynamics if and only if all eigenvalues of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ have strictly negative real part. Since $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ is symmetric (see \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian}), all eigenvalues are real, so this reduces to requiring $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ to be negative definite. By \Cref{thm:SI_Soth}, this is equivalent to requiring both $A^{+} = A+(N-1)\Delta$ and $A^{-} = A-\Delta$ to be negative definite. Crucially, while the full Jacobian has dimension $NL\times NL$, the stability check reduces to two $L\times L$ eigenvalue problems whose size is independent of $N$; increasing the number of players $N$ only changes the off-diagonal weight through the factor $(N-1)$ in $A^{+}$, not the dimension of the computation. We can simplify this condition further in the one-dimensional case.
            \subsubsection{One Dimensional influencer's games}\label{subsubsec:SI_one-dimensional}
                
                 For 1-D  symmetric influencer's games the Hessian, blocks are each one by one blocks, if players are in the same position then \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian} implies the Jacobian of the system is given by 
                \begin{equation}
                    \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*=\begin{pmatrix}
                    \alpha & \delta& \cdots & \delta\ \\ %
                    \delta & \alpha & \cdots & \delta\ \\ %
                    \vdots &  & \ddots &\vdots \\ %
                    \delta & \delta & \cdots & \alpha\ \\ %
                    \end{pmatrix}.
                \end{equation}
                where $\alpha,\delta$ are one-by-one blocks. In order for a point $\mathbf{x}^*$ to be stable by \Cref{thm:SI_Soth} we must find the maximum of the two eigenvalues $\lambda_1=\alpha-\delta$ and $\lambda_2=\alpha+(n-1)\delta$ of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}$. 
                \begin{theorem}\label{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}
                    For a symmetric $N$-player influencer game with $N \geq 2$, influence kernels $f_i(\cdot\,,b) \in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$, and resource distribution $B(b) \geq 0$, the symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,x^*,\dots,x^*)$ is locally asymptotically stable if and only if\begin{equation}\label{eq:SS_threshold_1d_general}
                        \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\left((N-1)d'(b) + (N-2)d^2(b)\right) < 0
                    \end{equation}
                    or equivalently,
                    \begin{equation}
                        -\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d'(b) > \frac{N-2}{N-1}
                        \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d^2(b)
                    \end{equation}
                    where $d(b) = d_i(x_i^*,b) = d_j(x_j^*,b)$ and $d'(b) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}d_i(x_i,b)\big|_{x_i=x^*}$ for all $i,j \in I$. For $N=2$ this reduces to $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}}B(b)\,d'(b) < 0$.
                \end{theorem}

                \begin{proof}
                    From \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian}, the Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ evaluated at $\mathbf{x}^*$ takes the scalar block form
                    \begin{equation}
                        \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^* = \begin{pmatrix}
                            \alpha & \delta & \cdots & \delta \\
                            \delta & \alpha & \cdots & \delta \\
                            \vdots & & \ddots & \vdots \\
                            \delta & \delta & \cdots & \alpha
                        \end{pmatrix}
                    \end{equation}
                    where $\alpha = \beta_{i,i}$ and $\delta = \beta_{i,j}$, $i \neq j$, are scalars in the one-dimensional case. From 
                    \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_A,eq:SI_jacobians_B}, the second partial derivatives are
                    \begin{align}
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_i} &= \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, 
                        G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b)\left(\pdv{d_i(x_i,b)}{x_i} + d_i(x_i,b)^2
                        \left[G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) - G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\right]\right) \\
                        \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_j\,\partial x_i} &= -\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                        G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, G_j(\mathbf{x},b)\, d_i(x_i,b)\, d_j(x_j,b)
                        \left[G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) - G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\right]
                    \end{align}
                    Evaluating at $\mathbf{x}^*$, using $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$, 
                    $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}$, and $d_i(x_i^*,b) = d(b)$, 
                    $d'_i(x_i^*,b) = d'(b)$ for all $i \in I$:
                    \begin{align}
                        \alpha &= \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\frac{N-1}{N^2}
                        \left(d'(b) + \frac{N-2}{N}d^2(b)\right) \\
                        \delta &= -\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\frac{N-2}{N^3} d^2(b)
                    \end{align}
                    where the factor $\frac{N-2}{N}$ in $\delta$ arises from
                    $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) - G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N} - \frac{1}{N} = \frac{N-2}{N}$.
                    Since $B(b) \geq 0$, $d^2(b) \geq 0$, and $N - 2 \geq 0$ for all $N \geq 2$,
                    it follows that $\delta \leq 0$, with $\delta = 0$ precisely when $N = 2$
                    or $d(b) = 0$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$.
                    Since $\delta \leq 0$ and $N - 1 \geq 1$, we have
                    $-(N-1)\delta \geq 0 \geq (N-1)\delta$, so
                    \begin{equation}
                        \alpha - \delta \geq \alpha + (N-1)\delta
                    \end{equation}
                    and $\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*) = \alpha - \delta$ is the largest eigenvalue.
                    For an $N \times N$ matrix of the form $\alpha I + \delta(\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top - I)$,
                    the eigenvalues are $\alpha - \delta$ with multiplicity $N-1$ and
                    $\alpha + (N-1)\delta$ with multiplicity $1$.
                    The matrix is negative definite if and only if
                    $\lambda_{\max} = \alpha - \delta < 0$.
                    Computing $\alpha - \delta$ explicitly from the expressions above:
                    \begin{align}
                        \alpha - \delta
                        &= \frac{N-1}{N^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)
                        \left(d'(b) + \frac{N-2}{N}d^2(b)\right)
                        + \frac{N-2}{N^3}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d^2(b) \notag\\
                        &= \frac{1}{N^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}}
                        B(b)\left((N-1)d'(b) + (N-2)d^2(b)\right)
                    \end{align}
                    Since $\frac{1}{N^2} > 0$, the condition $\alpha - \delta < 0$ is equivalent
                    to \Cref{eq:SS_threshold_1d_general}.
                    For $N = 2$, $\delta = 0$ exactly, so both eigenvalues equal
                    $\alpha = \frac{1}{4}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d'(b)$,
                    and the stability condition reduces to $\alpha < 0$,
                    i.e.\ $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d'(b) < 0$.
                \end{proof}
                
                \begin{corollary}
                    A direct result from \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} is that any symmetric 1-D strictly log-concave two player influence game will have a unique stable symmetric Nash equilibrium. 
                \end{corollary}
                \begin{proof}
                    \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} and $N=2$ implies stability since
                    \begin{equation}
                          -\sum_{b\in \B} B(b)d'(b)>\frac{N-2}{N-1}\sum_{b\in \B} B(b)d^2(b)=0
                    \end{equation}
                    is always true since strict log-concavity of $f_i(\cdot,b)$ implies $d'(b) = \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_i^2}\ln f_i(x_i^*,b) < 0$ for all $b\in\mathbb{B}$ \cite{saumard2014log}, so $-\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)d'(b) > 0$. 
                \end{proof}
            \subsubsection{Multi-dimensional Spatial influencer's games}
                Because there are many eigenvalues of $A^+$ and $A^-$ for games in more than one dimension it is not simple to generalize the condition for stability of a symmetric Nash in any dimension, but we may examine each dimensional case by case. If we limit ourselves to two players it is possible to generally analyze when a symmetric two-player Nash equilibrium is stable across dimensions.
                
\begin{lemma}[Multi-Dimensional 2-Player Symmetric Nash
                Stability]\label{lem:SI_two_player_stability_multi_dim}
                    Let $T$ be a 2-player symmetric influencer game with $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ ($L\in\mathbb{N}$), resource space $\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^K$ ($K\in\mathbb{N}$), and influence kernels satisfying $f_i(\cdot\,,b)\in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ for all $b\in\mathbb{B}$. Suppose further that $f_i(\cdot\,,b)$ is strictly log-concave with $\nabla_{x_i}^2\ln f_i(x_i,b)\prec 0$ for all $x_i\in\mathbb{D}$ and $b\in\mathbb{B}$ (strict negative definiteness of the log-kernel Hessian, which holds for Gaussian, multivariate Gaussian, and Dirichlet kernels \cite{saumard2014log}). Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ that is locally asymptotically stable under gradient-ascent dynamics.
                \end{lemma}

            \begin{proof}
                Existence and uniqueness of the symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ follow from \Cref{thm:SI_sym_Nash} and \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} respectively. It remains to establish stability. From \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian}, the Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ at $\mathbf{x}^*$ takes the $2\times 2$ block form
                \begin{equation}
                    \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^* = \begin{pmatrix} A & \Delta \\ \Delta & A \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                where $A$ and $\Delta$ are $L \times L$ blocks. We first show that $\Delta = 0$. At a symmetric equilibrium with $N=2$, we have $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{2}$ and therefore $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{2} = G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b)$, so $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) - G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = 0$. Substituting into \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_B}:
                \begin{align}
                    \pdv[2]{u_i}{x_{(j,L)}\,\partial x_{(i,l)}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}^*} 
                    &= -\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b)\, G_j(\mathbf{x}^*,b)
                    \cdot d_{(i,l)}(b)\, d_{(j,L)}(b)
                    \left[G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) - G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b)\right] = 0
                \end{align}
                so $\Delta = 0$ and $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ is block diagonal:
                \begin{equation}
                    \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^* = \begin{pmatrix} A & 0 \\ 0 & A \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                The eigenvalues of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ are precisely the eigenvalues of $A$, each with multiplicity 2. From \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_A}, the $(l, L)$ entry of $A$ evaluated at $\mathbf{x}^*$ is
                \begin{equation}
                    A_{lL} = \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, \frac{1}{4}
                    \left[\pdv{d_{(i,l)}(x_i^*,b)}{x_{(i,L)}} + 
                    d_{(i,l)}(b)\, d_{(i,L)}(b)
                    \cdot 0\right] = \frac{1}{4}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                    \pdv[2]{}{x_{(i,l)}\,\partial x_{(i,L)}}\ln f_i(x_i^*,b)
                \end{equation}
                where we have again used $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) - G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = 0$. Therefore
                \begin{equation}
                    A = \frac{1}{4}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                    \nabla_{x_i}^2 \ln f_i(x_i^*,b)
                \end{equation}
                which is a nonnegative linear combination of Hessians of $\ln f_i(\cdot\,,b)$. By the hypothesis of the lemma, $\nabla_{x_i}^2\ln f_i(x_i^*,b)\prec 0$ for every $b\in\mathbb{B}$. Since $B(b)\geq 0$ and $B$ is not identically zero there exists $b_0$ with $B(b_0)>0$, so $A$ contains at least one negative definite summand with a strictly positive weight. A nonnegative linear combination of negative semidefinite matrices that includes at least one negative definite summand with positive coefficient is itself negative definite. Hence $A\prec 0$, all eigenvalues of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ are strictly negative, and $\mathbf{x}^*$ is locally asymptotically stable under gradient-ascent dynamics for any $L\in\mathbb{N}$.
            \end{proof}
            This is an immensely powerful result: for any two-player symmetric influencer game whose log-kernels have everywhere negative definite Hessians, the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium is locally asymptotically stable under gradient-ascent dynamics regardless of the dimension of the strategy space. In two-player games we can therefore guarantee local convergence to the symmetric Nash equilibrium under gradient-ascent dynamics without checking any further spectral conditions. As we see in subsequent sections, this leads players to converge to what we call ``locally'' symmetric Nash equilibria across a wide variety of games and dimensions.


            Next we look at the 2-dimensional stability conditions, which are more complex than 1-dimensional space. The 2-dimensional case, while still more complex than the 1-dimensional case, is relatively simple to examine and we can find a maximum eigenvalue for the Jacobian in general. However, extending the results to 3 dimensions, while possible, becomes a tedious calculation.
            \begin{theorem}[2-Dimensional Stability]\label{thm:SI_two_dimensional_stability}
                Let $T(I,\mathbf{X},\mathbf{u})$ be a symmetric $N$-player influencer game with $N\geq 2$, $\mathbb{D}, \mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^2$, influence kernels $f_i(\cdot\,,b) \in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$, and symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x_1^*,\dots,x_N^*)$ with $x_i^* = (x_{(i,1)}^*, x_{(i,2)}^*)$. Let $a_{lm}$ and $\delta_{lm}$ denote the $(l,m)$ entries of the blocks $A$ and $\Delta$ defined in \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_A,eq:SI_jacobians_B}. Then $\mathbf{x}^*$ is locally asymptotically stable under gradient dynamics if and only if both
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_stab_plus}
                    \bigl(a_{11}+(N-1)\delta_{11}\bigr) + \bigl(a_{22}+(N-1)\delta_{22}\bigr) + 
                    \sqrt{\bigl(a_{11}-a_{22}+(N-1)(\delta_{11}-\delta_{22})\bigr)^2 + 
                    4\bigl(a_{12}+(N-1)\delta_{12}\bigr)^2} < 0
                \end{equation}
                and
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_stab_minus}
                    (a_{11}-\delta_{11}) + (a_{22}-\delta_{22}) + 
                    \sqrt{(a_{11}-a_{22}-\delta_{11}+\delta_{22})^2 + 
                    4(a_{12}-\delta_{12})^2} < 0
                \end{equation}
                hold simultaneously, where \Cref{eq:SI_stab_plus} and \Cref{eq:SI_stab_minus} are the conditions for $A^+ = A+(N-1)\Delta$ and $A^- = A-\Delta$ (\Cref{eq:SI_Apm_def}) to be negative definite, respectively. If $\Delta \preceq 0$ (negative semidefinite), then \Cref{eq:SI_stab_minus} is the binding condition.
            \end{theorem}

            \begin{proof}
                From \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian}, the Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ takes the block form
                \begin{equation}
                    \mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^* = \begin{pmatrix}
                        A & \Delta & \cdots & \Delta \\
                        \Delta & A & \cdots & \Delta \\
                        \vdots & & \ddots & \vdots \\
                        \Delta & \Delta & \cdots & A
                    \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                In the 2-dimensional case the blocks are
                \begin{equation}
                    A = \begin{pmatrix} a_{11} & a_{12} \\ a_{21} & a_{22} \end{pmatrix}, 
                    \qquad
                    \Delta = \begin{pmatrix} \delta_{11} & \delta_{12} \\ 
                    \delta_{21} & \delta_{22} \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                with entries defined in \Cref{eq:SI_jacobians_A,eq:SI_jacobians_B}. By \Cref{thm:SI_Soth} and \Cref{eq:SI_Apm_def}, the spectrum of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ decomposes as
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma(\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*) = 
                    \sigma(A^+) \cup \sigma(A^-)
                \end{equation}
                where $A^+ = A + (N-1)\Delta$ and $A^- = A - \Delta$. Since $A$ and $\Delta$ are both symmetric (by \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian}), so are $A^+$ and $A^-$, ensuring that all their eigenvalues are real. Using the closed-form eigenvalue formula for real symmetric $2\times 2$ matrices, the eigenvalues of each $2\times 2$ block are
                \begin{align}
                    \Lambda_{\pm} &= \frac{1}{2}\left[
                    \underbrace{\bigl(a_{11}+(N-1)\delta_{11}\bigr) + \bigl(a_{22}+(N-1)\delta_{22}\bigr)}_{\Lambda_a}
                    \pm 
                    \underbrace{\sqrt{\bigl(a_{11}-a_{22}+(N-1)(\delta_{11}-\delta_{22})\bigr)^2 + 
                    4\bigl(a_{12}+(N-1)\delta_{12}\bigr)^2}}_{\Lambda_r}
                    \right] \\
                    \lambda_{\pm} &= \frac{1}{2}\left[
                    \underbrace{(a_{11}-\delta_{11}) + (a_{22}-\delta_{22})}_{\lambda_a}
                    \pm 
                    \underbrace{\sqrt{(a_{11}-a_{22}-\delta_{11}+\delta_{22})^2 + 
                    4(a_{12}-\delta_{12})^2}}_{\lambda_r}
                    \right]
                \end{align}
                Stability requires all eigenvalues of $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ to be strictly negative, i.e. both $\Lambda_+$ and $\lambda_+$ must be negative. Since $\Lambda_r, \lambda_r \geq 0$, negativity of $\Lambda_+$ and $\lambda_+$ is equivalent to negativity of $\Lambda_a + \Lambda_r$ and $\lambda_a + \lambda_r$ respectively, giving \Cref{eq:SI_stab_plus,eq:SI_stab_minus}.

                To determine which condition is binding, note that $A^+ - A^- = N\Delta$. We consider two cases:

                \begin{itemize}
                    \item \textbf{Case 1: $\Delta \preceq 0$ (negative semidefinite).} 
                    Negative semidefiniteness of $\Delta$ (equivalently $\delta_{11}\delta_{22} - \delta_{12}^2 \geq 0$ and $\delta_{11} \leq 0$) implies $(N-1)\Delta \preceq 0$ for $N\geq 2$. By the eigenvalue monotonicity principle \cite[Cor.~4.3.12]{horn2012matrix}, $A^+ = A + (N-1)\Delta \preceq A - \Delta = A^-$, so $\lambda_+ \geq \Lambda_+$ and the binding condition is \Cref{eq:SI_stab_minus}.

                    \item \textbf{Case 2: $\Delta$ indefinite.} If 
                    $\delta_{11}\delta_{22} - \delta_{12}^2 < 0$, then $\Delta$ has one positive and one negative eigenvalue. Let $v$ be the unit eigenvector of $A^-$ associated with $\lambda_+$. By first-order eigenvalue perturbation theory \cite[thm.~6.3.12]{horn2012matrix}, the leading-order shift from $A^-$ to $A^+ = A^- + N\Delta$ is
                    \begin{equation}
                        \Lambda_+ - \lambda_+ = N v^\top \Delta v + O(N^2\|\Delta\|^2)
                    \end{equation}
                    so, to leading order, $\Lambda_+ > \lambda_+$ when $v^\top \Delta v > 0$. A computable sufficient condition for $v^\top\Delta v > 0$---obtained by bounding $v$ via the Gershgorin circle theorem \cite[Thm.~6.1.1]{horn2012matrix}---is
                    \begin{equation}
                        (a_{11} - a_{22})(\delta_{11} - \delta_{22}) + 
                        4a_{12}\delta_{12} > 0.
                    \end{equation}
                    When this condition holds, \Cref{eq:SI_stab_plus} is (to leading order) the binding condition; otherwise \Cref{eq:SI_stab_minus} binds. In practice, both conditions must be checked simultaneously, since the perturbative argument identifies which is likely binding but is not an exact substitute for verifying both inequalities.
                \end{itemize}
                In all cases, stability holds if and only if both \Cref{eq:SI_stab_plus,eq:SI_stab_minus} are satisfied simultaneously .
            \end{proof}
            While not nearly as simple as the one-dimensional case, \Cref{thm:SI_two_dimensional_stability} gives a complete characterization of the stability of symmetric Nash equilibria in two-dimensional symmetric influencer's games. The conditions are more complex than in the one-dimensional case, but they can be evaluated directly from the influence kernels and resource distribution. For higher dimensions, while a similar approach can be taken, the algebra becomes increasingly cumbersome and may not yield as interpretable conditions for stability. However, we can utilize \Cref{thm:SI_two_dimensional_stability} to analyze specific two-dimensional games of interest.
            
                
    \section{Rich Dynamics in One Dimensional Sentiment Landscapes}
        From the previous section we have a toolbox to study most one-dimensional influencer's games. To summarize, if our influencer game is in a 1-D environment, i.e.\ $\D\subset \R$ is compact and convex, $\B\subset \R$, the game is symmetric, and the influence kernel satisfies $f_{i}(x_i,b)>0$, $f_{i}(x_i,b)\in C^2(\D)$, and is quasi-concave in $x_i$ for all players $i\in I$, then existence of a symmetric Nash equilibrium is guaranteed by \Cref{thm:SI_sym_Nash}. Additionally, the local asymptotic stability of any such equilibrium under gradient-ascent dynamics can be determined via \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}. Finally, if the influence kernels are log-concave with respect to their strategies, then uniqueness of the symmetric Nash equilibrium is guaranteed by \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness}. 
        \subsection{Gaussian Influence Kernels}\label{subsec:SI_Gaussian}
            One of the most popular models for measuring influence kernels has been the Gaussian model of influence. Gaussian kernels have been used to approximate political influence \cite{yang2020us} as well as species home ranges and territories in ecology \cite{borger2008effects}. A Gaussian influence kernel has the following form: 
            \begin{definition}[Gaussian influence]
                A player $i\in I$ using strategy $x_i\in \D$, where $\D\subset\R$ is convex and compact, and environment $b\in\B\subset\R$, with Gaussian influence with bandwidth ('reach') $\sigma_i > 0$, has the influence kernel
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_Gaussian_infl}
                    f_i(x_i,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_i}\exp\!\left(-\frac{(b-x_i)^2}{2\sigma_i^2}\right)
                \end{equation}
            \end{definition}
            \begin{remark}
                The normalization constant $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_i}$ makes $f_i(\cdot,b)$ a valid probability density in $x_i$ \cite{billingsley1995probability}. Since all payoff quantities depend only on ratios of influence values (via the contest success function $G_i$), this constant cancels throughout and the results below are invariant to it. We include it for definitional precision.
            \end{remark}
            If the game is symmetric then $\sigma=\sigma_i>0$ for all $i\in I$. 
            
            \subsubsection{Symmetric Nash equilibrium}
            \begin{corollary}[Symmetric Gaussian Influence Nash 
            Equilibrium]\label{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with $\mathbb{D}, \mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}$, resource distribution $B(b) \geq 0$ with $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b) > 0$, and Gaussian influence kernels as defined in \Cref{eq:SI_Gaussian_infl}. Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x_1^*,x_2^*,\dots,x_N^*)$ with $x_i^* = x^*$ for all $i \in I$, where
                \begin{equation}
                    x^* = \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} b \cdot B(b)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)}
                    = \mathbb{E}_B[b]
                \end{equation}
