                provided $x^* \in \mathbb{D}$, where $\mathbb{E}_B[b]$ denotes the $B$-weighted mean of $b$. If $x^* \notin \mathbb{D}$, the symmetric Nash equilibrium is attained on the boundary of $\mathbb{D}$ by the constrained first order conditions.
            \end{corollary}

            \begin{proof}
                The Gaussian influence kernel is $f_i(x_i,b) = \exp\left(-\frac{(x_i-b)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)$, so
                \begin{equation}
                    d_i(x_i,b) = \pdv{}{x_i}\ln f_i(x_i,b) = 
                    \pdv{}{x_i}\left(-\frac{(x_i-b)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right) = 
                    \frac{b-x_i}{\sigma^2}
                \end{equation}
                From \Cref{lem:SI_requirements} the first order condition is
                \begin{align}
                    \pdv{u_i(\mathbf{x})}{x_i} &= \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, 
                    d_i(x_i,b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b) \\
                    &= \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\, \frac{b-x_i}{\sigma^2}\, 
                    G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\, G_i^c(\mathbf{x},b)
                \end{align}
                At a symmetric equilibrium $x_i^* = x^*$ for all $i \in I$, we have $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$ and $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}$, so $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b)G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N^2}$. Substituting:
                \begin{align}
                    \pdv{u_i(\mathbf{x})}{x_i}\bigg|_{\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^*} 
                    &= \frac{N-1}{N^2\sigma^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)(b - x^*)
                \end{align}
                Setting this to zero and noting $\frac{N-1}{N^2\sigma^2} > 0$ for $N \geq 2$:
                \begin{align}
                    0 &= \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)(b - x^*) \\
                    &= \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} b\, B(b) - x^* \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b) \\
                    \implies x^* &= \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} b\, B(b)}
                    {\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} = \mathbb{E}_B[b]
                \end{align}
                which is well-defined since $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b) > 0$ by assumption. Uniqueness of $x^*$ as an interior solution follows from \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness}. If $x^* \notin \mathbb{D}$, continuity of $u_i$ on the compact set $\mathbb{D}$ guarantees that a maximum is attained \cite[Thm.~4.16]{rudin1976principles}. Since $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}$ is a compact interval and $\partial u_i / \partial x_i$ is strictly decreasing in $x_i$ with unique zero at $x^*$, the utility $u_i$ is strictly increasing on $\mathbb{D}$ when $x^* > \sup\mathbb{D}$ and strictly decreasing when $x^* < \inf\mathbb{D}$. Hence the constrained maximizer is
                \begin{equation}
                    \hat{x} = \begin{cases} \sup\mathbb{D} & \text{if } x^* > \sup\mathbb{D}, \\ 
                    \inf\mathbb{D} & \text{if } x^* < \inf\mathbb{D}, \end{cases}
                \end{equation}
                which is the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium on $\partial\mathbb{D}$.
            \end{proof}            
            Thus the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium for a symmetric Gaussian influencer game with $N \geq 2$ players is the $B$-weighted mean of the resource distribution. This is analogous to the well-known median-voter result in spatial competition \cite{hotelling1929stability}, but here players converge to the mean rather than the median, a consequence of the Gaussian (rather than uniform or linear) influence kernel. 
        
            \subsubsection{Stability of the Symmetric Nash Equilibrium}
            Following \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} we have the following corollary on the stability of a symmetric Gaussian influence game. 
            \begin{corollary}[Stability of the Symmetric Gaussian Influencer Game's 
            Nash Equilibrium]\label{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with $N\geq 2$ players, Gaussian influence kernels, and interior symmetric Nash equilibrium $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b] \in \mathrm{int}(\mathbb{D})$ as in \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}. Then $x^*$ is locally asymptotically 
                stable under gradient-ascent dynamics if and only if
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma > \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B
                \end{equation}
                where
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma_B = \sqrt{\mathbb{E}_B[b^2] - \mathbb{E}_B[b]^2} = 
                    \sqrt{\frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} b^2 B(b)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} 
                    - \left(\frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} b\, B(b)}
                    {\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)}\right)^2}
                    = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_B(b)}
                \end{equation}
                is the $B$-weighted standard deviation of $b$. For $N=2$ the condition reduces to $\sigma > 0$, so the equilibrium is unconditionally stable.
            \end{corollary}

            \begin{proof}
                From \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}, $x^*$ is stable if and only if
                \begin{equation}
                    -\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d'(b) > 
                    \frac{N-2}{N-1}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d^2(b)
                \end{equation}
                For the Gaussian kernel, from \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian} we have $d_i(x_i,b) = \frac{b-x_i}{\sigma^2}$, so
                \begin{align}
                    d'(b) &= \pdv{}{x_i}d_i(x_i,b)\bigg|_{x_i=x^*} = 
                    \pdv{}{x_i}\frac{b-x_i}{\sigma^2} = -\frac{1}{\sigma^2} \\
                    d^2(b) &= d_i(x_i^*,b)^2 = \frac{(b-x^*)^2}{\sigma^4}
                \end{align}
                Substituting $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$:
                \begin{equation}
                    d^2(b) = \frac{1}{\sigma^4}\left(b^2 - 2b\,\mathbb{E}_B[b] + 
                    \mathbb{E}_B[b]^2\right)
                \end{equation}
                Inserting into the stability condition:
                \begin{align}
                    \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b) &> 
                    \frac{N-2}{N-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma^4}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} 
                    B(b)\left(b^2 - 2b\,\mathbb{E}_B[b] + \mathbb{E}_B[b]^2\right)
                \end{align}
                Since $\sigma > 0$, multiplying both sides by $\frac{\sigma^4}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}}B(b)} > 0$:
                \begin{align}
                    \sigma^2 &> \frac{N-2}{N-1}\cdot
                    \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\left(b^2 - 2b\,\mathbb{E}_B[b] 
                    + \mathbb{E}_B[b]^2\right)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} \\
                    &= \frac{N-2}{N-1}\left(\mathbb{E}_B[b^2] - 2\mathbb{E}_B[b]^2 
                    + \mathbb{E}_B[b]^2\right) \\
                    &= \frac{N-2}{N-1}\left(\mathbb{E}_B[b^2] - 
                    \mathbb{E}_B[b]^2\right) \\
                    &= \frac{N-2}{N-1}\,\mathrm{Var}_B(b)
                \end{align}
                Taking square roots (both sides non-negative):
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma > \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B
                \end{equation}
                For $N=2$, $\frac{N-2}{N-1} = 0$, so the condition becomes $\sigma > 0$, 
                which holds for any $\sigma_i > 0$ by the definition of the Gaussian kernel. Local asymptotic stability of $x^*$ under gradient-ascent dynamics then follows by the Lyapunov linearization theorem \cite[Thm.~1 §8.4]{hirsch2013differential}, since all eigenvalues of the Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ are strictly negative precisely when the stability inequality holds.
            \end{proof}
            The preceding corollary establishes that, provided $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b] \in \mathrm{int}(\mathbb{D})$, players in a symmetric Gaussian influencer game locally converge to the $B$-weighted mean of the resource distribution under gradient-ascent dynamics if and only if their influence bandwidth $\sigma$ exceeds the threshold $t_* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$.

            To see that the scaling factor $h(N) = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}$ is strictly increasing in $N$ for integer $N \geq 2$, write $\frac{N-2}{N-1} = 1 - \frac{1}{N-1}$, which is strictly increasing since $\frac{1}{N-1}$ is strictly decreasing. Since $h(N)^2 = 1 - \frac{1}{N-1} \to 1$ as $N\to\infty$, we have $h(N) \to 1$, so $t_* \nearrow \sigma_B$ from below as $N\to\infty$. This has two immediate implications. First, stability is harder to achieve in larger games: as $N$ increases, a wider influence kernel is required to guarantee convergence to the symmetric Nash equilibrium, and in the many-player limit the stability condition approaches the sharp threshold $\sigma > \sigma_B$. This limiting threshold has a natural interpretation: stability requires the player's influence bandwidth to dominate the resource bandwidth, a condition that parallels bandwidth thresholds arising in classical spatial competition models \cite{hotelling1929stability,drezner2024competitive}. Second, for $N=2$ the threshold vanishes entirely ($t_* = 0$), so the symmetric Nash equilibrium is unconditionally stable for any $\sigma > 0$ and any resource distribution; this is consistent with the general two-player result of \Cref{lem:SI_two_player_stability_multi_dim}. Intuitively, a larger $\sigma$ means each player's influence is more spatially diffuse and less sensitive to the positions of competitors, reducing the incentive to deviate and thereby stabilizing the equilibrium at the resource mean.
            \subsubsection{Three Player Dynamics} \label{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics}
                    The preceding subsection established that the stability of the symmetric Nash equilibrium in Gaussian influencer's games depends on the number of players $N$, with instability possible for $N > 2$ when $\sigma < \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$. It is therefore natural to investigate the dynamics that emerge below this stability threshold. We focus on the case $N=3$ as the simplest setting in which destabilization can occur, and characterize the resulting dynamics.

                    To facilitate this analysis, we assume throughout this subsection and subsequent sections that the resource distribution takes the form of a bimodal mixture of two Gaussians:
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution}
                        B(b) = \exp\left(-\frac{(b-(0.5-\alpha))^2}{2\sigma_r^2}\right) + 
                        \exp\left(-\frac{(b-(0.5+\alpha))^2}{2\sigma_r^2}\right)
                    \end{equation}
                    where $\alpha > 0$ controls the separation between the two modes and $\sigma_r > 0$ controls the spread of each mode. The constant normalization factor is omitted because it cancels in any ratio of influence values and therefore has no impact on the Nash position or its stability. The distribution is symmetric about $b = 0.5$ for all $\alpha$: the substitution $b \mapsto 1 - b$ maps $B(b)$ to itself (since $(1{-}b) - (0.5 \pm \alpha) = -(b - (0.5 \mp \alpha))$), so by the symmetry of the integrand $\mathbb{E}_B[b] = 0.5$ for all $\alpha, \sigma_r$. Hence by \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian} the symmetric Nash equilibrium satisfies $x^* = 0.5$ regardless of $\alpha$ or $\sigma_r$, while the stability threshold $t_* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$ varies with both (since $\mathrm{Var}_B(b)$ depends on $\alpha$ and $\sigma_r$), provided $0.5 \in \mathbb{D}$.
                    \begin{figure}
                        \centering
                        \includesvg[width=0.5\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig1/1d_resource_distribution_0_agents.svg}
                        \caption{The resource distribution used for the bifurcation diagram in \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation,fig:4player_bifurcation} and many other figures in future sections. The resource distribution is a bimodal sum of Gaussians with variance $\sigma_i^2$ and $\sigma_j^2$ and means at $0.5-\alpha$ and $0.5+\alpha$. As $\alpha$ increases the two peaks of the resource distribution become more separated. For most of the paper the variance of the resource distribution is fixed at $\sigma_i^2=\sigma_j^2=0.01$ and we vary $\alpha$ to see how the separation of the peaks impacts the dynamics.}
                        \label{fig:bimodal_resource}
                    \end{figure} 
                    The game dynamics depend sensitively on the geometry of the resource distribution. The bimodal form \eqref{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution} provides a one-parameter family that interpolates continuously between a unimodal distribution ($\alpha \to 0$) and a sharply bimodal one ($\alpha \gg \sigma_r$), constituting a natural benchmark for studying how resource heterogeneity drives strategic dispersion, as indicated in Jones et al. \cite{jones2022polarization}. Crucially, varying $\alpha$ while fixing $\sigma_r$ changes $\sigma_B = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_B(b)}$ without shifting the equilibrium $x^* = 0.5$, enabling a clean identification of the stability threshold $t_* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$ as a function of resource separation alone.

                    Later in this section we observe that games with $N \geq 3$ players may exhibit dynamical regimes characteristic of either a 3-player system or a 2-player system, depending on their local configuration and initial conditions. Generically, trajectories converge to one of the equilibrium types classified below, with the outcome determined by $\sigma$ and the initial strategic positions. This phenomenon appears in \Cref{fig:bifurc_local_games:b}  and in \Cref{fig:bifurcation_tree} when $N=6$ players partition into two groups of equal size $(3,3)$. From the 3-player dynamics illustrated in \Cref{fig:3player_equilibria}, we observe that multiple local equilibria can coexist, with the set of attainable equilibria  depending on the reach parameter $\sigma$. We classify the possible equilibrium configurations as follows.

                    \begin{definition}[Clustered Equilibrium]\label{def:SI_clustered_equilibria}
                        Let $T(I, \mathbf{X}, \mathbf{u})$ be a symmetric influencer game with $N$ players and common strategy space $\mathbb{D}$. A Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* \in \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$ is called a clustered equilibrium if the players partition into $k \leq N$ groups, where all players within a group share the same strategic position (i.e.\ the support of $\mathbf{x}^*$ contains at most $k$ distinct points). We denote such a configuration by the partition type $(n_1, n_2, \ldots, n_k)$ with $\sum_{m=1}^k n_m = N$ and $n_1 \leq n_2 \leq \cdots \leq n_k$.
                    \end{definition}
                    \begin{remark}
                        We use the term clustered equilibrium rather than local equilibrium to avoid ambiguity with the standard game-theoretic notion of a local Nash equilibrium, in which no player can improve their payoff by a sufficiently small unilateral deviation \cite{osborne1994course}. Every clustered equilibrium satisfying the first-order conditions of \Cref{lem:SI_requirements} is a  equilibrium in the standard sense; the term ``clustered'' refers only to the structure of the strategy profile.
                    \end{remark}
                We provide two special cases of clustered equilibria that are particularly relevant for the dynamics of 3-player games, and more generally for the dynamics of larger games that may locally resemble a 2-player or 3-player system depending on their configuration. 
                \begin{definition}[$(1, N-2, 1)$ Equilibrium]\label{def:SI_1N21_equilibrium}
                    A $(1, N-2, 1)$ equilibrium is a strategy profile of the form
                    \begin{equation}
                        \mathbf{x}^* = (x_i,\, \underbrace{y, \ldots, y}_{N-2},\, x_j), \qquad x_i \neq y,\quad x_j \neq y,\quad x_i \neq x_j
                    \end{equation}
                    where players $i$ and $j$ occupy distinct isolated positions and the remaining $N-2$ players share a common position $y \in \mathbb{D}$. This configuration requires $N \geq 3$.
                \end{definition}

                \begin{definition}[$(N-1, 1)$ Equilibrium]\label{def:SI_N11_equilibrium}
                    A $(N-1, 1)$ equilibrium is a strategy profile of the form
                    \begin{equation}
                        \mathbf{x}^* = (\underbrace{y, \ldots, y}_{N-1},\, x_i), 
                        \qquad x_i \neq y
                    \end{equation}
                    where all players except player $i$ share a common position $y\in \mathbb{D}$, and player $i$ occupies a distinct isolated position $x_i \in \mathbb{D}$. This configuration requires $N \geq 2$.
                \end{definition}

            \begin{remark}
                The symmetric Nash equilibrium $(x^*, x^*, \ldots, x^*)$ established in \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian} corresponds to the fully-pooled configuration $(N)$ in the notation of \Cref{def:SI_clustered_equilibria}. For $N = 3$, the four possible clustered Nash equilibrium types are $(3)$, $(2,1)$, $(1,2)$, and $(1,1,1)$, corresponding respectively to the symmetric Nash equilibrium, a $(N-1,1)$ clustered equilibrium, a $(1,N-1)$ clustered equilibrium, and a fully dispersed equilibrium. These four types are demonstrated numerically in \Cref{fig:3player_equilibria}. In addition, gradient-ascent trajectories may fail to converge to any equilibrium and instead cycle; the existence of such limit cycles in continuous-time gradient and replicator dynamics is well established \cite{hofbauer2003evolutionary,sandholm2010population}.
            \end{remark}

            In \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation} we examine how the bifurcation structure for a 
            fixed initial condition varies as $\sigma$ and the resource distribution separation parameter $\alpha$ change. The results demonstrate that the resource distribution geometry has a substantial effect on the critical values of $\sigma$ at which bifurcations occur. While \Cref{fig:3player_equilibria,fig:3player_bifurcation} report results for specific initial conditions, the existence and multiplicity of these equilibria are not artifacts of initialization. To verify this, we perform a systematic grid search over initial conditions combined with a trust-region Newton method \cite{conn2000trust} applied to the first-order stationarity conditions from \Cref{lem:SI_requirements}, solving for strategy profiles $\mathbf{x}$ satisfying $\nabla_{x_i} u_i(\mathbf{x}) = 0$ for all $i \in I$. The results for $\sigma = 0.16$ are presented in \Cref{fig:3player_heatmap}, which displays the distribution of equilibrium types attained across initial conditions and confirms that all four equilibrium configurations $(3)$, $(2,1)$, $(1,2)$, and $(1,1,1)$ occur for this parameter value.
 
                    \begin{figure}[ht!] 
                    \centering
                        \begin{subfigure}[b]{.47\linewidth}
                            \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig2/1d_3a_all_3_agents_s0.25.svg} 
                            \caption{$\sigma=0.25$: 1 shared unique equilibrium } 
                            \label{fig:3player_equilibria:a} 
                        \end{subfigure} 
                        \begin{subfigure}[b]{.47\linewidth}
                            \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig2/1d_3a_all_3_agents_s0.17.svg}
                            \caption{$\sigma=0.17$: 1-separated symmetrical non-unique equilibrium} 
                            \label{fig:3player_equilibria:b} 
                        \end{subfigure} 
                        \vspace{4ex}
                    
                        \begin{subfigure}[b]{.47\linewidth}
                            \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig2/1d_3a_all_3_agents_s0.16.svg}
                            \caption{$\sigma=0.16$: dampening cycle about a non-unique equilibrium}
                            \label{fig:3player_equilibria:c} 
                        \end{subfigure}
                        \begin{subfigure}[b]{.47\linewidth}
                            \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig2/1d_3a_all_3_agents_s0.15.svg}
                            \caption{$\sigma=0.15$: cycle about a non-unique equilibrium} 
                            \label{fig:3player_equilibria:d} 
                        \end{subfigure} 
                        \vspace{4ex}
                
                        \begin{subfigure}[b]{.47\linewidth}
                            \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig2/1d_3a_all_3_agents_s0.08.svg} 
                            \caption{$\sigma=0.08$: 1-separate an unsymmetrical non-unique equilibrium near peak} 
                            \label{fig:3player_equilibria:e} 
                        \end{subfigure} 
                
                        \caption{Five qualitatively distinct dynamical regimes for a 3-player 1-D influencer game, spanning four clustered Nash equilibrium types (\Cref{def:SI_clustered_equilibria}) and one cycling regime. In each sub-figure the left panel shows player trajectories in the 3-dimensional state space $(x_1,x_2,x_3)$ and the right panel shows the final positions on $[0,1]$. (a) $\sigma=0.25$: all players converge to the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium, type $(3)$. (b) $\sigma=0.17$: players converge to a dispersed $(1,1,1)$ clustered equilibrium; the realized position depends on initial conditions. (c) $\sigma=0.16$: players exhibit damped oscillations (spiral sink) about a $(1,1,1)$ configuration. (d) $\sigma=0.15$: persistent cycling with no convergence to a equilibrium. (e) $\sigma=0.08$: convergence to an asymmetric $(2,1)$ clustered equilibrium near one peak of the resource distribution. Initial condition $(0.4,0.55,0.6)$, $\alpha=0.5$, learning rate $0.001$.}
                        \label{fig:3player_equilibria} 
                    \end{figure}


                    \begin{figure}
                        \centering
                        \includesvg[width=.9\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig3/1d_bifurcation_equilibrium_combined_3_agents.svg}
                        \caption{The main figure (left upper corner), is a bifurcation diagram demonstrating how the resource distribution mode split $\alpha$ impacts the $\sigma$ values for bifurcations to occur in. Here we see that as $\alpha$ increases the symmetric Nash equilibrium becomes unstable at higher $\sigma$ values, leading to the emergence of the non-unique equilibria at higher $\sigma$ values. In each subfigure, a,b,c,d we take a random sample of 10,000 initial  conditions and use gradient ascent to find the equilibria they converge to. Each color represents a different equilibrium type: blue is the symmetric nash stable region, purple is the $(1,1,1)$ region, yellow is an estimated cycling region, and red is the $(2,1)$ equilibrium. Using a fixed initial condition we can estimate the boundary for the where cycles occur and where the $(2,1),(1,2)$ equilibria dominate. The intial condition used for the main plot is $(0.4,0.55,0.6)$. For figures a,b,c,d the initial conditions are randomly sampled, using the seed=42, parameters $\sigma =(a:0.25,b:0.18,c:0.125,d:0.05)$, and learning rates $(a:0.001,b:0.001,c:0.001,d:0.0001)$.} 
                        \label{fig:3player_bifurcation}
                     \end{figure}


                    \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation} demonstrates the richness of three-player dynamics: depending on the resource distribution and reach, players may converge to the symmetric Nash equilibrium, to one of several clustered equilibria, or not converge at all. This stands in sharp contrast to the two-player case, in which the symmetric Nash equilibrium is unconditionally stable (\Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability} with $N=2$, $t_*=0$). However, \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation} reports only convergent behavior for a fixed initial condition. \Cref{fig:3player_heatmap} provides a complementary snapshot for fixed $\sigma=0.16$ and $\alpha=0.5$: by initiating trajectories on a grid and applying a trust-region Newton method \cite{conn2000trust} to the stationarity conditions, we confirm that all four clustered Nash equilibrium configurations $(3)$, $(2,1)$, $(1,2)$, and $(1,1,1)$ coexist at this parameter value, together with unstable equilibria not visible from the convergent trajectories alone.

                    In later sections we observe that games with more than 3 players may exhibit the behavior of a 3-player system or a 2-player game depending on their local configuration and initial conditions. More precisely, when players cluster into sub-groups that are mutually far apart in strategy space, each sub-group behaves approximately as an independent symmetric influencer game of size equal to the sub-group cardinality, and the stability of each sub-group's local symmetric equilibrium is governed by \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability} applied to that sub-game size. Often these games converge to one of the 3-player dynamical regimes or a two-player local symmetric equilibrium depending on $\sigma$. This decomposition is illustrated in \Cref{fig:3player_equilibria} for 3-player games at small $\sigma$, and in \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation:b} when 6 players partition into two groups of equal size $(3,3)$. 

                    \begin{figure}[ht!] 
                        \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_1/fig4/1d_equil_heat_3_agents.svg}
                        \caption{Clustered Nash equilibrium types for $\sigma=0.16$ and $\alpha=0.5$, corresponding to the purple region (b) of \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation}. Two main categories are observed: the symmetric Nash equilibrium (stable only on the diagonal $x_1=x_2=x_3$) and asymmetric clustered equilibria. The latter include stable $(1,1,1)$ equilibria (green) and unstable $(2,1)$ equilibria (red). Grid: $10\times 10$ initial conditions; trust-region Newton method \cite{conn2000trust} with maximum radius $0.1$, maximum $20{,}000$ iterations, positional tolerance $10^{-5}$, gradient tolerance $10^{-4}$.} 
                        \label{fig:3player_heatmap}
                    \end{figure}

                \clearpage
                \subsubsection{Four Player Dynamics}
                    Extending our analysis to $N = 4$ players, dynamics analogous to the three-player case emerge. By the exchangeability symmetry of the game and the fact that the gradient-ascent flow commutes with permutations of players, the submanifold
                    \begin{equation}
                        \mathcal{H} = \{\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{D}^4 : x_1 = x,\, x_2 = x_3 = y,\, 
                        x_4 = z,\; x,y,z \in \mathbb{D}\}
                    \end{equation}
                    is forward-invariant under the gradient-ascent dynamics: if the initial condition lies in $\mathcal{H}$, the trajectory remains in $\mathcal{H}$ for all time. Restricting attention to $\mathcal{H}$, we observe a bifurcation from the centered $(1,2,1)$ clustered equilibrium, denoted $\mathrm{c}(1,2,1)$, to other mixed equilibrium types as $\sigma$ decreases below the symmetric Nash stability threshold $\sigma_0^*$ established in \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}. A key distinction from the three-player case is that below $\sigma_0^*$, a $(2,2)$ equilibrium emerges as the dominant attractor, in which the four players split into two pairs each converging to a local symmetric equilibrium. This suggests that influencer's games with $N$ players may, under appropriate initial conditions, decompose locally into smaller sub-games. In the limit, this decomposition may reduce to collections of 2- or 3-player local games. However, as illustrated in \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation}, determining which equilibrium type a given trajectory will converge to presents a significant challenge, as the basins of attraction depend jointly on the resource distribution geometry and the reach parameter $\sigma$. While the dominant equilibrium type remains consistent across $\sigma$, the realized equilibrium is highly sensitive to initial conditions. This is demonstrated in \Cref{fig:4player_bifurcation} (c,d), where the proportion of trajectories converging to each of the $(2,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(3,1)$, and $(1,2,1)$ equilibrium types varies substantially with initialization.

                    To characterize the full equilibrium landscape, we perform a systematic analysis over the submanifold $\mathcal{H}$ for fixed $\sigma = 0.16$ and resource separation $\alpha = 0.5$ (corresponding to region (b) of \Cref{fig:4player_bifurcation}), with results presented in \Cref{fig:4player_heatmap}. In addition to the symmetric Nash equilibrium $(4)$ and the stable $(2,2)$ clustered equilibrium, we identify an unstable $(3,1)$ clustered equilibrium and a semi-stable $(1,2,1)$ clustered equilibrium. Here semi-stable means the equilibrium is stable in some directions of $\mathcal{H}$ and unstable in others; its local structure is that of a saddle on the center manifold \cite{perko2001differential}. The coexistence of stable, unstable, and semi-stable equilibrium configurations confirms that the equilibrium structure of a 4-player influencer game below the threshold $\sigma_0^*$ is substantially richer than in the 2-player case, and that convergence behavior is not determined by $\sigma$ alone but depends critically on the geometry of the initial condition relative to the basins of attraction of each equilibrium type.

Sub-figures (a,b,c) are at $\alpha=0.5$, while subfig  ure (d) is at $\alpha=0.8$.}
                        \label{fig:4player_bifurcation}
                    \end{figure}

                        \caption{For a 4-player game restricted to the submanifold $\mathcal{H} = \{(x,y,y,z)\}$, the heatmap shows the clustered Nash equilibrium type attained from each initial condition. Two main categories arise: the symmetric Nash equilibrium (stable only on the diagonal $x_1=x_2=x_3=x_4$) and asymmetric clustered equilibria. The latter include the stable $(2,2)$ equilibria (green), the unstable $(3,1)$ equilibria (red), and the semi-stable $(1,2,1)$ equilibria (yellow). Grid: $10\times 10$ initial conditions in $\mathcal{H}$; trust-region Newton method \cite{conn2000trust} with maximum radius $0.1$, maximum $20{,}000$ iterations, positional tolerance $10^{-5}$, gradient tolerance $10^{-4}$.}

        \subsection{Gaussian Bifurcation of Equilibria into Local Games in Symmetric influencer's games}
            The results of \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian} characterize the equilibrium structure for $N = 3$ and $N = 4$ player games. It is natural to ask how this structure extends to larger player sets. From the preceding analysis, we know that games with $N > 3$ players can exhibit complex equilibrium landscapes in which subsets of players coordinate on local symmetric equilibria, effectively decomposing the $N$-player game into smaller sub-games. For $\sigma < t_*$, where $t_* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$ is the stability threshold from \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}, numerical evidence indicates that for Lebesgue-almost-all initial conditions the long-run equilibria resemble collections of local symmetric equilibria supported on strict subsets of players; see \Cref{fig:3player_heatmap,fig:4player_heatmap} for verification. We explore this behavior for a symmetric resource distribution in \Cref{fig:bifurc_local_games}. In \Cref{fig:bifurc_local_games} we identify two qualitatively distinct types of bifurcation as $\sigma$ decreases. The first type, denoted $\sigma_i^{(1)}$, are pitchfork-type bifurcations \cite{perko2001differential} to locally symmetric clustered equilibria, occurring when a real eigenvalue of the Jacobian $\mathbf{J}_{\mathbf{u}}^*$ crosses zero \cite{khalil2002nonlinear}, so that a subset of players collapses onto a shared strategic position. These bifurcations are associated with the loss of stability of the global symmetric Nash equilibrium, and the critical values $\sigma_i^{(1)}$ can be approximated analytically using the threshold conditions of \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}. The second type, denoted $\sigma_i^{(2)}$, are global bifurcations---including heteroclinic connections and basin-boundary metamorphoses---arising from changes in the basins of attraction of competing local equilibria, or from the emergence of new non-symmetric equilibrium configurations. Unlike type-1 bifurcations, type-2 bifurcations are not signalled by any eigenvalue crossing zero and are substantially harder to locate analytically; we examine this difficulty in greater detail in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_explanation_bifurcations}.

Here $\alpha=0.5$ controls the mode separation, and $\sigma_r=0.1$ is the standard deviation of each Gaussian component (variance $\sigma_r^2 = 0.01$).}
                \label{fig:bifurc_local_games:a}
            \end{figure}

As $\sigma$ decreases, the symmetric Nash equilibrium loses stability and bifurcates into multiple clustered or cycling regimes. Two bifurcation types are distinguished: $\sigma_i^{(1)}$ are pitchfork-type bifurcations \cite{perko2001differential} to clustered Nash equilibria, approximable analytically via \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability,cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}; $\sigma_i^{(2)}$ are bifurcations due to changes in basins of attraction. \Cref{fig:asymm_bifurcation:b} completes the 3-player bifurcation to show both the $(2,1)$ and $(1,2)$ equilibria.             \subsubsection{Explanation for Gaussian Bifurcation Behavior}\label{subsubsec:SI_explanation_bifurcations}
                While \Cref{fig:bifurc_local_games} demonstrates the occurrence of bifurcations, it also reveals how the number of players $N$ has a substantial effect on the structure of the bifurcation sequence. \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation} shows that as $\sigma$ decreases, the global symmetric Nash equilibrium successively splits into multiple distinct strategic clusters. For Lebesgue-almost-all initial conditions, each cluster undergoes its own pitchfork bifurcation at a distinct threshold value of $\sigma$ before splitting further. We observe numerically that, in the limit $\sigma \to 0$, the long-run equilibrium approaches a configuration consisting of at most $\lfloor N/2\rfloor$ distinct strategic positions, each occupied by at most two players; this is consistent with the Voronoi-partition limit established in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_exp_stability}. We state this as a conjecture, as a rigorous proof would require establishing global convergence of the gradient-ascent flow for all $\sigma > 0$, which remains an open problem.
                
                A natural explanation for the type-1 bifurcations identified in \Cref{fig:bifurc_local_games} is that the global $N$-player game decomposes into a collection of weakly coupled local sub-games, each of which admits its own local symmetric Nash equilibrium. More precisely, suppose players partition into clusters $C_1,\ldots,C_K$ with respective local equilibrium positions $\{x_k^*\}_{k=1}^K$. If the inter-cluster distance dominates the influence bandwidth, i.e.
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:decoupling_condition}
                    \sigma \ll \min_{k \neq \ell}\,|x_k^* - x_\ell^*|,
                \end{equation}
                then for each $b$ near $x_\ell^*$ the cross-cluster influence ratio $f_i(x_i,b)\big/\sum_{j\in C_\ell}f_j(x_j,b)$ is exponentially small in $(\min_{k\neq\ell}|x_k^*-x_\ell^*|)^2/(2\sigma^2)$, so inter-cluster interactions are negligible and each sub-game of size $|C_k|$ behaves approximately as an independent symmetric influencer game. By \cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}, the local symmetric equilibrium of each such sub-game is locally asymptotically stable if and only if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:local_stability}
                    \sigma > \sqrt{\frac{n-2}{n-1}}\,\sigma_{\mathrm{local}}
                \end{equation}
                where $\sigma_{\mathrm{local}}$ denotes the $B$-weighted standard deviation of the resource distribution restricted to the support of the local sub-game, i.e.
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma_{\mathrm{local}} = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{B_{\mathrm{local}}}(b)}, \qquad 
                    B_{\mathrm{local}}(b) = B(b)\cdot \mathbf{1}_{b \in \mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{local}}}
                \end{equation}
                for some local resource domain $\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{local}} \subseteq \mathbb{B}$.

                In practice, $\sigma_{\mathrm{local}}$ is not directly observable, as the partition of players into sub-games and the associated local resource domains are themselves outputs of the dynamics rather than fixed inputs. Nevertheless, a conservative (sufficient) estimate for the smallest $\sigma$ at which a group of $n$ players can sustain a local symmetric Nash equilibrium is obtained by taking
                \begin{equation}
                    \sigma_{\mathrm{threshold}}(n) = \sqrt{\frac{n-2}{n-1}}\,
                    \max_{\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{local}} \subseteq \mathbb{B}}\, 
                    \sigma_{\mathrm{local}}
                \end{equation}
                that is, by replacing $\sigma_{\mathrm{local}}$ with its worst-case value over all admissible local resource domains. If $\sigma > \sigma_{\mathrm{threshold}}(n)$, any local $n$-player symmetric Nash configuration is guaranteed to be stable by \cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}. For the bimodal distribution \cref{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution}, each half-distribution $B^\pm$ is approximately unimodal with standard deviation $\sigma_r < \sigma_B = \sqrt{\alpha^2 + \sigma_r^2}$, so $\sigma_{\mathrm{threshold}}(n) < t_*$ for all $n \leq N$, confirming that sub-games can sustain stable local equilibria at values of $\sigma$ lower than the global threshold $t_*$. This bound is explored further in the next subsection.

                \subsubsection{Estimate for Higher-Order Bifurcation Thresholds}\label{subsubsec:SI_exp_stability}

                A key feature of symmetric influencer's games is that as $\sigma \to 0$, each player's influence kernel $f_i(x_i, b)$ becomes increasingly concentrated around $x_i$, so that in the limit each player exerts near-total influence over the resources in their immediate neighborhood and negligible influence elsewhere. Formally, as $\sigma \to 0$ the Gaussian kernel 
                \begin{equation}
                    f_i(x_i, b) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma} 
                    \exp\!\left(-\frac{(b - x_i)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right) 
                    \;\longrightarrow\; \delta(b - x_i)
                \end{equation}
                in the sense of distributions \cite{billingsley1995probability}, so that---provided all player positions are distinct---the relative influence satisfies $G_i(\mathbf{x}, b) \to \mathbf{1}\bigl[i = \arg\min_{j}|b - x_j|\bigr]$ for $B$-almost-all $b$, and resource allocation approaches the Voronoi partition of $\mathbb{B}$ induced by the player positions \cite{aurenhammer1991voronoi}. (When two or more players share a position the Voronoi cell is split equally, consistent with the symmetric equilibrium structure of \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}.) This limiting behavior is illustrated in \Cref{fig:sigma_density}.
                \begin{figure}[htbp!]
                \centering
                    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}
                        \includesvg[width=\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_2/fig2/1d_probability_2_agents_sig_100.svg}
                    \end{subfigure}
                    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}
                       \includesvg[width=\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_2/fig2/1d_probability_2_agents_sig_25.svg}
                    \end{subfigure}
                    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}
                       \includesvg[width=\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_2/fig2/1d_probability_2_agents_sig_03.svg}
                    \end{subfigure}
                    \caption{Panels (a)--(c) show the effect of varying $\sigma$ on players' influence densities. Going from left (a) to right (c), $\sigma = 1, 0.25, \approx 0$. As $\sigma \to 0$, the Gaussian kernel concentrates on a Dirac delta \cite{billingsley1995probability}, and each player's influence domain converges to the Voronoi cell nearest to their position \cite{aurenhammer1991voronoi}.}
                    \label{fig:sigma_density}
                \end{figure}
                Assuming that in the limit $\sigma \to 0$ each group of players captures with near-unit probability the resources closest to their shared position, we obtain an upper bound for $\sigma_{\mathrm{local}}$ by partitioning the global resource distribution $B(b)$ into local sub-distributions associated with each group. Specifically, we partition $\mathbb{B}$ at the global symmetric Nash position $\mu^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$, defining the upper and lower sub-distributions
                \begin{equation}
                    B^+(b) = \begin{cases} B(b) & \text{if } b \geq \mu^* \\ 0 & \text{otherwise} 
                    \end{cases}, 
                    \qquad
                    B^-(b) = \begin{cases} B(b) & \text{if } b < \mu^* \\ 0 & \text{otherwise} 
                    \end{cases}
                \end{equation}
                so that $B(b) = B^+(b) + B^-(b)$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$. By \cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}, the local symmetric Nash of each sub-distribution is its $B^\pm$-weighted mean $\mu^\pm = \mathbb{E}_{B^\pm}[b]$. Applying \cref{eq:local_stability} to each sub-game of size $m_g$, the local stability thresholds for the two groups are
                \begin{align}
                    t^+ &= \sqrt{\frac{m_1 - 2}{m_1 - 1}}\,\sigma_{B^+} \label{eq:t_plus}\\
                    t^- &= \sqrt{\frac{m_2 - 2}{m_2 - 1}}\,\sigma_{B^-} \label{eq:t_minus}
                \end{align}
                where $m_g$ denotes the number of players in group $g \in \{1, 2\}$, and $\sigma_{B^\pm} = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{B^\pm}(b)}$ is the $B^\pm$-weighted standard deviation defined as in \cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}.

                This constitutes a first-order approximation. As illustrated in \Cref{fig:sigma_density}, players partition the strategy space near the midpoint of their positions rather than exactly at $\mu^*$, so the split point is better approximated by the midpoint of the local means $\mu^+$ and $\mu^-$:
                \begin{equation}
                    \mu_{\mathrm{split}} = \frac{\mu^+ + \mu^-}{2}
                \end{equation}
                This refinement can be applied iteratively: replacing $\mu^*$ by $\mu_{\mathrm{split}}$, recomputing $B^\pm$ and $\mu^\pm$, and repeating until the split points stabilize. For symmetric unimodal sub-distributions this fixed-point iteration converges geometrically to a unique split; for multimodal sub-distributions convergence is not guaranteed and the procedure should be treated as a heuristic approximation. Regardless, for a game with $N$ players, the binary-tree structure of the partition implies that at most $\lceil \log_2 N \rceil$ successive splits are required before each sub-group reaches size 2 or 3, at which point the stability conditions of \cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} apply directly. This procedure is formalized in \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate}.
                \begin{algorithm}[ht!]
                \caption{Bifurcation Threshold Estimation}\label{alg:SI_t_estimate}
                \begin{algorithmic}[1]
                \Require Number of players $N \in \mathbb{N}$, resource distribution $B : 
                    \mathbb{B} \to \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$, refinement iterations $R \in \mathbb{N}$
                \Ensure Sequence of estimated bifurcation thresholds 
                    $\{\sigma^{(k)}\}_{k=0}^{K}$, $K = \lceil \log_2 N \rceil$
                \State $K \leftarrow \lceil \log_2 N \rceil$; initialize 
                    $\mathrm{ts} \leftarrow [\,]$, 
                    $\mathrm{splits} \leftarrow [\,]$
                \For{$k = 0, 1, \ldots, K$}
                    \If{$k = 0$}
                        \State$\mu^* \leftarrow \mathbb{E}_B[b]$ 
                            \Comment{Global symmetric Nash, \cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}}
                        \State$\sigma^{(0)} \leftarrow \sqrt{\tfrac{N-2}{N-1}}\,
                            \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_B(b)}$
                            \Comment{Global stability threshold, \cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}}
                        \State$\mathrm{splits} \leftarrow [\mu^*]$; 
                            $\mathrm{ts}.\mathrm{append}(\sigma^{(0)})$
                    \Else
                        \State$(m_1, m_2, \ldots, m_{2^k}) \leftarrow 
                            \textsc{SplitPlayers}(N, k)$
                            \Comment{Partition $N$ players into $2^k$ groups}
                        \For{$r = 1, \ldots, R$}
                            \Comment{Iterative refinement of split points}
                            \State$\{B_g\}_{g=1}^{2^k} \leftarrow 
                                \textsc{Split}(B,\, \mathrm{splits})$
                                \Comment{Partition $B$ at current split points}
                            \State$\mu_g \leftarrow \mathbb{E}_{B_g}[b]$ 
                                for each $g = 1, \ldots, 2^k$
                            \State$\mathrm{splits} \leftarrow 
                                \tfrac{1}{2}(\mu_1 + \mu_2,\, \mu_2 + \mu_3,\, \ldots,\, 
                                \mu_{2^k - 1} + \mu_{2^k})$
                                \Comment{Update split points to inter-group midpoints}
                        \EndFor
                        \State$\sigma^{(k)} \leftarrow \max_{g}\; 
                            \sqrt{\tfrac{m_g - 2}{m_g - 1}}\,\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{B_g}(b)}$
                            \Comment{Conservative threshold across all groups}
                        \State$\mathrm{ts}.\mathrm{append}(\sigma^{(k)})$
                    \EndIf
                \EndFor
                \State\Return $\mathrm{ts}$
                \end{algorithmic}
                \end{algorithm}

            where the partition operator is defined as
            \begin{equation}
                \textsc{Split}(B,\, \{\mu_s\}_{s=1}^{S}) = 
                \{B_g\}_{g=1}^{S+1}, \qquad
                B_g(b) = \begin{cases} B(b) & \text{if } \mu_{g-1} \leq b < \mu_g \\ 
                0 & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}
            \end{equation}
            with the conventions $\mu_0 = \inf \mathbb{B}$ and $\mu_{S+1} = \sup \mathbb{B}$.
                
            \subsubsection{Effects of Number of Players on Higher Order Bifurcation Behavior}
                The number of players $N$ has a substantial effect on the bifurcation structure of symmetric influencer's games, particularly in determining the sequence of higher-order bifurcations and the types of equilibria that can be realized. This is demonstrated empirically in \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation}. In particular, \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation} shows that games with larger $N$ are more prone to successive bifurcations into smaller strategic clusters, with certain cluster configurations exhibiting greater asymptotic stability than others. Specifically, as established in \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate}, the number of successive binary splits required before each sub-group reaches a stable 2- or 3-player configuration scales as $\lceil \log_2 N \rceil$, so that larger games admit a richer hierarchy of intermediate bifurcation thresholds $\{\sigma^{(k)}\}_{k=0}^{\lceil \log_2 N \rceil}$.

                As discussed in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics}, 3-player sub-games can themselves exhibit complex behavior below the stability threshold $\sigma_0^* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$, including cyclic trajectories and non-symmetric equilibria of type $(1,1,1)$. When such sub-games arise as components of a larger $N$-player game, their local dynamics inject additional complexity into the global equilibrium structure, making precise prediction of long-run behavior difficult without detailed knowledge of the initial conditions and the geometry of the basins of attraction identified in \Cref{fig:3player_heatmap,fig:4player_heatmap}.

                    \centering

                        \caption{6 players} 
                        \label{fig:nplayer_bifurcation:b}

                    \end{subfigure}%%

Both have a learning rate scheduling, $\eta_t = 10^{-\max(3,\,5(T-t)/T)}$ with decay period 500 steps and minimum rate $10^{-3}$, where $T = 400$ and $t\in[0,400]$ where $t=0$ correspons to $\sigma=0.03$.}

                To examine the bifurcation complexity observed in \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation} in a tractable setting, we restrict attention to $N = 6$ players. This choice is deliberate: a 6-player game admits local sub-games of size 2 through 5, providing a sufficiently rich hierarchy of equilibrium types while remaining computationally feasible. By the sub-game decomposition framework introduced in \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate}, any $N$-player influencer game can in principle be analyzed as a nested collection of local games of decreasing size, terminating at the minimal stable configurations. By \cref{lem:SI_two_player_stability_multi_dim}, 2-player symmetric influencer's games with strictly log-concave kernels are unconditionally stable for all $\sigma > 0$ and cannot bifurcate further; similarly, a 1-player game is trivially stable. These therefore constitute the irreducible building blocks of the local game hierarchy.

                The equilibrium types admissible at each level of this hierarchy are as follows.

                \begin{itemize}
                    \item \textbf{3-player local games.} As established in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics}, a 3-player symmetric influencer game admits four qualitatively distinct dynamical regimes depending on $\sigma$ and the resource distribution: the globally symmetric $(3)$-equilibrium, a fully asymmetric $(1,1,1)$-equilibrium, an asymmetric $(2,1)$- or $(1,2)$-equilibrium, determined by the thresholds of \cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} and the basin structure characterized in \Cref{fig:3player_heatmap}.

                    \item \textbf{4-player local games.} A 4-player symmetric influencer game admits the globally symmetric $(4)$-equilibrium, a split $(2,2)$-equilibrium corresponding to two independent 2-player local Nash equilibria, and asymmetric $(3,1)$- or $(1,3)$-equilibria. The $(3,1)$- and $(1,3)$-configurations are not terminal: their 3-player components can themselves bifurcate further, potentially yielding $(2,1,1)$- or $(1,2,1)$-equilibria or cyclic sub-game behavior, as characterized above.

                    \item \textbf{5-player local games.} Analogously, 5-player local games admit the symmetric $(5)$-equilibrium together with asymmetric configurations including $(2,3)$-, $(3,2)$-, $(4,1)$-, and $(1,4)$-equilibria, each of which may cascade into the 3- and 4-player behaviors described above.
                \end{itemize}

                For a 6-player game, these local game types combine to produce the equilibrium landscape observed in \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation:b}, where configurations of type $(3,3)$, $(2,1,1,2)$, $(2,2,2)$, and cyclic behavior are all observed as $\sigma$ decreases. A representative bifurcation tree is presented in \Cref{fig:bifurcation_tree}, illustrating five possible sequences of transitions between local game types as $\sigma \to 0$. Several important caveats apply to this tree.

                \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
                    \item \textbf{Non-exhaustiveness.} The bifurcation tree in \Cref{fig:bifurcation_tree} depicts five realizations of the bifurcation sequence for a specific initial conditions and a fixed resource distribution. Many other bifurcation paths are possible, and the realized path depends sensitively on both the initial strategic configuration and the geometry of $B(b)$.

                    \item \textbf{Non-uniform path frequencies.} Not all bifurcation paths occur with equal probability. The relative frequency of different equilibrium types is governed by the sizes of their respective basins of attraction, which depend on $\sigma$ and the resource distribution. In particular, the $(3,3)$ which decompose into 3-player local games, tend to be the most frequently realized for many $\sigma$ values.

                    \item \textbf{Resource distribution dependence.} The resource distribution $B(b)$ has a direct effect on both the $\sigma$-thresholds at which bifurcations occur and the relative frequencies of different equilibrium types, as formalized in \cref{eq:local_stability}. This dependence is demonstrated in \Cref{fig:equilibria_probability}, where varying the mode separation parameter $\alpha$ of the bimodal resource distribution \cref{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution} shifts the bifurcation thresholds and alters the empirical frequencies of each equilibrium type.
                \end{enumerate}
                    

            \begin{figure}[ht!] 
                    \includegraphics[width=1\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_2/fig4/1d_bif_adp_marl_6_agents.pdf}
                    \caption{A sample bifurcation tree for a 6 player influencer game as sigma decreases. Here we see how local games may bifurcate into smaller local games as sigma decreases. However, this tree is not exhaustive as there are many other possible bifurcation paths that can occur based on initial conditions and resource distributions.The resource distribution here is the bimodial Gaussian distribution wiht $\alpha=0.8$. Additionally, paths do not occur with equal probability as some local games are more stable then others and the resource distribution has a major impact on the probability of different local games occurring and thus the bifurcation paths that can occur.}
                    \label{fig:bifurcation_tree} 
                \end{figure}
            
            \begin{figure}

Here we see that as $\alpha$ increases the symmetric Nash equilibrium becomes unstable at higher $\sigma$ values, leading to the emergence of the non-unique equilibria at higher $\sigma$ values, but the probability of different equalbiria types occuring also changes. In each subfigure, a,b,c,d we take a random sample of 20,000 initial conditions and use adaptive dynamics to find the equilibria they converge to. }
                        \label{fig:equilibria_probability}

            \clearpage
            \subsubsection{Effects of Asymmetric Resource Distributions on Bifurcation Behavior}\label{subsubsec:SI_asymmetric_bifurcation}

                Beyond the number of players $N$ and the reach parameter $\sigma$, the shape of the resource distribution $B(b)$ itself has a substantial effect on the bifurcation structure of symmetric influencer's games. In particular, breaking the symmetry of $B(b)$ introduces asymmetry into the local resource sub-distributions $B^+$ and $B^-$ defined in \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate}, which in turn shifts the local stability thresholds $t^\pm$ of \cref{eq:local_stability} and alters the relative frequencies of admissible equilibrium types. We demonstrate these effects in \Cref{fig:asymm_bifurcation} using the asymmetric bimodal resource distribution illustrated in \Cref{fig:asymm_bifurcation:a}, defined as
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_asymmetric_resource}
                    B(b) = \exp\!\left(-\frac{(b - (0.5 - \alpha))^2}{2\sigma_r^2}\right) + 
                    \frac{1}{2}\exp\!\left(-\frac{(b - (0.5 + \alpha))^2}{2\sigma_r^2}\right)
                \end{equation}
                with $\alpha = 0.5$ and $\sigma_r = 0.1$. This distribution retains the two-mode structure of \cref{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution} but breaks its reflective symmetry about $b = 0.5$ by assigning unequal weights to the two modes, so that the global symmetric Nash $\mu^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$ no longer coincides with the midpoint of the two modes.

                A key consequence of this asymmetry is that players may switch strategic clusters as $\sigma$ decreases, a phenomenon not observed in the symmetric case. This behavior arises because the two local sub-distributions $B^\pm$ now have unequal variances $\sigma_{B^+}^2 \neq \sigma_{B^-}^2$, so that the local stability thresholds $t^+$ and $t^-$ of \cref{eq:local_stability} differ between the two groups. As a result, the two local sub-games undergo their respective bifurcations at different values of $\sigma$, producing an asymmetric bifurcation sequence in which the relative attractiveness of different cluster configurations changes with $\sigma$. This is demonstrated in \Cref{fig:asymm_bifurcation:b,fig:asymm_bifurcation:c} for $N = 3$ and $N = 6$ players respectively.

                \begin{figure}[ht!]
                    \centering
                    \includesvg[width=.6\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_2/fig6/1d_bimodal_alpha_05_asym.svg}
                    \caption{The asymmetric bimodal resource distribution \cref{eq:SI_asymmetric_resource} used in \Cref{fig:asymm_bifurcation:b,fig:asymm_bifurcation:c}, with mode  separation $\alpha = 0.5$ and per-mode standard deviation $\sigma_r = 0.1$. The upper mode at $b = 0.5 + \alpha = 1.0$ is assigned weight $1/2$ relative to the lower mode at $b = 0.5 - \alpha = 0.0$, breaking the reflective symmetry of \cref{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution}. The resulting global symmetric Nash is $\mu^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b] \neq 0.5$.}
                    \label{fig:asymm_bifurcation:a}
                \end{figure}

                \subsubsection{Noisy Resource Distributions}\label{subsubsec:SI_noisy_bifurcation}

                To verify that the local-game bifurcation structure identified in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_asymmetric_bifurcation} is not an artifact of the smooth bimodal structure of \cref{eq:SI_bimodal_resource_distribution,eq:SI_asymmetric_resource}, we now examine a resource distribution with irregular local structure. The noisy resource distribution is constructed as follows. Let $b_1 < b_2 < \cdots < b_n$ be $n = 100$ uniformly spaced evaluation points on $[0.001, 0.999]$. At each point $b_i$, define the raw signal
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_noisy_raw}
                    y_i = \left|U_i + Z_i\right|, \qquad 
                    U_i \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathrm{Uniform}(0,1), \qquad 
                    Z_i \overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(0.5,\, 0.1^2)
                \end{equation}
                where $\{U_i\}$ and $\{Z_i\}$ are mutually independent. The absolute value in \cref{eq:SI_noisy_raw} ensures non-negativity, consistent with the requirement $B(b) \geq 0$ imposed throughout. The raw signal $\{y_i\}$ is then smoothed via a symmetric moving-average (box) filter of half-width $w = 10$:
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_noisy_smoothed}
                    B(b_i) = \frac{1}{2w+1}\sum_{j=i-w}^{i+w} y_j, \qquad i = w+1, \ldots, n-w
                \end{equation}
                where boundary points are handled by reflection padding. The resulting distribution $B(b)$ is illustrated in \Cref{fig:noisy_bifurcation:a}. It retains multiple local maxima and irregular local structure while being sufficiently regular for gradient-based adaptive dynamics, since $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B}, \mu)$ with $B(b) \geq 0$ $\mu$-almost everywhere as required by \cref{def:SI_utility_function}. Under this distribution, the local sub-distributions $B^\pm$ of \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate} are no longer well-approximated by simple unimodal distributions, so the local stability thresholds $t^\pm$ of \cref{eq:local_stability} are harder to estimate accurately. Nevertheless, the qualitative bifurcation behavior identified in \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian} persists: as $\sigma$ decreases below the global stability threshold $\sigma_0^* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$ of \cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}, players bifurcate into local strategic clusters, each converging to a local symmetric Nash equilibrium of the corresponding sub-distribution. This is demonstrated in \Cref{fig:noisy_bifurcation:b,fig:noisy_bifurcation:c} for $N = 3$ and $N = 6$ players respectively, where the qualitative bifurcation structure is consistent with that observed in \Cref{fig:bifurc_local_games,fig:nplayer_bifurcation,fig:asymm_bifurcation}, despite the increased complexity of the resource landscape.

                    \caption{The noisy resource distribution $B(b)$ defined by \cref{eq:SI_noisy_raw,eq:SI_noisy_smoothed}, evaluated at $n = 100$ uniformly spaced points on $[0.001, 0.999]$. The distribution is constructed by combining independent uniform and Gaussian random samples and smoothing with a symmetric moving-average filter of half-width $w = 10$, producing an irregular landscape with multiple local maxima while satisfying $B(b) \geq 0$ everywhere.}
                \label{fig:noisy_bifurcation:a}
                \end{figure}

                \begin{figure}[ht!]

Both panels are computed using gradient dynamics with the following parameters: $\sigma \in [0.03, 0.3]$ sampled at 400 equally spaced points; $40{,}000$ gradient steps per $\sigma$ value; equilibrium classification based on the final $10\%$ of steps ($4{,}000$ steps); learning rate schedule $\eta_t = 10^{-\max(3,\,5(T-t)/T)}$ with decay period 500 steps and minimum rate $10^{-3}$, where $T = 400$ and $t\in[0,400]$ where $t=0$ correspons to $\sigma=0.03$.}
