                \subsection{Beta Influence Kernels}\label{subsec:SI_beta}

                A second canonical influence kernel is based on the Beta distribution, which is particularly well-suited to strategy spaces $\mathbb{D} \subset (0,1)$ and provides a flexible family of unimodal influence profiles on a bounded domain. The standard Beta probability density function is
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:beta_dist}
                    f(b;\alpha,\beta) = \frac{1}{B(\alpha,\beta)}\,b^{\alpha-1}(1-b)^{\beta-1}, 
                    \qquad b \in (0,1),\quad \alpha,\beta > 0
                \end{equation}
                where $B(\alpha,\beta)$ is the Beta function
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:beta_func}
                    B(\alpha,\beta) = \frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\,\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}
                \end{equation}
                and $\Gamma(\cdot)$ is the Gamma function. The standard parametrization by shape parameters $(\alpha,\beta)$ is not directly interpretable in the influencer game context. We therefore reparametrize by the mode $x_i \in (0,1)$ and a concentration parameter $\sigma_i \in (0, \frac{1}{2})$ via the substitution
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:beta_subs}
                    \alpha_i = x_i\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i} - 2\right) + 1, \qquad 
                    \beta_i = (1-x_i)\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i} - 2\right) + 1
                \end{equation}
                Under this parametrization, $x_i$ is the mode of the resulting Beta density and $\sigma_i$ controls the concentration of influence around $x_i$: smaller $\sigma_i$ yields a more concentrated kernel, analogously to the role of $\sigma$ in the Gaussian case of \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian}. The constraints $x_i \in (0,1)$ and $\sigma_i \in (0, \frac{1}{2})$ are necessary and sufficient to ensure $\alpha_i, \beta_i > 1$, which guarantees that $x_i$ is indeed an interior mode of the density and that $f_i(x_i, b) \in C^2(\mathbb{D})$ as required by the general framework.

                \begin{definition}[Beta Influence Kernel]\label{def:beta_infl}
                    Player $i \in I$ is said to have a beta influence kernel with strategy $x_i \in \mathbb{D} \subset (0,1)$ and concentration parameter $\sigma_i \in (0, \frac{1}{2})$ if
                    \begin{equation}\label{eq:beta_kernel}
                        f_i(x_i, \sigma_i;\, b) = \frac{b^{\,\alpha_i - 1}(1-b)^{\,\beta_i - 1}}{B(\alpha_i,\, \beta_i)}, \qquad b \in (0,1)
                    \end{equation}
                    where $\alpha_i = x_i\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2\right)+1$,$\beta_i = (1-x_i)\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2\right)+1$, and $B(\cdot,\cdot)$ is the Beta function \cref{eq:beta_func}. The kernel satisfies $f_i(x_i,\sigma_i;b) > 0$ for all $b \in (0,1)$, $x_i \in (0,1)$, $\sigma_i \in (0,\frac{1}{2})$, consistent with the positivity requirement of the general framework.
                \end{definition}

                \begin{definition}[Polygamma Function]\label{def:polygamma}
                    The $p$-th order polygamma function $\psi_p : \mathbb{R}_{>0} \to \mathbb{R}$ is defined as
                    \begin{equation}
                        \psi_p(a) = \frac{d^{p+1}}{da^{p+1}}\ln\Gamma(a), \qquad a > 0,\quad p \in \mathbb{N}_0
                    \end{equation}
                    In particular, $\psi_0 = \psi$ is the digamma function and $\psi_1$ is the trigamma function. Key properties used below are $\psi_1(a) > 0$ for all $a > 0$ and $\psi_1$ strictly decreasing.
                \end{definition}

                \begin{lemma}[Log-derivatives of the Beta Influence Kernel]\label{lem:SI_beta_log_derivs}
                    Let $f_i(x_i,\sigma_i;\,b)$ be the beta influence kernel of \Cref{def:beta_infl}. Then:
                    \begin{enumerate}
                        \item \textbf{First derivative:}
                        \begin{equation}\label{eq:d_ln_beta}
                            d_i(x_i, b) := \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\ln f_i(x_i,\sigma_i;\,b) = \left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2\right)\left(\ln\frac{b}{1-b} - \psi_0(\alpha_i) + \psi_0(\beta_i)\right)
                        \end{equation}
                        \item \textbf{Second derivative:}
                        \begin{equation}\label{eq:d_ln_2_beta}
                            \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_i^2}\ln f_i(x_i,\sigma_i;\,b) = -\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2\right)^2\!\left[\psi_1(\alpha_i) + \psi_1(\beta_i)\right]
                        \end{equation}
                    \end{enumerate}
                    where $\alpha_i = x_i\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2\right)+1$, $\beta_i = (1-x_i)\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2\right)+1$, and $\psi_0$, $\psi_1$ are the digamma and trigamma functions of \Cref{def:polygamma}.
                \end{lemma}
                \begin{proof}
                    Since $\ln f_i(x_i,\sigma_i;\,b) = (\alpha_i-1)\ln b + (\beta_i-1)\ln(1-b) - \ln B(\alpha_i,\beta_i)$, differentiating with respect to $x_i$ and using $\frac{\partial \alpha_i}{\partial x_i} = \frac{1}{\sigma_i}-2 = -\frac{\partial \beta_i}{\partial x_i}$ together with $\frac{\partial}{\partial \alpha}\ln B(\alpha,\beta) = \psi_0(\alpha) - \psi_0(\alpha+\beta)$ yields \cref{eq:d_ln_beta}. Differentiating again and using $\frac{\partial}{\partial \alpha}\psi_0(\alpha) = \psi_1(\alpha)$ yields \cref{eq:d_ln_2_beta}.
                \end{proof}
                Since $\psi_1(a) > 0$ for all $a > 0$, \cref{eq:d_ln_2_beta} gives
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_i^2}\ln f_i(x_i,\sigma_i;\,b) \leq 0 \qquad \forall\, x_i \in (0,1),\; b \in (0,1)
                \end{equation}
                with strict inequality whenever $\sigma_i \in (0,\frac{1}{2})$. Hence the beta influence kernel is strictly log-concave in $x_i$, and \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} guarantees the existence and uniqueness of the symmetric Nash equilibrium for any symmetric influencer game with beta influence kernels.

            \begin{corollary}[Symmetric Nash Equilibrium: Beta]\label{cor:nash_beta}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with $N \geq 2$ players, common beta influence kernels as in \Cref{def:beta_infl} with common concentration parameter $\sigma \in (0,\frac{1}{2})$, strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset (0,1)$, resource space $\mathbb{B} \subset (0,1)$, and resource distribution $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$ with $B(b) \geq 0$ and $\sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b) > 0.$ Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,x^*,\ldots,x^*)$ where $x^* \in \mathbb{D}$ is the unique solution to
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_beta_nash_condition}
                    \psi_0(\alpha^*) - \psi_0(\beta^*) = \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)}
                \end{equation}
                with $\alpha^* = x^*\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}-2\right)+1$ and $\beta^* = (1-x^*)\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}-2\right)+1$, provided $x^* \in \mathbb{D}$. If $x^* \notin \mathbb{D}$, the symmetric Nash is attained on the boundary of $\mathbb{D}$.
            \end{corollary}
            \begin{proof}
                Applying \cref{lem:SI_requirements} with $d_i(x_i,b)$ from \cref{eq:d_ln_beta} and substituting $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$, $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}$ at the symmetric profile, the first-order condition reduces to
                \begin{equation}
                    \sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\left(\ln\frac{b}{1-b} - \psi_0(\alpha^*) + \psi_0(\beta^*)\right) = 0
                \end{equation}
                which rearranges to \cref{eq:SI_beta_nash_condition}. Uniqueness follows from \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} since $\ln f_i(\cdot,b)$ is strictly concave.
            \end{proof}

            \begin{remark}[Symmetric Resource Distribution]\label{rem:SI_beta_symmetric_nash}
                If the resource distribution satisfies
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} = 0
                \end{equation}
                then \cref{eq:SI_beta_nash_condition} reduces to $\psi_0(\alpha^*) = \psi_0(\beta^*)$, which by strict monotonicity of $\psi_0$ implies $\alpha^* = \beta^*$ and hence $x^* = 0.5$. A sufficient condition is that $B(b)$ is symmetric about $b = 0.5$, i.e. $B(b) = B(1-b)$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$, since then $\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)$ and $\ln\!\left(\frac{1-b}{b}\right)$ contribute equally and cancel in the weighted sum.
            \end{remark}

            \begin{corollary}[Stability of the Symmetric Nash: Beta]\label{cor:SI_stability_Beta}
                Under the conditions of \Cref{cor:nash_beta}, the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $x^*$ is locally asymptotically stable under best-response dynamics if and only if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_beta_stability}
                    \psi_1(\alpha^*) + \psi_1(\beta^*) > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\left(\frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)^2}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} - \left[\frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)}\right]^2\right)
                \end{equation}
                where the right-hand side is $\frac{N-2}{N-1}\,\mathrm{Var}_B\!\left(\ln\frac{b}{1-b}\right)$, the $B$-weighted variance of the log-odds $\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)$. For $N = 2$ the condition reduces to $\psi_1(\alpha^*) + \psi_1(\beta^*) > 0$, which holds unconditionally since $\psi_1(a) > 0$ for all $a > 0$.
            \end{corollary}
            \begin{proof}
                Applying \cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} with $d(b) = d_i(x_i^*,b)$ from \cref{eq:d_ln_beta} and $d'(b) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}d_i(x_i,b)|_{x_i=x^*}$ from \cref{eq:d_ln_2_beta}, and factoring out the common $\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}-2\right)^2 > 0$, the stability condition \cref{eq:SS_threshold_1d_general} becomes \cref{eq:SI_beta_stability} after recognizing the right-hand side as the $B$-weighted variance of $\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)$.
            \end{proof}

            \subsubsection{Bifurcation Behavior of Beta influencer's games}\label{subsubsec:SI_beta_bifurcation}
            The beta influence kernel exhibits bifurcation behavior qualitatively similar to that of the Gaussian kernel analyzed in \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian}, with the same catalogue of equilibrium types --- $(N)$, $(N-1,1)$, $(1,1,\ldots,1)$, and cyclic behavior --- arising as $\sigma$ decreases below the stability threshold implied by \cref{eq:SI_beta_stability}. The recurrence of these equilibrium types across two structurally distinct influence kernels suggests that the bifurcation phenomenology is a consequence of the log-concavity of the influence kernel rather than a property specific to the Gaussian functional form.

            A key quantitative difference, however, is that the ordering of the bifurcation regions differs between the two kernel types. In the 3-player Gaussian game (\Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics}), the symmetric Nash $(3)$ loses stability directly to a $(1,1,1)$-dominated region as $\sigma$ decreases. By contrast, in the 3-player beta influencer game, the symmetric Nash first transitions to a $(2,1)$-dominated region, which is subsequently replaced by a $(1,1,1)$-dominated region, followed by a mixed region of cyclic and $(2,1)$ behavior at smaller $\sigma$. This difference in bifurcation ordering is attributable to the distinct curvature properties of the two kernels: the stability condition \cref{eq:SI_beta_stability} involves the $B$-weighted variance of the log-odds $\ln\!\left(\frac{b}{1-b}\right)$ rather than the variance of $b$ itself, which alters the relative sizes of the basins of attraction of the competing equilibrium types. This is demonstrated in \Cref{fig:beta_bifurcation}, where the bifurcation regions for the beta kernel are compared directly against those of the Gaussian kernel from \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation}.

            \begin{figure}[ht!]
                \centering
                    \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/4_3/fig1/1d_bifurcation_equilibrium_combined_3_agents.svg}
                \caption{Bifurcation diagrams for a 3-player symmetric beta influencer game as a function of $\sigma$ and resource distribution mode separation $\alpha$, analogous to \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation} for the Gaussian case. In each panel (a)--(d), $20{,}000$ initial conditions are sampled uniformly from $\mathbb{D}$ and gradient dynamics are run to convergence; the resulting equilibrium type is recorded and the empirical frequency of each type is displayed. As $\alpha$ increases, the symmetric Nash $(3)$ loses stability at larger values of $\sigma$, consistent with \cref{eq:SI_beta_stability} since larger $\alpha$ increases $\mathrm{Var}_B\!\left(\ln\frac{b}{1-b}\right)$. Compared to \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation}, the bifurcation regions appear in a different order: the $(2,1)$ region precedes the $(1,1,1)$ region as $\sigma$ decreases, in contrast to the Gaussian case. The same four equilibrium types --- $(3)$, $(2,1)$, $(1,1,1)$, and cyclic --- occur in both cases, consistent with the view that the equilibrium catalogue is determined by log-concavity rather than the specific kernel form.}
            \label{fig:beta_bifurcation}
            \end{figure}
            \clearpage

        \subsection{Other Examples}
            In addition to the Gaussian and Beta influence kernels analyzed in \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian,subsec:SI_beta}, we introduce two further influence kernels that exhibit theoretically interesting behavior: an exponential influence kernel and the influence kernel introduced by Jones et al.\ in \cite{jones2022polarization}. These examples serve to illustrate the breadth of the influencer game framework and to connect our general stability results to existing models in the literature.

        \subsubsection{Exponential Influence Kernel}\label{subsubsec:SI_exponential_influence}
            \begin{definition}[Exponential Influence Kernel]\label{def:SI_exponential_kernel}
                Player $i \in I$ is said to have an exponential influence kernel with strategy $x_i \in \mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ and reach parameter $\sigma > 0$ if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_exponential_kernel}
                    f_i(x_i, b) = \frac{1}{x_i}\exp\!\left(-\frac{b}{x_i}\right), \qquad b \in \mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
                \end{equation}
                which is the probability density function of an exponential distribution with mean $x_i$, so that $x_i$ controls both the location and scale of the player's influence.
            \end{definition}

            \begin{corollary}[Symmetric Nash Equilibrium: Exponential]\label{cor:SI_exp_nash}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with $N \geq 2$ players, exponential influence kernels as in \Cref{def:SI_exponential_kernel}, strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}_{>0}$, resource space $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$, and resource distribution $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$ with $B(b) \geq 0$ and $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b) > 0$. Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,\ldots,x^*)$ with
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_exp_nash}
                    x^* = \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} b\,B(b)}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} = \mathbb{E}_B[b]
                \end{equation}
                provided $x^* \in \mathbb{D}$. Moreover, $\mathbf{x}^*$ is locally asymptotically stable if and only if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_exp_stability}
                    \sigma > \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B
                \end{equation}
                where $\sigma_B = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_B(b)}$ is the $B$-weighted standard deviation of $b$, as in \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}.
            \end{corollary}

            \begin{proof}
                For the exponential kernel \cref{eq:SI_exponential_kernel}, the log-derivative is
                \begin{equation}
                    d_i(x_i, b) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\ln f_i(x_i,b) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\left(-\ln x_i - \frac{b}{x_i}\right) = \frac{b - x_i}{x_i^2}
                \end{equation}
                Applying \cref{lem:SI_requirements} and substituting $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$, $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}$ at the symmetric profile, the first-order condition reduces to
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{N-1}{N^2\,(x^*)^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)(b - x^*) = 0
                \end{equation}
                Since $\frac{N-1}{N^2(x^*)^2} > 0$ for all $N \geq 2$ and $x^* > 0$, this gives \cref{eq:SI_exp_nash} directly. For stability, the second log-derivative is
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}d_i(x_i,b)\bigg|_{x_i = x^*} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\frac{b-x_i}{x_i^2}\bigg|_{x_i=x^*} = \frac{-x^* - 2(b-x^*)}{(x^*)^3}\bigg|_{x_i=x^*} = \frac{2b - x^*}{(x^*)^3} - \frac{2}{(x^*)^2}
                \end{equation}
                Noting that at $x_i = x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$, we have $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}}B(b)(b-x^*) = 0$, so the stability condition of \cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} simplifies to \cref{eq:SI_exp_stability} after the same algebraic steps as in the proof of \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}, with $d(b) = \frac{b-x^*}{(x^*)^2}$ playing the role of $\frac{b-x^*}{\sigma^2}$.
            \end{proof}
            The exponential influence kernel is notable in that it yields the same symmetric Nash position $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$ and the same stability threshold $\sigma > \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$ as the Gaussian kernel (\Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}), despite operating on an unbounded strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}_{>0}$. This demonstrates that the core stability results of \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian} extend beyond compact strategy spaces, provided a symmetric Nash equilibrium exists in the interior of $\mathbb{D}$.

        \subsubsection{The Jones et al.\ Influence Kernel}\label{subsubsec:SI_jones_model}
            Jones et al.\ \cite{jones2022polarization} study a political influencer game in which candidates' positions diverge from the median voter equilibrium due to factors including asymmetric resource distributions, fixed third-party competitors, and abstaining voters. Their model employs the influence kernel
            \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_Jones_influence_kernel}
                f_i(x_i, b) = |x_i - b|^{\,p}, \qquad p \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}
            \end{equation}
            which measures a player's influence at resource point $b$ as a power of their distance from $b$. We note that this kernel does not satisfy $f_i(x_i,b) > 0$ at $x_i = b$; we therefore assume $x_i \neq b$ for all $b \in \mathbb{B}$ throughout this subsection, or equivalently that $\mathbb{B}$ is chosen so that no resource point coincides with any player's equilibrium position.

            The log-concavity properties of \cref{eq:SI_Jones_influence_kernel} depend critically on the sign of $p$:
            \begin{itemize}
                \item If $p > 0$, then $\ln f_i(x_i,b) = p\ln|x_i - b|$ is concave in $x_i$ away from $x_i = b$.
                \item If $p < 0$, then $\ln f_i(x_i,b) = p\ln|x_i - b|$ is convex in $x_i$ away from $x_i = b$.
            \end{itemize}
            By \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness}, log-concavity is sufficient for the existence of a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium, while \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability} implies that a globally convex log-kernel cannot admit a stable symmetric Nash equilibrium. We therefore expect instability for $p < 0$, which we now verify.

            \begin{corollary}[Stability of the Jones et al.\ Symmetric Nash]\label{cor:SI_jones_stability}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric $N$-player influencer game with Jones et al.\ influence kernels \cref{eq:SI_Jones_influence_kernel} and resource distribution $B(b) \geq 0$. If an interior symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,\ldots,x^*)$ exists, then it is locally asymptotically stable if and only if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_jones_stability}
                    \frac{1}{p} > \frac{N-2}{N-1}
                \end{equation}
                In particular:
                \begin{enumerate}
                    \item For $p < 0$: the condition \cref{eq:SI_jones_stability} requires $\frac{1}{p} > \frac{N-2}{N-1} \geq 0$, which cannot hold since $\frac{1}{p} < 0$. Hence the symmetric Nash is never locally asymptotically stable for $p < 0$, for any $N \geq 2$.
                    \item For $p > 0$ and $N = 2$: the condition reduces to $\frac{1}{p} > 0$, which holds for all $p > 0$. Hence the symmetric Nash is unconditionally stable for any two-player game with $p > 0$.
                    \item For $p > 0$ and $N \geq 3$: the condition \cref{eq:SI_jones_stability} requires $p < \frac{N-1}{N-2}$, so stability is lost for sufficiently large $p$.
                \end{enumerate}
            \end{corollary}

            \begin{proof}
                For the Jones kernel, the log-derivative and its $x_i$-derivative are
                \begin{align}
                    d_i(x_i, b) &= \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\ln|x_i-b|^p = \frac{p}{x_i - b} \label{eq:SI_jones_d}\\
                    \frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}d_i(x_i,b) &= -\frac{p}{(x_i-b)^2} \label{eq:SI_jones_dprime}
                \end{align}
                Substituting into the stability condition of \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}:
                \begin{equation}
                    -\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\cdot\left(-\frac{p}{(x^*-b)^2}\right) > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\cdot\frac{p^2}{(x^*-b)^2}
                \end{equation}
                Since $\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,\frac{1}{(x^*-b)^2} > 0$, dividing both 
                sides by this quantity gives
                \begin{equation}
                    p > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\,p^2
                \end{equation}
                For $p \neq 0$, dividing by $p^2 > 0$ yields \cref{eq:SI_jones_stability}. The three cases follow immediately from the sign of $p$ and the value of $N$.
            \end{proof}

            The instability result for $p < 0$ is consistent with the analysis in Jones et al.\ \cite{jones2022polarization}, where candidate divergence from the median is attributed precisely to the destabilizing effect of the power-law kernel with negative exponent. Importantly, \Cref{cor:SI_jones_stability} shows that this instability is not a feature of the political model specifically, but a direct consequence of the convexity of $\ln f_i$ for $p < 0$, as predicted by the general theory of \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}. In a later section we demonstrate that the qualitative political polarization dynamics of Jones et al.\ \cite{jones2022polarization} can be reproduced within our framework using log-concave influence kernels.
                    
       
    \section{Multi-Dimensional Sentiment Landscapes}\label{sec:SI_multidimensional}
        The general influencer game framework of \Cref{subsec:SI_model} is defined for arbitrary strategy and resource spaces $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ and $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ with $L \geq 1$. In this section we extend the analysis to multi-dimensional settings $L > 1$, examining three canonical multi-dimensional influence kernels: the multivariate Gaussian kernel (\Cref{subsec:SI_mvg}) and the Dirichlet kernel in mode parametrization (\Cref{subsec:SI_dirichlet_mode}). In each case we derive the symmetric Nash equilibrium position and stability conditions as explicit functions of the resource distribution $B(b)$ and the kernel parameters, extending the 1-dimensional results.

        \subsection{Multivariate Gaussian Influence Kernel}\label{subsec:SI_mvg}

            \begin{definition}[Multivariate Gaussian Influence Kernel]\label{def:SI_mvg_kernel}
                Player $i \in I$ is said to have a multivariate Gaussian influence kernel with strategy $x_i \in \mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$ and positive definite covariance matrix $\Sigma \in \mathbb{R}^{L \times L}$ if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_mvg_kernel}
                    f_i(x_i, b) = \exp\!\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}(b - x_i)^\top \Sigma^{-1} (b - x_i)\right), \qquad b \in \mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^L
                \end{equation}
                The normalization constant of the multivariate Gaussian density is omitted since it does not depend on $x_i$ and therefore cancels in the relative influence $G_i(\mathbf{x},b)$ of \cref{eq:SI_relative_influence}.
            \end{definition}
            
            The precision matrix $\Gamma := \Sigma^{-1}$ is symmetric positive definite, with entries $\gamma_{lj} = [\Gamma]_{lj}$ for $l,j = 1,\ldots,L$. The diagonal entries $\gamma_{ll}$ control the concentration of influence in each strategic dimension $l$, while the off-diagonal entries $\gamma_{lj}$, $l \neq j$, couple the dimensions and determine the orientation of the influence ellipsoid. In particular:
            \begin{itemize}
                \item A diagonal precision matrix $\Gamma = \mathrm{diag}(\gamma_{11},\ldots,\gamma_{LL})$ corresponds to strategically independent dimensions, with influence spread $\sigma_l = \gamma_{ll}^{-1/2}$ in dimension $l$.
                \item Off-diagonal entries $\gamma_{lj} \neq 0$ introduce coupling between strategic dimensions, tilting the influence ellipsoid and potentially affecting the stability of the symmetric Nash equilibrium, as we examine below.
            \end{itemize}

        \subsubsection{Symmetric Nash Equilibrium}\label{subsec:SI_mvg_nash}
        
            \begin{corollary}[Symmetric Nash Equilibrium: Multivariate Gaussian]\label{cor:SI_mvg_nash}
                Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with $N \geq 2$ players, multivariate 
                Gaussian influence kernels as in \Cref{def:SI_mvg_kernel} with common precision matrix $\Gamma = \Sigma^{-1}$, strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$, resource space $\mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^L$, and resource distribution $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$ with $B(b) \geq 0$ and $\int_\mathbb{B} B(b)\,d\mu(b) > 0$. Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,\ldots,x^*)$ with
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_mvg_nash}
                    x_{(i,l)}^* = \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,b_l}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)} = \mathbb{E}_B[b_l] \qquad \forall\, i \in I,\; l = 1,\ldots,L
                \end{equation}
                That is, $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$, the $B$-weighted mean of the resource distribution, regardless of the precision matrix $\Gamma$.
            \end{corollary}

            \begin{proof}
                The log-derivative of the multivariate Gaussian kernel in dimension $l$ is
                \begin{align}
                    d_{(i,l)}(x_i, b) &= \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\ln f_i(x_i,b) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}(b-x_i)^\top\Gamma(b-x_i)\right) \notag \\
                    &= \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^L\sum_{j=1}^L (b_k - x_{(i,k)})(b_j - x_{(i,j)})\gamma_{kj}\right) \notag \\
                    &= \sum_{j=1}^L \gamma_{lj}(b_j - x_{(i,j)}) \label{eq:SI_mvg_log_deriv}
                \end{align}
                where we have used the symmetry $\gamma_{lj} = \gamma_{jl}$. Applying \cref{lem:SI_requirements} and substituting $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$, $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}$ at the symmetric profile, the first-order condition in dimension $l$ becomes
                \begin{equation}
                    \frac{N-1}{N^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b) \sum_{j=1}^L \gamma_{lj}(b_j - x_{(i,j)}^*) = 0\qquad \forall\, l = 1,\ldots,L
                \end{equation}
                In matrix form, this is $\Gamma\left(\mathbb{E}_B[b] - x^*\right) = 0$. Since $\Gamma$ is positive definite and hence invertible, the unique solution is $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$, giving \cref{eq:SI_mvg_nash}. Uniqueness of the symmetric Nash follows from \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness}, since the multivariate Gaussian kernel is strictly log-concave in $x_i$ for any positive definite $\Gamma$.
            \end{proof}
            
            The result \cref{eq:SI_mvg_nash} is a direct multi-dimensional analogue of \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}: the symmetric Nash equilibrium is always the $B$-weighted centroid of the resource distribution, independently of the covariance structure $\Sigma$. The covariance matrix affects the stability of this equilibrium but not its location, as we now examine.

        \subsubsection{Stability of the Symmetric Nash Equilibrium}\label{subsec:SI_mvg_stability}
        
            \begin{corollary}[Stability of the Symmetric Nash: Multivariate Gaussian with 
                Diagonal Precision Matrix]\label{cor:SI_mvg_stability}Let $T$ be a symmetric $N$-player influencer game with multivariate Gaussian influence kernels as in \Cref{def:SI_mvg_kernel}, $\mathbb{D}, \mathbb{B} \subset \mathbb{R}^2$, and diagonal isotropic precision matrix
                \begin{equation}
                    \Gamma = \Sigma^{-1} = \frac{1}{\sigma^2}I_2 = 
                    \begin{pmatrix} 
                        \sigma^{-2} & 0 \\ 
                        0 & \sigma^{-2} 
                    \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                for some $\sigma > 0$. Then the symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,\ldots,x^*)$ with $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$ is locally asymptotically stable if and only if
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_mvg_stability}
                    \sigma^2 > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Var}_B(b_1) + \mathrm{Var}_B(b_2) + \sqrt{\left(\mathrm{Var}_B(b_1) - \mathrm{Var}_B(b_2)\right)^2 + 4\,\mathrm{Cov}_B(b_1,b_2)^2}}{2}
                \end{equation}
                where $\mathrm{Var}_B(b_l) = \mathbb{E}_B[b_l^2] - \mathbb{E}_B[b_l]^2$ and $\mathrm{Cov}_B(b_1,b_2) = \mathbb{E}_B[b_1 b_2] - \mathbb{E}_B[b_1]\mathbb{E}_B[b_2]$ are the $B$-weighted variance and covariance of the resource coordinates. The right-hand side of \cref{eq:SI_mvg_stability} equals $\frac{N-2}{N-1}\Lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_B)$, where $\Lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_B)$ is the largest eigenvalue of the $B$-weighted covariance matrix of $b$,
                \begin{equation}
                    \Sigma_B = \begin{pmatrix} \mathrm{Var}_B(b_1) & \mathrm{Cov}_B(b_1,b_2) \\ \mathrm{Cov}_B(b_1,b_2) & \mathrm{Var}_B(b_2) \end{pmatrix}
                \end{equation}
                so the stability condition \cref{eq:SI_mvg_stability} is equivalently $\sigma^2 > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\Lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_B)$.
            \end{corollary}
            
            \begin{proof}
                From \cref{eq:SI_mvg_log_deriv} with $\gamma_{lj} = \sigma^{-2}\delta_{lj}$, the log-derivative simplifies to $d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b) = \sigma^{-2}(b_l - x_{(i,l)})$, which is the direct multi-dimensional analogue of the 1-D Gaussian case. The second mixed log-derivatives are
                \begin{align}
                    \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_{(i,l)}\,\partial x_{(i,m)}}\ln f_i(x_i,b)\bigg|_{x_i=x^*} &= -\frac{\delta_{lm}}{\sigma^2}\label{eq:SI_mvg_hessian_diag}
                \end{align}
                so the diagonal block $A$ of the Jacobian \cref{eq:paper_jacobian_general} has entries
                \begin{equation}
                    a_{lm} = \frac{N-1}{N^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\left(-\frac{\delta_{lm}}{\sigma^2} + \frac{N-2}{N}\cdot\frac{(b_l - x_l^*)(b_m - x_m^*)}{\sigma^4}\right)
                \end{equation}
                and the off-diagonal block $\Delta$ has entries
                \begin{equation}
                    \delta_{lm} = -\frac{1}{N^2}\cdot\frac{N-2}{N}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma^4}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)(b_l - x_l^*)(b_m - x_m^*)
                \end{equation}
                Forming $A^- = A - \Delta$ and applying \Cref{thm:SI_two_dimensional_stability}, the stability condition reduces to the largest eigenvalue of $A^-$ being negative. Direct computation gives
                \begin{equation}
                    A^- = \frac{N-1}{N^2}\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma^2}I_2 + \frac{N-2}{N-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma^4}\Sigma_B\right)
                \end{equation}
                Since $\frac{N-1}{N^2} > 0$, negative definiteness of $A^-$ is equivalent to $\frac{1}{\sigma^2}I_2 \succ \frac{N-2}{N-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma^4}\Sigma_B$, i.e. $\sigma^2 I_2 \succ \frac{N-2}{N-1}\Sigma_B$, which holds if and only if $\sigma^2 > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\Lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_B)$. Substituting the eigenvalue formula for the $2\times 2$ symmetric matrix $\Sigma_B$ gives \cref{eq:SI_mvg_stability}.
            \end{proof}
            \Cref{cor:SI_mvg_stability} is the natural multi-dimensional analogue of \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}: the stability threshold for the isotropic multivariate Gaussian game is determined by the largest eigenvalue of the $B$-weighted covariance matrix $\Sigma_B$, which corresponds to the direction of maximum variance in the resource distribution. Intuitively, instability arises first along the principal axis of $\Sigma_B$, and the players' influence spread $\sigma^2$ must exceed the scaled variance in that direction to maintain stability. This result illustrates how multi-dimensional resource geometry directly governs the stability of the symmetric Nash equilibrium, beyond what can be inferred from 1-dimensional marginals alone.

\subsubsection{Bifurcation Behavior of the Multivariate Gaussian Influencer Game}\label{subsubsec:SI_mvg_bifurcation}
    \Cref{fig:2d_mvg_bifurcation,fig:2d_agent_density} demonstrate the bifurcation structure of the symmetric multivariate Gaussian influencer game as $\sigma$ decreases. The qualitative behavior mirrors the 1-dimensional case of \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian}: for $N = 2$ players the game converges to the global symmetric Nash $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$ for all $\sigma > 0$, consistent with \Cref{lem:SI_two_player_stability_multi_dim}, while for $N \geq 3$ the symmetric Nash loses stability below the threshold \cref{eq:SI_mvg_stability} and players partition into local strategic clusters, each converging to a local symmetric Nash equilibrium of the corresponding sub-distribution. However, the higher-dimensional setting introduces additional degrees of freedom: players are no longer constrained to move along a line, and the local sub-games admit a richer set of cluster configurations than in the 1-dimensional case. In particular, the equilibrium partition type $(n_1, n_2, \ldots, n_k)$ of \Cref{def:SI_clustered_equilibria} need not correspond to a linear ordering of positions, and multiple geometrically distinct cluster configurations may coexist at the same partition type.

    Cyclic behavior analogous to that observed in the 1-dimensional 3-player case (\Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics}) are also present in the 2-dimensional setting, as demonstrated in \Cref{fig:2d_cycles} for $N = 3$ and $N = 5$ players. Together, \Cref{fig:2d_mvg_bifurcation,fig:2d_agent_density,fig:2d_cycles} confirm that the local-game decomposition framework of \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate} extends to multiple dimensions: the hierarchy of bifurcation thresholds, the catalogue of admissible equilibrium types, and the occurrence of cyclic behavior all persist under the multivariate Gaussian kernel. In the following subsection we push this analysis further by examining a more complex influence kernel defined on a bounded multi-dimensional domain.

    The resource distribution used in \Cref{fig:2d_mvg_bifurcation,fig:2d_agent_density,fig:2d_cycles} is a symmetric mixture of four isotropic multivariate Gaussians centered at the corners of $[0,5]^2$:\begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_mvg_resource_distribution}B(b) = \sum_{l=1}^{4}\exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2}(b - \mu_l)^\top \Sigma_r^{-1}(b - \mu_l)\right), \qquad b \in [0,5]^2\end{equation}where $\mu_1 = (0,0)^\top$, $\mu_2 = (5,0)^\top$, $\mu_3 = (0,5)^\top$, $\mu_4 = (5,5)^\top$, and $\Sigma_r = I_2$. By the symmetry of the mixture, the $B$-weighted mean is $\mathbb{E}_B[b] = (2.5, 2.5)^\top$, so the global symmetric Nash equilibrium is $x^* = (2.5, 2.5)^\top$ by \Cref{cor:SI_mvg_nash}. The $B$-weighted covariance matrix is $\Sigma_B = \mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{B,1}^2, \sigma_{B,2}^2)$ with $\sigma_{B,1}^2 = \sigma_{B,2}^2$ by symmetry, and the stability threshold \cref{eq:SI_mvg_stability} reduces to $\sigma^2 > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\sigma_{B,1}^2$.

    \subsection{Dirichlet Influence Kernel: Mode Parametrization}\label{subsec:SI_dirichlet_mode}

    The Dirichlet distribution admits an alternative parametrization by its mode.The mode parametrization has the advantage of centering the influence kernel exactly at the player's chosen strategy $x_i$ on the simplex, without requiring one component of the concentration vector to be fixed exogenously.This makes the mode parametrization more natural for influencer's games, where $x_i$ is interpreted directly as the player's target position in the resource space.

    \begin{definition}[Dirichlet Influence Kernel: Mode Parametrization]
    \label{def:SI_dirichlet_mode_kernel}
        Let $\Delta^{L-1} = \{b \in \mathbb{R}^L : b_l > 0,\, \sum_{l=1}^L b_l = 1\}$ denote the open $(L-1)$-simplex.
        Player $i \in I$ is said to have a mode-parametrized Dirichlet influence kernel with strategy $x_i \in \Delta^{L-1}$ and concentration parameter $\sigma > 0$ if the concentration vector is defined componentwise by
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_alpha}
            \alpha_{(i,l)} = 1 + \frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)}, \qquad l = 1,\ldots,L
        \end{equation}
        so that $\alpha_0 := \sum_{l=1}^L \alpha_{(i,l)} = L + \frac{1}{\sigma}$.
        Under this parametrization, the mode of the Dirichlet distribution with concentration vector $(\alpha_{(i,1)},\ldots,\alpha_{(i,L)})$ is precisely $x_i$, since $\frac{\alpha_{(i,l)}-1}{\alpha_0 - L} = x_{(i,l)}$ for all $l$.
        The influence kernel is
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_kernel}
            f_i(x_i, b;\sigma) = \frac{\Gamma(L+\frac{1}{\sigma})}{\prod_{l=1}^L \Gamma(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)})}
            \prod_{l=1}^L b_l^{\,\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)}}, \qquad b \in \Delta^{L-1}
        \end{equation}
        with log-kernel
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_log_kernel}
            \ln f_i(x_i, b;\sigma)
            = \ln\Gamma(L+\frac{1}{\sigma})
            - \sum_{l=1}^L \ln\Gamma(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)})
            + \frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{l=1}^L x_{(i,l)}\ln b_l
        \end{equation}
        The kernel satisfies $f_i(x_i,b;\sigma) > 0$ for all $x_i, b \in \Delta^{L-1}$ and $\sigma > 0$, consistent with the positivity requirement of the general framework.
    \end{definition}

    \begin{lemma}[Log-Derivatives of the Mode-Parametrized Dirichlet Kernel]
    \label{lem:SI_dirichlet_mode_log_derivs}
        Let $f_i(x_i,b;\sigma)$ be the mode-parametrized Dirichlet influence kernel of \Cref{def:SI_dirichlet_mode_kernel}, with $\alpha_{(i,l)} = 1 + \frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)}$ and $\psi_p(\cdot)$ the $p$-th polygamma function of \Cref{def:polygamma}.
        
        Then:
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item \textbf{First derivative:}
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d}
                d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)
                := \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{(i,l)}} \ln f_i(x_i,b;\sigma)
                = \frac{1}{\sigma}\!\left(\ln b_l - \psi_0(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)})\right),
                \qquad l = 1,\ldots,L
            \end{equation}
            \item \textbf{Second derivative:}
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d2}
                \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_{(i,l)}\,\partial x_{(i,k)}} \ln f_i(x_i,b;\sigma)
                = \begin{cases}
                    -\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\,\psi_1(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)}) & l = k \\
                    0 & l \neq k
                \end{cases}, \qquad l,k = 1,\ldots,L
            \end{equation}
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{lemma}

    \begin{proof}
        Differentiating \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_log_kernel} with respect to $x_{(i,l)}$ and using $\frac{d}{d\alpha}\ln\Gamma(\alpha) = \psi_0(\alpha)$ together with $\frac{\partial \alpha_{(i,l)}}{\partial x_{(i,l)}} = \frac{1}{\sigma} $ gives \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d}.
        Differentiating again and using $\frac{d}{d\alpha}\psi_0(\alpha) = \psi_1(\alpha)$ gives the diagonal entry of \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d2}.
        The off-diagonal entries vanish because $\alpha_{(i,l)}$ depends only on $x_{(i,l)}$ and not on $x_{(i,k)}$ for $k \neq l$.
    \end{proof}

    Since $\psi_1(a) > 0$ for all $a > 0$, \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d2} shows that the Hessian $\nabla_{x_i}^2 \ln f_i(x_i,b;\sigma)$ is diagonal with strictly negative entries $-\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\psi_1(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)}) < 0$ for all $l$.
    Hence $\ln f_i(\cdot,b;\sigma)$ is strictly concave in $x_i$ on $\Delta^{L-1}$ for every $b \in \Delta^{L-1}$ and $\sigma > 0$, and \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} guarantees the existence and uniqueness of the symmetric Nash equilibrium.

    \subsubsection{Symmetric Nash Equilibrium}\label{subsubsec:SI_dirichlet_mode_nash}

        \begin{corollary}[Symmetric Nash Equilibrium: Mode-Parametrized Dirichlet]\label{cor:SI_dirichlet_mode_nash}
            Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with $N \geq 2$ players, mode-parametrized Dirichlet influence kernels as in \Cref{def:SI_dirichlet_mode_kernel} with common concentration parameter $\sigma > 0$, strategy space $\mathbb{D} = \Delta^{L-1}$, resource space $\mathbb{B} = \Delta^{L-1}$, and resource distribution $B \in L^1(\mathbb{B},\mu)$ with $B(b) \geq 0$ and $\sum_{\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d\mu(b) > 0$.
            Then $T$ has a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^* = (x^*,\ldots,x^*)$ where $x^* = (x_1^*,\ldots,x_L^*) \in \Delta^{L-1}$ is the unique solution to the system
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_nash}
                \psi_0\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_l^*\right)
                = \mathbb{E}_B[\ln b_l]
                := \frac{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\ln b_l}{\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)},
                \qquad l = 1,\ldots,L
            \end{equation}
            provided $x^* \in \Delta^{L-1}$.
        \end{corollary}

        \begin{proof}
            Applying \cref{lem:SI_requirements} with $d_{(i,l)}(x_i,b)$ from \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d} and substituting $G_i(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{1}{N}$, $G_i^c(\mathbf{x}^*,b) = \frac{N-1}{N}$ at the symmetric profile, the first-order condition in dimension $l$ becomes
            \begin{equation}
                \frac{N-1}{N^2}\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)
                \left(\ln b_l - \psi_0\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_l^*\right)\right) = 0
            \end{equation}
            Since $\frac{N-1}{N^2}\frac{1}{\sigma} > 0$ for all $N \geq 2$ and $\sigma > 0$, and $\psi_0\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_l^*\right)$ does not depend on $b$, this reduces to \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_nash}. Uniqueness follows from \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} since $\ln f_i(\cdot,b;\sigma)$ is strictly concave.
        \end{proof}

        \begin{remark}[Dependence of the Symmetric Nash on $\sigma$]\label{rem:SI_dirichlet_mode_sigma_dependence}
            A key distinction between the mode-parametrized Dirichlet kernel and the multivariate Gaussian kernel of \Cref{subsec:SI_mvg} is that the symmetric Nash position $x^*$ defined by \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_nash} depends on $\sigma$. In contrast, the symmetric Nash of the multivariate Gaussian game with zero covariance is always $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$, independently of $\sigma$. This $\sigma$-dependence of the Dirichlet mode Nash means that as $\sigma$ decreases the equilibrium position itself shifts, coupling the bifurcation analysis with the Nash location in a way that does not occur in the Gaussian case.
        \end{remark}

    \subsubsection{Stability of the Symmetric Nash Equilibrium}\label{subsubsec:SI_dirichlet_mode_stability}

        From \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_d2}, the Hessian of $\ln f_i$ with respect to $x_i$ is diagonal with entries $-\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\psi_1(1+\frac{1}{\sigma} x_{(i,l)})$. Substituting into the Jacobian blocks of \Cref{thm:SI_Jacobian}, the diagonal block $A$ and off-diagonal block $\Delta$ at the symmetric Nash $\mathbf{x}^*$ have entries
        \begin{align}
            a_{lk}\big|_{\mathbf{x}^*}
            &= \frac{N-1}{N^2}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)
            \left[-\sigma^2\psi_1(\alpha_l^*)\delta_{lk}
            + \frac{N-2}{N}\,d_{(i,l)}^*(b)\,d_{(i,k)}^*(b)\right]
            \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_A} \\
            \delta_{lk}\big|_{\mathbf{x}^*}
            &= -\frac{N-2}{N^3}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,d_{(i,l)}^*(b)\,d_{(i,k)}^*(b)
            \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_Delta}
        \end{align}
        where $\alpha_l^* = 1 + \frac{1}{\sigma} x_l^*$, $d_{(i,l)}^*(b) = \frac{1}{\sigma}(\ln b_l - \psi_0(\alpha_l^*))$, and $\delta_{lk}$ is the Kronecker delta. For $L > 1$, the stability condition requires all eigenvalues of both $A^+ = A + \Delta$ and $A^- = A - \Delta$ to be negative, as established in \Cref{thm:SI_Soth}. Since the blocks \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_A,eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_Delta} do not simplify to closed-form eigenvalues for general $L$ and $B$, we rely on numerical evaluation of the stability conditions via \Cref{thm:SI_two_dimensional_stability} for $L = 2$, or direct computation of $\mathrm{spec}(A^\pm)$ for $L > 2$. The $\sigma$-dependence of $x^*$ noted in \Cref{rem:SI_dirichlet_mode_sigma_dependence} further complicates the analysis, as both the Nash position and the Jacobian blocks vary with $\sigma$.We therefore examine the stability properties numerically in the following subsection.

    \subsubsection{Bifurcation Behavior of the Mode-Parametrized Dirichlet Influencer Game}\label{subsubsec:SI_dirichlet_mode_bifurcation}

        We examine the bifurcation behavior of the mode-parametrized Dirichlet influencer game numerically, as the analytical stability conditions of \Cref{subsubsec:SI_dirichlet_mode_stability} do not reduce to closed-form thresholds for general $L$ and $B$. The results are presented in \Cref{fig:simplex_dirl_bifurcation,fig:dirichlet_simplex} for $N \in \{3,\ldots,8\}$ players on the 2-simplex $\Delta^2$.

        Two qualitative features distinguish the Dirichlet mode bifurcation structure from the multivariate Gaussian case of \Cref{subsubsec:SI_mvg_bifurcation}. First, as established in \Cref{rem:SI_dirichlet_mode_sigma_dependence}, the symmetric Nash equilibrium $x^*(\sigma)$ defined by \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_nash} is not fixed but varies with $\sigma$: as $\sigma$ decreases, the equilibrium position shifts continuously on $\Delta^2$, so that the bifurcation diagram reflects both the movement of the Nash position and the loss of its stability. Second, as a consequence of the simplex geometry, player trajectories are constrained to $\Delta^2$ and tend to concentrate along the edges and interior of the simplex near the resource modes, rather than spreading freely as in $\mathbb{R}^2$. Despite these differences, the qualitative local-game decomposition behavior identified in \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate} persists: as $\sigma$ decreases below the stability threshold, players partition into local strategic clusters each converging to a local symmetric Nash equilibrium of the corresponding sub-distribution on $\Delta^2$, consistent with the 1-dimensional and multivariate Gaussian cases.

        The resource distribution used throughout this subsection is a mixture of three isotropic Gaussians embedded in the simplex via Cartesian coordinates:
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_resource}
            B(b) = \sum_{l=1}^{3}\exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2}(b_l - \mu_l)^\top\Sigma_r^{-1}(b_l - \mu_l)\right),
            \qquad b \in \Delta^2
        \end{equation}
        where $\mu_1 = (0.35, 0.15)^\top$, $\mu_2 = (0.65, 0.15)^\top$, $\mu_3 = (0.50, 0.45)^\top$, and $\Sigma_r = 0.005\,I_2$.
        This distribution places three resource modes near the corners of the simplex in a triangular arrangement, as illustrated in \Cref{fig:simplex_dirl_bifurcation:a}. The tight concentration $\Sigma_r = 0.005\,I_2$ ensures that the three modes are well separated, making the local sub-game structure visually distinguishable in the bifurcation diagrams.

        \begin{figure}[ht!]
            \centering
            \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.6\linewidth}
                \centering
                \includesvg[width=\textwidth]{figures/SI_plots/5_2/fig1/simplex_alpha_1.svg}
                \caption{Resource distribution $B(b)$ defined by \cref{eq:SI_dirichlet_mode_resource}, shown on the 2-simplex $\Delta^2$ in barycentric coordinates.

    \section{Applications}\label{sec:SI_applications}

        \subsection{Applications to Social Media and Social Networks}\label{subsec:SI_social_media}

            As the name suggests, influencer's games are particularly well-suited for studying competitive dynamics on social media platforms.
            Competition in these settings typically takes the form of content creators or job seekers competing for engagement metrics --- views, likes, subscriptions, shares, or job placements --- that are allocated according to the relative relevance of each participant's profile to the available opportunities.
            influencer's games provide a natural framework for this competition: the strategy space $\mathbb{D}$ represents the space of possible content or profile positions, the resource space $\mathbb{B}$ represents the distribution of available opportunities, and the influence kernel $f_i(x_i, b)$ quantifies how well a participant at position $x_i$ is matched to an opportunity at $b$.
            In the following subsection we demonstrate these ideas concretely using a real-world dataset of LinkedIn job postings.

        \subsubsection{LinkedIn: A Game of Professional Positioning}\label{subsubsec:SI_linkedin}

            LinkedIn has become a primary platform for professional networking, job seeking, and talent recruitment.
            Job seekers tailor their profiles to maximize their visibility to relevant postings, while employers post opportunities targeting specific skill profiles.
            This competitive dynamic --- many job seekers positioning themselves in a shared professional trait space to capture a limited supply of job opportunities --- maps directly onto the influencer game framework.

            We utilize a publicly available dataset of LinkedIn job postings \cite{arsh_koneru_2024}, each consisting of a job title, company name, and description. Using the \texttt{GPT-OSS-20B} language model deployed on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU, we classify each posting along two continuous dimensions: a tech score $b_1^{(j)} \in [0,1]$ and a finance score $b_2^{(j)} \in [0,1]$, where higher values indicate greater relevance to the respective sector. The classification prompt is given in \Cref{lst:linkedin_prompt}, with generation parameters $\{\texttt{max\_tokens}: 800,\, \texttt{temperature}: 0.1\}$ to minimize stochasticity and focus the model on the classification task. To focus on postings with meaningful sectoral relevance, we retain only postings satisfying $\max(b_1^{(j)}, b_2^{(j)}) \geq 0.1$, discarding postings that are neither tech- nor finance-adjacent. The resulting empirical resource distribution $B(b) = B(b_1, b_2)$ is displayed in \Cref{fig:linkedin_resources}.

\begin{lstlisting}[breaklines=true,
    caption={LinkedIn job classification prompt used with \texttt{GPT-OSS-20B}.
        Generation parameters: \texttt{max\_tokens}=800,
        \texttt{temperature}=0.1.},
    label={lst:linkedin_prompt}]

        Analyze this job posting and determine if it's a technology/research role AND if it's a finance role.
        
        Job Title: Marketing Coordinator
        Company: Assistant Restaurant Manager
        Description: The National Exemplar is accepting applications for an Assistant Restaurant Manager. We offer highly competitive wages, healthcare, paid time off, complimentary dining privileges and bonus opportunities. We are a serious, professional, long-standing neighborhood restaurant with over 41 years of service. If you are looking for a long-term fit with a best in class organization then you should apply now. Please send a resumes to pardom@nationalexemplar.com.
        
        Rate this job on TWO scales from 0.0 to 1.0:
        
        TECH SCORE:
        - 0.0 = Completely non-tech (retail, food service, manual labor, etc.)
        - 0.3 = Some tech interaction but not core tech role (sales at tech company, admin with some software use)
        - 0.7 = Moderate tech role (IT support, basic development, data entry/analysis)
        - 1.0 = High-tech role (software engineering, AI/ML, research scientist, data scientist)
        
        FINANCE SCORE:
        - 0.0 = Completely non-finance (retail, tech without finance, manual labor, etc.)
        - 0.3 = Some finance interaction (admin at financial company, sales of financial products)
        - 0.7 = Moderate finance role (accounting, financial analysis, banking operations)
        - 1.0 = High-finance role (investment banking, trading, portfolio management, hedge fund analyst)
        
        Respond with ONLY a JSON object in this exact format:
        {{"tech_score": 0.8, "finance_score": 0.2, "tech_category": "software_engineering", "finance_category": "non_finance", "reasoning": "brief explanation"}}
        """
    
\end{lstlisting}

        \begin{figure}[ht!]
            \centering
            \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.45\linewidth}
                \centering
                \includesvg[inkscapelatex=false,width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/6_1/fig1/resources_linkedin.svg}
                \caption{Empirical resource distribution $B(b_1, b_2)$ as a 3D surface, where $b_1$ is the tech score and $b_2$ is the finance score.
                    Color intensity indicates the density of job postings at each score combination, with darker regions indicating higher concentrations.
                    The distribution is dominated by a cluster of tech-focused postings near $(b_1, b_2) \approx (1.0, 0.0)$ and a secondary cluster of finance-adjacent postings.}
                \label{fig:linkedin_resources:a}
                \vspace{4ex}
            \end{subfigure}
            \hspace{4ex}
            \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.45\linewidth}
                \centering
                \includesvg[inkscapelatex=false,width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/6_1/fig1/Linkedin_Jobs_2d.svg}
                \caption{Heatmap of individual job postings in the $(b_1, b_2)$ score space, with color indicating posting density.
                    After filtering at $\max(b_1,b_2) \geq 0.1$, the retained postings concentrate near the tech-focused and finance-focused extremes of the score space.}
                \label{fig:linkedin_resources:b}
                \vspace{4ex}
            \end{subfigure}
            \caption{Resource distribution $B(b_1,b_2)$ for LinkedIn job postings, classified by \texttt{GPT-OSS-20B} into tech and finance relevance scores as described in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_linkedin}.
                The $x$-axis represents the tech score $b_1 \in [0,1]$ and the $y$-axis the finance score $b_2 \in [0,1]$.
                Postings with $\max(b_1,b_2) < 0.1$ are excluded.}
            \label{fig:linkedin_resources}
        \end{figure}


        We model job seekers as players in a symmetric 2-dimensional influencer game with strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset [0,1]^2$ and resource space $\mathbb{B} \subset [0,1]^2$, where $B(b)$ is the empirical job posting distribution of \Cref{fig:linkedin_resources}.Player $i$'s strategy $x_i = (x_{(i,1)}, x_{(i,2)}) \in \mathbb{D}$ represents their self-positioning in the tech--finance trait space, and their influence over a job posting at $b = (b_1, b_2)$ is modeled by a multivariate Gaussian kernel as in \Cref{def:SI_mvg_kernel}:
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_linkedin_kernel}
            f_i(x_i, b) = \exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2}(b - x_i)^\top \Sigma^{-1}(b - x_i)\right)
        \end{equation}
        For tractability and to focus on the effect of reach rather than covariance structure, we adopt the isotropic covariance matrix
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_linkedin_sigma}
            \Sigma = \sigma^2 I_2
            = \begin{pmatrix}
                \sigma^2 & 0 \\
                0 & \sigma^2
            \end{pmatrix}
        \end{equation}
        Under the equal-opportunity assumption that each job seeker has an equal a priori probability of being selected for any posting they influence, the probability that player $i$ captures the posting at $b$ is precisely the relative influence $G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)$ of \cref{eq:SI_relative_influence}, and the utility function takes the standard form of \cref{eq:SI_utility}:
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_linkedin_utility}
            u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\, G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)
        \end{equation}
        By \Cref{cor:SI_mvg_nash}, the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium of this game is $x^* = \mathbb{E}_B[b]$, the $B$-weighted centroid of the job posting distribution, and its stability is governed by \Cref{cor:SI_mvg_stability} with threshold $\sigma^2 > \frac{N-2}{N-1}\Lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_B)$.


        \Cref{fig:linkedin_trajectories} displays gradient ascent trajectories for $N \in \{3, 5, 11, 1000\}$ players at selected values of $\sigma$, with randomly initialized starting positions.
        Several features of the general theory are confirmed empirically:
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item \textbf{Cyclic behavior.}
            For small $N$ and $\sigma$ below the stability threshold $\sigma_0^* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\Lambda_{\max}^{1/2}(\Sigma_B)$, cyclic trajectories are observed (\Cref{fig:linkedin_trajectories:a,fig:linkedin_trajectories:b}), consistent with the cyclic regimes identified in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics,fig:2d_cycles}.
            \item \textbf{Local-game decomposition.}
            For larger $N$, players partition into local strategic clusters near resource-dense regions of $B(b)$, consistent with the sub-game decomposition of \Cref{alg:SI_t_estimate}.
            This is most clearly visible in the $N = 1000$ case (\Cref{fig:linkedin_trajectories:d}), where clusters form near the dominant tech-focused and finance-focused modes of $B$.
            \item \textbf{Natural sorting.}
            As $\sigma$ decreases, the $N=1000$ population sorts into progressively more concentrated clusters at resource peaks, as shown in \Cref{fig:linkedin_density}.
            The size of each cluster is approximately proportional to the local resource density, consistent with the Voronoi-partition interpretation of the $\sigma \to 0$ limit established in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_exp_stability}.
        \end{enumerate}

    \subsection{Applications to Political Science}\label{subsec:SI_political_science}


        influencer's games provide a natural framework for studying electoral competition, in which politicians compete for votes distributed across an ideological spectrum.
        Such models have a long history in political science and sociology \cite{jones2022polarization}, but have typically been restricted to two active players or systems with exogenously fixed additional players.
        Jones et al.\ \cite{jones2022polarization} study a 1-dimensional ideological space in which two active candidates compete for votes subject to asymmetric resource distributions, fixed third-party candidates, and abstaining voters, and conclude that all three factors can drive candidates away from the symmetric Nash equilibrium at the ideological mean.
        As we established in \Cref{cor:SI_jones_stability}, this instability is a direct consequence of the convexity of $\ln f_i$ for $p < 0$ in the Jones et al.\ influence kernel \cref{eq:SI_Jones_influence_kernel}: by \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}, no symmetric Nash equilibrium of a game with a globally log-convex influence kernel can be locally asymptotically stable, regardless of the number of players or the resource distribution.
        Moreover, the Jones et al.\ kernel $f_i(x_i,b) = |x_i - b|^p$ is non-differentiable at $x_i = b$, which complicates direct application of gradient-based adaptive dynamics.

        We now demonstrate that the qualitative conclusions of Jones et al.\ --- specifically, the destabilizing effects of fixed third parties and abstaining voters --- can be reproduced within our framework using the differentiable, log-concave Gaussian influence kernel of \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian}.
        This has two advantages: it places the political model within our general stability theory, and it admits gradient-based analysis of the equilibrium structure.

    \subsubsection{Voting with Fixed Third Parties and Abstaining Voters: Gaussian Influence}\label{subsubsec:SI_voting_gaussian}

        We model the ideological space as $\mathbb{D} = \mathbb{B} = [0,1]$, with voter density $B(v)$ representing the distribution of voter ideological positions $v \in [0,1]$.
        Two active candidates (players) compete for votes using Gaussian influence kernels \cref{eq:SI_Gaussian_infl} with common reach $\sigma > 0$.
        In addition to the two active candidates, the model includes two types of passive participants whose influence enters the denominator of the relative influence \cref{eq:SI_relative_influence} but whose positions are fixed exogenously.

        Fixed third-party candidates are placed at the ideological extremes $v = 0$ and $v = 1$, with combined influence kernel
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_third_party_kernel}
            f_{\mathrm{tp}}(v)
            = \exp\!\left(-\frac{v^2}{2R^2}\right)
            + \exp\!\left(-\frac{(1-v)^2}{2R^2}\right)
        \end{equation}
        where $R > 0$ controls the reach of the third-party influence.
        A smaller $R$ concentrates third-party influence near the extremes, drawing voters away from the center and reducing the effective resource available to the active candidates near the mean.


        Abstaining voters are modeled as casting their votes for a notional ``abstention candidate'' whose influence at ideological position $v$ increases with the distance of $v$ from all active candidates.
        Formally, the abstention influence kernel is
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:pp_abstain_1d}
            f_{\mathrm{abs}}(v;\mathbf{x}) = Q \prod_{j \in J}(v - x_j)^2
        \end{equation}
        where $J$ is the index set of active candidates, $x_j \in \mathbb{D}$ is the position of active candidate $j$, and $Q > 0$ controls the overall weight of abstention.
        The product form ensures that $f_{\mathrm{abs}}$ is large when $v$ is far from all active candidates, capturing the intuition that voters far from all candidates are more likely to abstain.
        Note that $f_{\mathrm{abs}}$ depends on the active candidates' positions $\mathbf{x}$, introducing a non-standard coupling into the relative influence that breaks the symmetry of the standard influencer game framework when $Q > 0$.


        With both fixed third parties and abstaining voters included, the relative influence of active candidate $i$ at voter position $v$ is
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_voting_relative_influence}
            G_i(\mathbf{x}, v)
            = \frac{f_i(x_i, v)}{\sum_{j \in J} f_j(x_j, v) + f_{\mathrm{tp}}(v) + f_{\mathrm{abs}}(v;\mathbf{x})}
        \end{equation}
        and the utility of active candidate $i$ is $u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \int_\mathbb{B} B(v)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},v)\,dv$ as in \cref{eq:SI_utility}.
        The effects of varying $R$ and $Q$ on the resulting gradient dynamics are demonstrated in \Cref{fig:voting_dynamics}.

        \begin{figure}[htbp]
            \centering
            \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}
                \centering
                \includesvg[width=\linewidth]{figures/SI_plots/6_2/fig1/1d_vector_2_agents_3party.svg}
                \caption{Fixed third parties only ($R=0.1$, $Q=0$).
                    Third parties at $v=0$ and $v=1$ draw players away from the mean, producing two stable equilibria symmetric about $v=0.5$.}
                \label{fig:voting_dynamics:b}

            \end{subfigure}
            \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\linewidth}

                    The abstention kernel \cref{eq:pp_abstain_1d} destabilizes the mean equilibrium and produces divergent dynamics, consistent with \Cref{cor:SI_jones_stability}.}

        \Cref{fig:voting_dynamics} demonstrates that fixed third parties at the ideological extremes shift the stable equilibrium away from the mean $v = 0.5$, producing two symmetric off-center stable positions.
        Abstaining voters, modeled via \cref{eq:pp_abstain_1d}, introduce a dynamic asymmetry that further destabilizes the mean equilibrium.
        When both effects are present, the equilibrium structure reflects a combination of the two: the the destabilization effect of the abstention kernel is partially counteracted by off-center displacement induced by third parties, producing an intermediate bifurcation structure.
        These results replicate the qualitative conclusions of Jones et al.\ \cite{jones2022polarization} using a differentiable, log-concave influence kernel, confirming that the destabilizing effects they identified are not artifacts of the Jones kernel's log-convexity, but arise from the structural asymmetry introduced by fixed third parties and dynamic abstention.
        In terms of our general framework, the fixed third parties reduce the effective resource density available to active candidates near the mean, while the abstention candidate effectively introduces a third active player with a position-dependent strategy, breaking the symmetry assumed in \Cref{def:SI_symmetric_influencers}.
