        A notable finding from \Cref{fig:voting_bifurcation} is that while abstaining voters alone produce a wider range of instability than fixed third parties alone, the combination of both effects yields a stability region intermediate between the two individual cases.
        This suggests that fixed third parties of relatively small reach $R$ can partially stabilize a political system that would otherwise be strongly destabilized by abstaining voters, by counteracting the asymmetric resource depletion induced by the abstention kernel \cref{eq:pp_abstain_1d}.

    \subsubsection{Relationship to the Median Voter Theorem}\label{subsubsec:SI_median_voter}

        The results of the preceding sections align with the mean voter theorem for symmetric influencer's games with Gaussian influence: by \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}, the symmetric Nash equilibrium is always at the $B$-weighted mean of the voter distribution.
        A complementary result holds in the opposite limit $\sigma \to 0$: the influencer game recovers the median voter theorem of classical spatial voting theory.

        To formalize this, let $\chi = (\chi_1 < \chi_2 < \cdots < \chi_K)$ be the sorted tuple of distinct player positions in $\mathbf{x}$, and let $m_k$ denote the number of players at position $\chi_k$.
        Define the Voronoi boundaries
        \begin{equation}
            \chi_k^- =
            \begin{cases}
                \inf\mathbb{D} & k = 1 \\
                \dfrac{\chi_{k-1} + \chi_k}{2} & k \geq 2
            \end{cases},
            \qquad
            \chi_k^+ =
            \begin{cases}
                \sup\mathbb{D} & k = K \\
                \dfrac{\chi_k + \chi_{k+1}}{2} & k \leq K-1
            \end{cases}
        \end{equation}
        \begin{proposition}[Recovery of the Median Voter Theorem]
        \label{prop:SI_median_voter}
            Let $T$ be a symmetric influencer game with Gaussian influence kernels \cref{eq:SI_Gaussian_infl} and strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}$.
            For any strategy profile $\mathbf{x}$ with sorted distinct positions $\chi_1 < \cdots < \chi_K$ and multiplicities $m_1,\ldots,m_K$, the relative influence satisfies
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_median_voter_limit}
                \lim_{\sigma \to 0} G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)
                = \frac{1}{m_k}\,\mathbf{1}_{[\chi_k^-, \chi_k^+)}(b),
                \qquad \text{for player } i \text{ at position } \chi_k
            \end{equation}
            where $\mathbf{1}_{[\chi_k^-, \chi_k^+)}(b)$ is the indicator function of the Voronoi cell $[\chi_k^-, \chi_k^+)$ associated with position $\chi_k$.
            Consequently, the utility of player $i$ at position $\chi_k$ satisfies
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_median_voter_utility}
                \lim_{\sigma \to 0} u_i(\mathbf{x})
                = \frac{1}{m_k}\int_{\chi_k^-}^{\chi_k^+} B(v)\,dv
            \end{equation}
            That is, in the limit $\sigma \to 0$, each player captures an equal share of the resources in their Voronoi cell, and the optimal strategy for each player is to position themselves at the median of the resource distribution within their cell.
            For $N = 2$ players with a unimodal symmetric $B$, this reduces to the classical median voter theorem: both players converge to the median of $B$.
        \end{proposition}

        \begin{proof}
            For fixed $b \neq \chi_k$ for all $k$, the Gaussian kernel ratio satisfies
            \begin{equation}
                \frac{f_i(x_i, b)}{f_j(x_j, b)}
                = \exp\!\left(-\frac{(b-\chi_k)^2 - (b-\chi_l)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)
                \xrightarrow{\sigma \to 0}
                \begin{cases}
                    \infty & |b - \chi_k| < |b - \chi_l| \\
                    1 & |b-\chi_k| = |b-\chi_l| \\
                    0 & |b-\chi_k| > |b-\chi_l|
                \end{cases}
            \end{equation}
            for players $i,j$ at positions $\chi_k, \chi_l$ respectively.
            Therefore, for $b \in (\chi_k^-, \chi_k^+)$, player $i$'s kernel dominates all other kernels in the limit, giving $G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \to \frac{1}{m_k}$ (shared equally among the $m_k$ players at $\chi_k$).
            For $b \notin [\chi_k^-, \chi_k^+]$, there exists a player $j$ with $|b - \chi_l| < |b - \chi_k|$, so $G_i(\mathbf{x},b) \to 0$.
            Since the boundary points $\{\chi_k^\pm\}$ form a set of measure zero with respect to $\mu$, \cref{eq:SI_median_voter_limit} holds $\mu$-almost everywhere, and \cref{eq:SI_median_voter_utility} follows by dominated convergence.
        \end{proof}

        \Cref{prop:SI_median_voter} establishes that the influencer game framework subsumes both the mean voter theorem (at large $\sigma$, via \Cref{cor:SI_stability_Gaussian}) and the median voter theorem (at $\sigma \to 0$, via \Cref{prop:SI_median_voter}) as limiting cases of a single continuous model.
        The parameter $\sigma$ thus interpolates between these two classical results, providing a richer characterization of electoral equilibria than either theorem alone.
        Moreover, \Cref{prop:SI_median_voter} extends the median voter theorem to games with $N > 2$ active candidates: in the limit $\sigma \to 0$, each candidate optimally positions at the median of the voter distribution within their Voronoi cell, generalizing the classical two-candidate result to a multi-candidate Voronoi partition of the ideological space.


        \subsection{Applications to Ecology}\label{subsec:SI_ecology}

            influencer's games have natural connections to ecological competition models, with potential applications to dispersal, home range formation, migration, and reproductive dynamics, among others.
            In each case, the influencer game framework provides a tractable model of competition for spatially distributed resources, with the influence kernel $f_i(x_i,b)$ capturing the species-specific mechanism by which a competitor at position $x_i$ exerts influence over a resource at $b$.
            The results of \Cref{sec:SI_multidimensional} are particularly relevant here, as ecological resource distributions are inherently spatial and often multi-dimensional.

        \subsubsection{Dispersal and Migration with Constant Movement Costs}\label{subsubsec:SI_ecology_dispersal}

            Consider $N$ species competing for a resource distributed across a spatial domain $\mathbb{B}$ according to density $B(b) \geq 0$, $b \in \mathbb{B}$.
            Each species $i \in I$ selects a spatial position $x_i \in \mathbb{D}$ to maximize its expected resource intake, modeled by the influencer game utility function
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_ecology_utility}
                u_i(\mathbf{x}) = \int_{\mathbb{B}} B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x},b)\,d\mu(b)
            \end{equation}
            where $G_i(\mathbf{x},b) = \frac{f_i(x_i,b)}{\sum_{j=1}^N f_j(x_j,b)}$ is the relative influence of species $i$ at resource point $b$, as in \cref{eq:SI_relative_influence}.
            This framework encompasses a range of ecological influence mechanisms: for instance, a Gaussian kernel $f_i(x_i,b) = \exp(-\|b - x_i\|^2 / 2\sigma^2)$ models a species whose probability of exploiting resource $b$ decays with distance from its home range center $x_i$, as in the home range models of \cite{borger2008effects}.

            In ecological settings, movement is rarely costless: dispersal or migration incurs energetic costs, predation risk, or other fitness penalties proportional to movement rate.
            We model the simplest such case by a linear movement cost, so that the instantaneous fitness rate of species $i$ is
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_ecology_fitness}
                R_i(\mathbf{x}, \dot{x}_i) = u_i(\mathbf{x}) - c\,|\dot{x}_i|
            \end{equation}
            where $c > 0$ is the cost per unit of movement speed and $\dot{x}_i = \frac{dx_i}{dt}$ is the movement velocity.
            The equilibrium condition $\frac{d}{dt}R_i = 0$ then gives, for $\dot{x}_i \neq 0$,
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_ecology_lienard}
                \nabla_{x_i} u_i(\mathbf{x})\,\dot{x}_i - c\,\operatorname{sgn}(\dot{x}_i)\,\ddot{x}_i = 0
            \end{equation}
            which is a Li\'{e}nard-type ordinary differential equation in $x_i(t)$.
            The stationary solutions of \cref{eq:SI_ecology_lienard} with $\ddot{x}_i = 0$ satisfy $\nabla_{x_i} u_i(\mathbf{x})\,\dot{x}_i = 0$, which holds either at a Nash equilibrium ($\nabla_{x_i} u_i = 0$, as characterized by \Cref{lem:SI_requirements}) or when $\dot{x}_i = 0$ (the species is stationary).
            For $\dot{x}_i(0) \neq 0$, dividing \cref{eq:SI_ecology_lienard} by $c\,\operatorname{sgn}(\dot{x}_i)\,\dot{x}_i = c\,|\dot{x}_i|$ and integrating gives the general solution
            \begin{equation}
                \label{eq:SI_ecology_velocity}
                \dot{x}_i(t) = \dot{x}_i(0)\exp\!\left(\frac{1}{c}\int_0^t \nabla_{x_i} u_i(\mathbf{x}(\tau))\,d\tau\right)
            \end{equation}
            This has two important implications.
            First, a species that begins at rest at the symmetric Nash equilibrium $\mathbf{x}^*$ of \Cref{lem:SI_requirements} remains stationary: since $\nabla_{x_i} u_i(\mathbf{x}^*) = 0$, the integral in \cref{eq:SI_ecology_velocity} vanishes and $\dot{x}_i(t) = \dot{x}_i(0) = 0$ for all $t$.
            Second, away from the Nash equilibrium, $c$ appears only in the denominator of the exponent in \cref{eq:SI_ecology_velocity}: a larger movement cost $c$ attenuates the velocity response to the utility gradient, slowing convergence toward $\mathbf{x}^*$ without altering its location or stability.
            This result has a natural ecological interpretation: species with higher dispersal costs converge more slowly to their optimal home range positions but ultimately reach the same competitive equilibrium as species with lower dispersal costs, provided the Nash equilibrium is stable in the sense of \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability}.

    \section{Relationship to Colonel Blotto Games}\label{sec:SI_blotto}
        
        influencer's games are structurally related to Colonel Blotto games, in which players allocate a fixed resource budget across multiple battlefields, with the player committing more resources to each battlefield winning that battlefield's reward. In the influencer game framework, players instead commit continuous influence via the kernel $f_i(x_i,b)$ to resource points $b \in \mathbb{B}$, and resource allocation is proportional rather than winner-takes-all.
        We now make this connection precise by constructing an influence kernel that recovers the Blotto payoff structure.

        Let $|\mathbb{B}| = M$ denote the number of battlefields (resource points), and let $x_i = (x_{i,1}, x_{i,2}, \ldots, x_{i,M}) \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^M$ be the vector of resources that player $i$ commits to each battlefield.
        Define the Blotto influence kernel with scaling parameter $\sigma > 0$ by
        \begin{equation}
            \label{eq:SI_blotto_influence_kernel}
            f_i(x_i, b, \sigma) = x_{i,b}^{\,\sigma}, \qquad b \in \mathbb{B}
        \end{equation}
        so that player $i$ exerts influence $x_{i,b}^\sigma$ at battlefield $b$.
        Note that this is equivalent to the indicator-sum form $\sum_{l \in \mathbb{B}} x_{i,l}^\sigma \,\mathbbm{1}_l(b)$, where
        \begin{equation}
            \mathbbm{1}_l(b)
            = \begin{cases}
                1 & \text{if } l = b \\
                0 & \text{otherwise}
            \end{cases}
        \end{equation}
        but since the indicator function evaluates the sum at the single term $l = b$, we write it directly as \cref{eq:SI_blotto_influence_kernel}.

        The standard Blotto budget constraint --- that each player allocates exactly $\chi$ total resources across all battlefields --- is recovered at $\sigma = 1$ via
        \begin{equation}
            \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} f_i(x_i, b, 1)
            = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} x_{i,b}
            = \chi \qquad \forall\, i \in I
        \end{equation}
        where $\chi > 0$ is the common resource budget.
        Under this constraint, the strategy space of each player is the scaled simplex
        \[
            \Delta_\chi^{M-1}
            = \{x \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^M : \sum_{b} x_b = \chi\},
        \]
        which is compact and convex, consistent with the general framework of \Cref{subsec:SI_model}.

        The log-concavity of $f_i(x_i, b, \sigma)$ in $x_{i,b}$ is determined by the sign of the second log-derivative.
        Since the kernel \cref{eq:SI_blotto_influence_kernel} depends on $x_i$ only through the single coordinate $x_{i,b}$, we compute
        \begin{align}
            \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i,b}} \ln f_i(x_i, b, \sigma)
            &= \frac{\sigma}{x_{i,b}},
            \label{eq:SI_blotto_log_deriv_1} \\
            \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_{i,b}^2} \ln f_i(x_i, b, \sigma)
            &= -\frac{\sigma}{x_{i,b}^2}.
            \label{eq:SI_blotto_log_deriv_2}
        \end{align}
        Since $\sigma > 0$ and $x_{i,b} > 0$, the second log-derivative \cref{eq:SI_blotto_log_deriv_2} is strictly negative for all $\sigma > 0$, so $\ln f_i(\cdot, b, \sigma)$ is strictly concave in $x_{i,b}$ for every $\sigma > 0$.
        The Blotto influence kernel is therefore log-concave in each coordinate $x_{i,b}$ regardless of $\sigma$, and \Cref{thm:SI_uniqueness} guarantees the existence and uniqueness of the symmetric Nash equilibrium.

        Since the Blotto kernel \cref{eq:SI_blotto_influence_kernel} depends only on $x_{i,b}$ at each battlefield independently, the partial derivative of $u_i$ with respect to $x_{i,b}$, evaluated at a symmetric profile $x_{j,b} = x_b^*$ for all $j \in I$, is
        \begin{equation}
            \frac{\partial u_i}{\partial x_{i,b}}\bigg|_{\mathrm{sym}}
            = B(b)\cdot\frac{\sigma(N-1)}{N^2\,x_{i,b}}.
        \end{equation}
        This is strictly positive for all $x_{i,b} > 0$, so the unconstrained optimum is unbounded: each player would increase their allocation at every battlefield without limit.
        The relevant Nash equilibrium must therefore be sought on the constrained strategy space $\Delta_\chi^{M-1}$.

        Applying a Lagrange multiplier $\lambda$ enforcing $\sum_b x_{i,b} = \chi$, the Lagrange stationarity conditions \cite{boyd2004convex} at the symmetric Nash equilibrium are
        \begin{equation}
            B(b)\cdot\frac{\sigma(N-1)}{N^2\,x_{i,b}^*} = \lambda
            \qquad \forall\, b \in \mathbb{B},
        \end{equation}
        which gives $x_{i,b}^* \propto B(b)$.
        Imposing the budget constraint $\sum_{b} x_{i,b}^* = \chi$ yields
        \begin{equation}
            x_{i,b}^* = \chi\cdot\frac{B(b)}{\sum_{b'\in\mathbb{B}}B(b')}
            \qquad \forall\, b \in \mathbb{B},\; i \in I.
        \end{equation}
        That is, the unique symmetric Nash equilibrium on the budget simplex is the value-proportional allocation, in which each player distributes their resources across battlefields in proportion to the battlefield values $B(b)$.
        This result is independent of $\sigma > 0$: while $\sigma$ governs the sensitivity of influence to allocation differences, the equilibrium allocation is determined entirely by the relative battlefield values.

        The value-proportional allocation Nash equilibrium of the influencer Blotto game should be contrasted with the classical Blotto game, in which it is well known that no pure strategy Nash equilibrium exists \cite{ roberson2006colonel}.
        The discrepancy arises from the key structural difference between the two models: in the classical Blotto game, resource allocation is winner-takes-all (the player committing more to battlefield $b$ captures the full reward $B(b)$), which introduces a discontinuity that prevents the existence of a pure Nash.
        In the influencer Blotto game, allocation is proportional --- each player captures a share of $B(b)$ equal to their relative influence $G_i(\mathbf{x}, b)$ --- which smooths the payoff landscape and restores the existence of a pure Nash on the simplex.
        In the limit $\sigma \to \infty$, the Blotto influence kernel $x_{i,b}^\sigma$ becomes increasingly sensitive to small differences in allocation, progressively concentrating influence on the most-funded battlefield.
        In this limit, the influencer Blotto game approaches the winner-takes-all structure of the classical Blotto game, and the value-proportional symmetric Nash equilibrium becomes increasingly unstable, consistent with the absence of a pure Nash in the classical limit.
   


    \section{Q-Learning Dynamics}\label{sec:SI_qlearning}

        While the adaptive dynamics framework developed in the preceding sections yields clean analytical results, it rests on the assumption that agents continuously and perfectly compute the gradient of their utility function. In practice, agents learn from noisy, discrete interactions with their environment and update their strategies based on observed rewards rather than analytic gradients. To assess whether the qualitative conclusions of the adaptive dynamics analysis persist under a more realistic learning model, we study influencer's games through the lens of multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) via independent Q-learning \cite{watkins1992q,tan1993multi,matignon2012independent}, in which each agent learns independently from its own experience.

        \subsection{Independent Q-Learning}\label{subsec:SI_iql}

            Independent Q-learning is a decentralized reinforcement learning scheme in which each agent $i \in I$ maintains its own Q-table and updates it based solely on its own observed transitions and rewards, without direct access to the strategies or Q-tables of other agents. We formalize the relevant components below.

            \begin{definition}[Q-Table]
                The Q-table of agent $i \in I$ is a function $Q_i : \mathbb{D} \times \mathcal{A} \to \mathbb{R}$, where $\mathbb{D}$ is the discrete state space and $\mathcal{A}$ is the action space. The entry $Q_i(s,a)$ represents agent $i$'s current estimate of the expected cumulative discounted reward obtained by taking action $a$ in state $s$ and following the optimal policy thereafter.
            \end{definition}

            \begin{definition}[Q-Update (Bellman Update)]
                At each time step $t$, agent $i$ observes its current state $s = x_i(t)$, takes action $a = a_i(t)$, receives reward $r = r_i(t)$, and transitions to state $s' = x_i(t+1)$. The Q-table is updated via the Bellman backup
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_qupdate}
                    Q_i(s,a) \;\leftarrow\; Q_i(s,a) + \alpha\Bigl( r + \gamma\max_{a'}Q_i(s',a') - Q_i(s,a)\Bigr)
                \end{equation}
                where $\alpha \in (0,1]$ is the learning rate and $\gamma \in [0,1)$ is the discount factor.
            \end{definition}

            \begin{definition}[Independent Q-Learning]
                Independent Q-learning (IQL) is the multi-agent extension of single-agent Q-learning \cite{watkins1992q,tan1993multi,matignon2012independent} in which each agent $i \in I$ applies the update rule \cref{eq:SI_qupdate} independently, treating all other agents as part of the environment. Agent $i$ selects actions according to an $\varepsilon$-greedy policy: with probability $\varepsilon$ it selects a uniformly random action, and with probability $1 - \varepsilon$ it selects $a^* = \arg\max_{a} Q_i(x_i(t), a)$.
            \end{definition}

        \subsection{Q-Learning Environment for influencer's games}\label{subsec:SI_qenv}

            The Q-learning environment for a one-dimensional influencer game is specified by the tuple $(\mathbb{D}, \mathcal{A}, \mathcal{R})$, where $\mathbb{D}$ is the discrete state and observation space, $\mathcal{A}$ is the action space, and $\mathcal{R}$ is the reward function.

            We discretize the strategy space $\mathbb{D} \subset \mathbb{R}$ into a uniform grid of $S+1$ positions:
            \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_discrete_D}
                \mathbb{D} = \{d_0, d_1, \ldots, d_S\}, \qquad d_{k+1} - d_k = \delta_{\mathrm{step}} \quad \forall\, k = 0, 1, \ldots, S-1
            \end{equation}
            where $\delta_{\mathrm{step}} > 0$ is the common step size. Each agent $i \in I$ observes only its own current position $x_i(t) \in \mathbb{D}$; the positions of other agents are not directly observed. This defines a partially observable environment.

            \begin{definition}[Observation Space]\label{def:SI_obs_space}
                The observation space of agent $i \in I$ is $\mathbb{D}$, the discrete strategy grid \cref{eq:SI_discrete_D}, with $|\mathbb{D}| = S+1$ states. Agent $i$ observes only its own position $x_i(t) \in \mathbb{D}$ at each time step $t$.
            \end{definition}

            \begin{definition}[action space]\label{def:SI_action_space}
                The action space is common to all agents:
                \begin{equation}
                    \mathcal{A} = \{\texttt{LEFT},\,\texttt{STAY},\,\texttt{RIGHT}\} \equiv \{0, 1, 2\}
                \end{equation}
                with position update rule
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_pos_update}
                    x_i(t+1) = \begin{cases}
                        \max(d_0,\; x_i(t) - \delta_{\mathrm{step}}) & \text{if } a_i(t) = 0 \\
                        x_i(t) & \text{if } a_i(t) = 1 \\
                        \min(d_S,\; x_i(t) + \delta_{\mathrm{step}}) & \text{if } a_i(t) = 2
                    \end{cases}
                \end{equation}
                where the $\min$ and $\max$ enforce the boundary constraint $x_i(t) \in \mathbb{D}$ at all times.
            \end{definition}

            At each time step, agent $i$ receives a reward equal to its current utility under the influencer game, evaluated at the current strategy profile $\mathbf{x}(t)$:

            \begin{definition}[Reward Function]\label{def:SI_reward}
                The instantaneous reward of agent $i \in I$ at time $t$ is
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_reward}
                    r_i(t) = u_i(\mathbf{x}(t)) = \sum_{b \in \mathbb{B}} B(b)\,G_i(\mathbf{x}(t), b)
                \end{equation}
                where $G_i(\mathbf{x}(t), b)$ is the relative influence of \cref{eq:SI_relative_influence}, which depends on the full strategy profile $\mathbf{x}(t)$ of all agents.
            \end{definition}

            Note that the reward \cref{eq:SI_reward} depends on the positions of all agents, not just agent $i$; this coupling is the source of the strategic interaction in the Q-learning environment, and its invisibility to the individual agent is the key consequence of partial observability discussed in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_partial_obs}.

            At each time step $t$, all agents simultaneously select actions via their $\varepsilon$-greedy policies, update their positions via \cref{eq:SI_pos_update}, receive rewards via \cref{eq:SI_reward}, and update their Q-tables via \cref{eq:SI_qupdate}. This procedure is formalized in \Cref{alg:SI_qstep}.

            \begin{algorithm}[ht!]
            \caption{Independent Q-Learning Step}
            \label{alg:SI_qstep}
            \begin{algorithmic}[1]
                \Require Current positions $\mathbf{x}(t)$,
                    Q-tables $\{Q_i\}_{i \in I}$,
                    parameters $\alpha, \gamma, \varepsilon$
                \Ensure Updated Q-tables $\{Q_i\}_{i \in I}$,
                    new positions $\mathbf{x}(t+1)$
                \For{each agent $i \in I$ (simultaneously)}
                    \State Select action $a_i(t)$ via
                        $\varepsilon$-greedy policy on
                        $Q_i(x_i(t), \cdot)$
                \EndFor
                \State Update positions:
                    $x_i(t+1) \leftarrow
                    \textsc{PosUpdate}(a_i(t), x_i(t))$
                    for all $i \in I$ via \cref{eq:SI_pos_update}
                \State Compute rewards:
                    $r_i(t) \leftarrow u_i(\mathbf{x}(t))$
                    for all $i \in I$ via \cref{eq:SI_reward}
                \For{each agent $i \in I$}
                    \State $Q_i(x_i(t), a_i(t)) \leftarrow
                        (1-\alpha)\,Q_i(x_i(t), a_i(t))
                        + \alpha\bigl(r_i(t) + \gamma
                        \max_{a} Q_i(x_i(t+1), a)\bigr)$
                \EndFor
                \State \Return $\{Q_i\}_{i \in I}$,
                    $\mathbf{x}(t+1)$
            \end{algorithmic}
            \end{algorithm}

            Throughout this section we restrict the influence kernels to the Gaussian form of \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian}; the qualitative results are expected to generalize to other log-concave influence kernels.

        \subsubsection{Partial Observability}\label{subsubsec:SI_partial_obs}

            The Q-learning environment described above is partially observable: each agent observes only its own position $x_i(t)$ and not the positions of other agents. Formally, the true state of the system at time $t$ is the full strategy profile $\mathbf{x}(t) \in \otimes_{i=1}^N X_i$, but each agent's Q-table is indexed only by its own position $x_i(t) \in \mathbb{D}$. As a result, a single Q-table entry $Q_i(d, a)$ aggregates expected rewards across all system configurations in which $x_i = d$, regardless of the positions of other agents. This is a form of state aliasing: many distinct true states map to the same observed state, preventing the agent from learning the true conditional dynamics.

            A direct consequence is that the convergence guarantees of single-agent Q-learning --- specifically, convergence to the optimal Q-function under standard step-size conditions \cite{watkins1992q} --- do not extend to the partially observable multi-agent setting. Agents may converge to suboptimal policies, fail to converge entirely, or converge to policies that do not correspond to any Nash equilibrium of the underlying influencer game. Despite these limitations, we demonstrate empirically in \Cref{subsec:SI_marl_vs_ad,subsec:SI_optimal_policy} that the qualitative bifurcation structure identified under adaptive dynamics is partially preserved under IQL, and that agents often learn policies broadly consistent with the symmetric Nash equilibrium, suggesting that the symmetric Nash is a robust attractor even under partial observability and stochastic exploration.

        \subsection{MARL Dynamics versus Adaptive Dynamics}\label{subsec:SI_marl_vs_ad}

            Beyond partial observability, independent Q-learning differs from adaptive dynamics in several additional respects. First, action selection under the $\varepsilon$-greedy policy is stochastic: with probability $\varepsilon > 0$, agents take a uniformly random action, introducing persistent exploration noise. Second, agents update their Q-tables from discrete, noisy reward signals rather than analytic utility gradients. Third, the discretization of $\mathbb{D}$ limits the resolution with which agents can represent incentive gradients, introducing a discretization error absent in continuous adaptive dynamics.

            Of particular interest is whether the cyclic behavior and non-Nash equilibrium configurations identified in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics} persist under IQL. \Cref{fig:marl_vs_adaptive} compares adaptive dynamics and IQL trajectories for $N = 3$ agents initialized at positions $(0.1, 0.4, 0.9)$ at $\sigma = 0.16$, which lies outside of the cycling regime identified in \Cref{fig:3player_bifurcation,fig:3player_equilibria:c}.

In both panels agents exhibit cyclic behavior, confirming that cycling is a robust feature of the influencer game that persists under stochastic MARL dynamics. In \Cref{fig:marl_vs_adaptive:a} (adaptive dynamics), the cycle is damped and agents converge toward a fixed point over time. In \Cref{fig:marl_vs_adaptive:b} (IQL), the cycle is more persistent: agent positions are smoothed by a sliding window average of width $10^4$ steps to reduce noise from the $\varepsilon$-greedy policy, and the stochastic exploration prevents convergence to a fixed point. IQL parameters: $4 \times 10^6$ iterations, $\gamma = 0.5$, $\alpha = 0.05$, $\varepsilon = 0.01$, temperature $= 0.5$, window size $= 10^4$ steps.}

            \Cref{fig:marl_bifurcation} displays bifurcation diagrams comparing adaptive dynamics and IQL for $N \in \{2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}$ agents as $\sigma$ varies, with initial conditions listed in \Cref{tab:SI_marl_initial_conditions}. The two learning paradigms yield qualitatively similar bifurcation structures: in both cases the symmetric Nash equilibrium loses stability near the theoretical threshold $\sigma_0^* = \sqrt{\frac{N-2}{N-1}}\,\sigma_B$ of \Cref{cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}, and players bifurcate into local strategic clusters at lower $\sigma$ values. Two systematic differences are observed. First, the IQL agents tend to bifurcate at slightly lower values of $\sigma$ than the adaptive agents, likely because the discretization of the strategy space effectively increases the local stability of the symmetric Nash by limiting the precision with which agents can detect and respond to off-center incentives. Second, the IQL bifurcation diagrams are more diffuse than their adaptive dynamics counterparts, reflecting the stochastic exploration of the $\varepsilon$-greedy policy; IQL agents tend to remain near the first-order bifurcation rather than cascading through the full hierarchy of higher-order bifurcations observed in \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation}.

        \subsection{Finding an Optimal Policy}\label{subsec:SI_optimal_policy}

            In the preceding subsection we studied IQL in a setting where agents learn to respond to a fixed $\sigma$ value from a given initialization. We now consider the complementary setting in which agents are repeatedly exposed to the same influencer game and accumulate experience across epochs, enabling them to learn a stationary policy that approximately optimizes their long-run utility. This iterated setting is relevant whenever agents face the same competitive environment repeatedly --- for instance, content creators on a stable platform, candidates in recurring elections, or species competing for a stationary resource distribution --- and have sufficient time to refine their strategies through experience.

            We implement this via independent Q-learning with synchronous steps. Each experiment consists of 100 independent trials, each comprising $10^5$ epochs of 50 episodes. At the end of each epoch, agent positions are reset to random initial values, but the Q-tables are not reset, allowing agents to accumulate policy knowledge across epochs. Initial positions for each value of $N$ are listed in \Cref{tab:SI_optimal_policy_init}. Resource distributions are restricted to the bimodal Gaussian mixture of \Cref{subsubsec:SI_three_player_dynamics}, with the number of modes varied to assess its effect on policy convergence.

            To visualize the learned policies, we compute the trial-averaged Q-table
            \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_avg_Q}
                \bar{Q}(d,a) = \frac{1}{100}\sum_{k=1}^{100} Q^{(k)}(d,a), \qquad d \in \mathbb{D},\; a \in \mathcal{A},
            \end{equation}
            and apply a softmax with visualization temperature $\tau_{\mathrm{vis}} = 0.15$ to obtain, for each state $d$, a probability distribution over actions:
            \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_softmax_policy}
                \pi_{\mathrm{vis}}(a \mid d) = \frac{\exp\!\bigl(\bar{Q}(d,a)/\tau_{\mathrm{vis}}\bigr)}{\sum_{a'\in\mathcal{A}}\exp\!\bigl(\bar{Q}(d,a')/\tau_{\mathrm{vis}}\bigr)}, \qquad a \in \mathcal{A}.
            \end{equation}
            Note that $\tau_{\mathrm{vis}}$ is used only for visualization and is distinct from the training temperature $\tau$ in \Cref{tab:SI_optimal_policy_params}. A policy consistent with convergence to the symmetric Nash $x^*$ concentrates \texttt{RIGHT} (respectively \texttt{LEFT}) mass at positions $d < x^*$ (respectively $d > x^*$), and \texttt{STAY} mass near $x^*$.

            The IQL parameters used throughout this subsection are listed in \Cref{tab:SI_optimal_policy_params}; initial positions are listed in \Cref{tab:SI_optimal_policy_init}.

            \subsubsection{Effects of Partial Observability}\label{subsubsec:SI_optimal_partial_obs}
                As formalized in \Cref{subsubsec:SI_partial_obs}, each agent's Q-table is indexed solely by its own position $x_i \in \mathbb{D}$, so the entry $Q_i(d, a)$ must account for the expected discounted return averaged over all configurations of the remaining $N-1$ agents. Formally, agent $i$ at position $d$ is effectively solving
                \begin{equation}\label{eq:SI_aggregated_Q}
                    Q_i(d, a) \;\approx\; \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{-i}}\!\left[ r_i\!\left((d, \mathbf{x}_{-i}), a\right) + \gamma \max_{a'} Q_i\!\left(x_i', a'\right) \;\middle|\; x_i = d \right],
                \end{equation}
                where $\mathbf{x}_{-i} \in \mathbb{D}^{N-1}$ denotes the positions of all agents other than $i$, and the expectation is taken over the empirical distribution of opponents' positions encountered during training. Since the reward $r_i$ depends on the full profile $\mathbf{x}$ via \Cref{eq:SI_reward}, the aggregation in \Cref{eq:SI_aggregated_Q} introduces a systematic bias: the single Q-value $Q_i(d,a)$ conflates incentives that vary with the unobserved configuration of opponents, an instance of state aliasing \cite{watkins1992q}. Consequently, the convergence guarantees of single-agent Q-learning do not extend to this setting, and agents may converge to suboptimal or non-Nash policies.

                Despite this limitation, we demonstrate empirically in the subsequent subsections that agents trained under partial observability often learn policies broadly consistent with the symmetric Nash equilibrium $x^*$. This empirical robustness suggests that the symmetric Nash is a stable attractor not only under the analytic gradient dynamics of \Cref{subsubsec:SI_one-dimensional}, but also under the stochastic, information-limited dynamics of IQL. 
              
    
            \subsubsection{Effect of the State-Space Size}
                A key factor governing Q-learning convergence is the size of the state space $|\mathbb{D}|$. A finer discretization increases $|\mathbb{D}|$ and therefore the number of Q-table entries that must be estimated, requiring proportionally more training experience to achieve the same estimation accuracy. Formally, the sample complexity of tabular Q-learning scales at least linearly with $|\mathbb{D}|$ even under complete observability \cite{watkins1992q}; under the partial observability of the present setting, larger state spaces further dilute the empirical distribution over opponents' positions, compounding the state-aliasing bias identified in \cref{eq:SI_aggregated_Q}.
    
                \Cref{fig:qlearn_states} examines this effect empirically for $N = 2$ agents at $\sigma = 0.15$, comparing state spaces of size $|\mathbb{D}| = 11$ ($\delta_{\mathrm{step}} = 0.1$) and $|\mathbb{D}| = 101$ ($\delta_{\mathrm{step}} = 0.01$), each under a unimodal and a bimodal resource distribution. Each condition was repeated across 100 independent trials of $10^4$ epochs. In both cases there is a discernible tendency for the learned policies to favor movement toward the symmetric Nash $x^* = 0.5$, but the policies become substantially noisier as $|\mathbb{D}|$ increases. The increased noise is unlikely to be attributable solely to insufficient training, given the number of epochs; rather, it reflects the increased estimation burden imposed by the larger state space.
                
                The resource distribution shape has a secondary but detectable effect: policies are somewhat noisier under the bimodal distribution than under the unimodal distribution at the same $|\mathbb{D}|$. This is consistent with the multimodal reward landscape introducing additional local optima in the Q-table that complicate convergence. Nonetheless, the dominant factor governing policy quality is the state-space size, and the bimodal effect is largely subsumed by it as $|\mathbb{D}|$ grows.

            \subsubsection{Effect of the Reach Parameter on Learned Policies}
                As established in \Cref{thm:SI_one-dimensional-stability,cor:SI_Gaussian_stability}, the reach parameter $\sigma$ governs the stability of the symmetric Nash equilibrium under adaptive dynamics. \Cref{fig:qlearn_reach} examines whether this dependence persists in the learned policies of IQL agents. We fix $N=2$ and vary $\sigma \in \{0.15, 0.35, 1.0\}$ across two state-space sizes, $|\mathbb{D}|=11$ ($\delta_{\mathrm{step}}=0.1$) and $|\mathbb{D}|=101$ ($\delta_{\mathrm{step}}=0.01$).

                For $|\mathbb{D}| = 11$, policies at all three reach values exhibit directional concentration consistent with the symmetric Nash, though the effect weakens with increasing $\sigma$. At $\sigma = 1.0$ the reward surface is nearly flat --- large-$\sigma$ Gaussian kernels equalize relative influence across most of $[0,1]$ --- and the learned policy approaches a nearly uniform distribution over actions, indicating that gradient information is effectively lost. For $|\mathbb{D}| = 101$, policies do not converge fully within the training budget at any $\sigma$ value; nonetheless, a systematic directional tendency toward $x^*$ remains visible. Together these results confirm that $\sigma$ exerts qualitatively the same destabilizing effect on IQL convergence as on adaptive dynamics: sufficiently large reach suppresses the incentive gradient that drives agents toward the symmetric Nash, and this suppression is more severe in larger state spaces where estimation noise is amplified.

            \clearpage
    
            \subsubsection{Effect of the Number of Agents}
                As in the preceding \Cref{subsec:SI_Gaussian}, the number of agents $N$ exerts a substantial effect on convergence to the symmetric Nash. Under complete observability, the joint state space of an $N$-agent system grows as $|\mathbb{D}|^N$, and the training episodes required for convergence scale accordingly --- an instance of the curse of dimensionality. Under the partial observability of the present setting, each agent's Q-table remains of size $|\mathbb{D}| \times |\mathcal{A}|$ regardless of $N$, so the per-agent storage cost does not increase; however, the state-aliasing bias in \cref{eq:SI_aggregated_Q} worsens with $N$, since the marginal distribution of opponents' positions becomes an increasingly coarse summary of the $N-1$ unobserved agents. We therefore limit the analysis to $N \in \{2, 3, 4\}$ and demonstrate empirically that policy convergence toward the symmetric Nash can still be achieved, albeit with increasing degradation as $N$ grows.
    
                As established in \Cref{fig:nplayer_bifurcation}, the stability boundary $\sigma_0^*(N)$ of the symmetric Nash decreases with $N$ under adaptive dynamics, so that larger groups require a narrower range of $\sigma$ values to support convergence to $x^*$. The same qualitative trend is visible in \Cref{fig:qlearn_agents}: for $N \in \{2, 3, 4\}$ at fixed $|\mathbb{D}|=11$, the learned policies continue to exhibit directional concentration toward $x^*$ at $\sigma = 0.15$ and $\sigma = 0.35$, but the concentration depends on $N$. Notably, for $N = 3$ at $\sigma = 0.15$ the policy suggests convergence to a position near $x^*$ rather than the bifurcated equilibrium predicted by adaptive dynamics (cf.\ \Cref{fig:3player_equilibria}), consistent with the finding in \Cref{subsec:SI_marl_vs_ad} that IQL agents tend to remain closer to the symmetric Nash than their adaptive-dynamics counterparts. However, for $N=4$ at $\sigma=0.15$ the policy instead suggests convergence to a bifurcated equilibrium consistent with the predicted adaptive dynamics, indicating that the IQL-specific tendency to remain near $x^*$ is overcome by the stronger bifurcation force at larger $N$. At $\sigma = 1.0$ the learned policies degrade toward uniformity, and this degradation is more pronounced as $N$ decreases, consistent with the combined effect of reward-surface flattening and growing state-aliasing bias. Together these observations suggest that the range of $\sigma$ values over which IQL agents converge to the symmetric Nash is not only bounded but increases as $N$ increases, tracking the increasing stability boundary $\sigma_0^*(N)$ of adaptive dynamics.

Each condition is averaged over 100 independent trials of $10^4$ epochs with 100 episodes per epoch. Rows correspond to $N=2$ (\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:a}--\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:c}), $N=3$ (\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:d}--\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:f}), and $N=4$ (\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:g}--\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:i}). For $N=2$ and $N=3$ at $\sigma=0.15$ and $\sigma=0.35$, the policies exhibit directional mass consistent with convergence toward $x^*$; notably, for $N=3$ at $\sigma=0.15$ (\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:d}) the learned policy concentrates near $x^*$ rather than indicating the bifurcated equilibrium predicted by adaptive dynamics (cf.\ \Cref{fig:3player_equilibria}), consistent with the IQL tendency to remain closer to the symmetric Nash observed in \Cref{fig:marl_bifurcation}. In contrast, for $N=4$ at $\sigma=0.15$ (\subref{fig:qlearn_agents:g}) the policy shifts to indicate a bifurcated equilibrium consistent with the adaptive-dynamics prediction, suggesting the stronger bifurcation force at larger $N$ overcomes the IQL stabilizing tendency. At $\sigma=1.0$ the policies degrade toward uniformity for all $N$, with the degradation more pronounced as $N$ decreases, reflecting the combined effect of reward-surface flattening and growing state-aliasing bias. Together, the panels indicate that the range of $\sigma$ values supporting IQL convergence to $x^*$ is bounded and increase as $N$ increases, tracking the increasing stability boundary $\sigma_0^*(N)$ of adaptive dynamics.}

Thus, we have a much more complex system with many parameters that impact the existence and stability of the symmetric Nash equilibrium.
